\chapter{Probability Distribution of Functions Of Random Variables}

\section{Preliminaries}

In mathematical statistics we constantly form new random variables from old
ones. If $X_1, X_2, \dots, X_n$ is a sample, the sample mean
$\bar{X} = \frac{1}{n}\sum X_i$, the largest observation, and the ratio of two
sample variances are all functions of the sample, and their distributions are
what estimators and tests are built on. This chapter develops the methods for
finding such distributions. This section collects the facts about single and
joint distributions that the methods use.

\subsection{Probability Mass and Density Functions}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:pmfpdf}
A random variable $X$ is \emph{discrete} if it takes values in a countable set
$\{x_1, x_2,\dots\}$. Its \emph{probability function} (p.f.) is
$f_X(x;\theta) = P(X = x)$, which satisfies
\[f_X(x;\theta) \geq 0 \qquad\text{and}\qquad \sum_{i} f_X(x_i;\theta) = 1 .\]
A random variable $X$ is \emph{continuous} if there is a function
$f_X(x;\theta)$, its \emph{probability density function} (p.d.f.), with
\[f_X(x;\theta) \geq 0 ,\qquad \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} f_X(x;\theta)\, dx = 1
,\qquad P(a\leq X\leq b) = \int_a^b f_X(x;\theta)\,dx .\]
\end{definition}

Here $\theta$ denotes the parameters of the distribution, written when they
matter and omitted otherwise. For a continuous variable every single point has
probability zero, $P(X = a) = 0$, so whether the end points of an interval are
included makes no difference.

\subsection{The Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF)}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:cdf}
The \emph{cumulative distribution function} of any random variable $X$ is
\[F_X(x) = P(X \leq x),\qquad -\infty<x<\infty .\]
\end{definition}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:cdfprops}
Every c.d.f. has the following properties.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Monotonicity:} if $a < b$, then $F_X(a) \leq F_X(b)$.
\item \textbf{Limits:} $\lim_{x\to -\infty}F_X(x) = 0$ and
$\lim_{x\to\infty}F_X(x) = 1$.
\item \textbf{Right-continuity:} $\lim_{h \to 0^+} F_X(x+h) = F_X(x)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
For $a<b$ the event $\{X\leq a\}$ is contained in $\{X\leq b\}$, which gives 1.
The events $\{X\leq -n\}$ decrease to the empty set and $\{X\leq n\}$ increase to
the whole sample space as $n\to\infty$, so by continuity of probability their
probabilities tend to $0$ and $1$, which with monotonicity gives 2. Likewise the
events $\left\{X\leq x + \frac1n\right\}$ decrease to $\{X\leq x\}$, giving 3.
\end{proof}

The c.d.f. is the one description that serves discrete and continuous variables
alike. For a discrete variable it is a step function, jumping by $P(X = x_i)$ at
each $x_i$; for a continuous variable it is continuous, with
$f_X(x) = F_X'(x)$ wherever the derivative exists.

\subsection{The Moment Generating Function (MGF)}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:mgf}
The \emph{moment generating function} of $X$ is
\[M_X(t) = E\left(e^{tX}\right),\]
provided this expectation is finite for all $t$ in some interval $-h<t<h$.
\end{definition}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:mgfprops}
If the m.g.f. of $X$ exists, then
\begin{enumerate}
\item for every $m = 1,2,\dots$, $E(X^m) = M_X^{(m)}(0)$, the $m$th
derivative at $t = 0$;
\item it determines the distribution: two random variables whose m.g.f.s agree
on an interval about $0$ have the same distribution;
\item $M_{aX + b}(t) = e^{bt}M_X(at)$ for constants $a$ and $b$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
For 1, expand $e^{tX} = \sum_m \frac{t^mX^m}{m!}$ and take expectations term by
term, which the existence of the m.g.f. justifies: $M_X(t) = \sum_m
E(X^m)\frac{t^m}{m!}$, a power series whose $m$th derivative at $0$ is $E(X^m)$.
Property 3 is $E\left(e^{t(aX + b)}\right) = e^{bt}E\left(e^{(at)X}\right)$.
Property 2, the uniqueness theorem, is the inversion theorem for Laplace
transforms and is quoted without proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mgfnormal}
Find the m.g.f. of $X\sim N(\mu,\sigma^2)$, and use it to find $E(X)$ and
$\text{Var}(X)$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
For $Z\sim N(0,1)$, completing the square in the exponent,
\[M_Z(t) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{tz}\frac{e^{-z^2/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,dz =
e^{t^2/2}\int_{-\infty}^\infty\frac{e^{-(z - t)^2/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,dz =
e^{t^2/2} .\]
Since $X = \mu + \sigma Z$, property 3 gives
$M_X(t) = e^{\mu t}M_Z(\sigma t) = e^{\mu t + \sigma^2t^2/2}$. Then
$M_X'(t) = (\mu + \sigma^2t)M_X(t)$, so $E(X) = M_X'(0) = \mu$, and
$M_X''(t) = \left[\sigma^2 + (\mu + \sigma^2t)^2\right]M_X(t)$, so
$E(X^2) = \sigma^2 + \mu^2$ and $\text{Var}(X) = \sigma^2$.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Multivariate Distributions and Independence}

When dealing with functions of several variables we work with random vectors
$\mathbf{X} = (X_1, X_2, \dots, X_k)^{\mathsf T}$.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:jointdiscrete}
If $X_1,X_2,\dots,X_k$ are discrete random variables, their \emph{joint
probability function} is
\[f(x_1,x_2,\dots,x_k) = P(X_1=x_1,\ X_2=x_2,\ \dots,\ X_k=x_k),\]
which is non-negative and satisfies
$\sum_{x_1}\cdots\sum_{x_k} f(x_1,\dots, x_k) = 1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:jointcontinuous}
The random variables $X_1,X_2,\dots,X_k$ are \emph{jointly continuous} with
\emph{joint p.d.f.} $f(x_1,x_2,\dots,x_k)$ if
\begin{enumerate}
\item $f(x_1,\dots,x_k)\geq 0$ for all $(x_1, \dots, x_k)$;
\item $\int_{-\infty}^\infty\cdots\int_{-\infty}^\infty f(x_1,\dots,x_k)\,dx_k
\cdots dx_1 = 1$;
\end{enumerate}
and then $P(\mathbf X\in A) = \int\cdots\int_A f(x_1,\dots,x_k)\,dx_k\cdots dx_1$
for every region $A$.
\end{definition}

The \emph{marginal} p.f. or p.d.f. of one variable is obtained by summing or
integrating the joint function over all the others; for example
$f_{X_1}(x_1) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty f(x_1,x_2)\,dx_2$ in the bivariate case.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:independence}
The random variables $X_1,X_2,\dots,X_k$ (discrete or continuous) are
\emph{independent} if and only if
\[f(x_1,x_2,\dots,x_k) = f_{X_1}(x_1)\, f_{X_2}(x_2)\cdots f_{X_k}(x_k) =
\prod^k_{j=1} f_{X_j} (x_j) ,\]
for all $(x_1,\dots,x_k)$, where $f_{X_j}$ is the marginal p.f. or p.d.f. of
$X_j$.
\end{definition}

A practical consequence, used repeatedly below, is the \emph{factorisation
criterion}: two jointly continuous variables are independent if and only if
their joint p.d.f. can be written as $g(x)h(y)$ on a support that is a product
set $A\times B$ (a ``rectangle''), the factors then being proportional to the
marginals. A support such as $0<x<y<1$ is not a product set, and variables with
such a support are never independent.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:marginals}
Let $f(x,y) = 6x$ for $0<x<y<1$. Find the marginal p.d.f.s and decide whether
$X$ and $Y$ are independent.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
For $0<x<1$, $f_X(x) = \int_x^16x\,dy = 6x(1 - x)$, and for $0<y<1$,
$f_Y(y) = \int_0^y6x\,dx = 3y^2$. Their product $18x(1 - x)y^2$ is not
$f(x,y)$, and indeed the support is not a product set, so $X$ and $Y$ are
dependent.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Joint Moment Generating Functions}

The joint m.g.f. handles several variables at once, and in particular sums of
independent variables.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:jointmgf}
The \emph{joint moment generating function} of $X_1, X_2 , \dots,X_k$ is
\[M_{\mathbf X}(\mathbf t) = M(t_1,\dots,t_k) = E\left(e^{t_1X_1 + \cdots +
t_kX_k}\right) = E\left(e^{\mathbf t^{\mathsf T}\mathbf X}\right),\]
when it exists for all $\mathbf t$ in a neighbourhood of $\mathbf 0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:jointmgfindep}
The random variables $X_1,X_2,\dots,X_k$ are independent if and only if their
joint m.g.f. is the product of the marginal m.g.f.s,
\[M(t_1,\dots, t_k) = M_{X_1}(t_1)\cdots M_{X_k}(t_k) = \prod^k_{j=1}
M_{X_j}(t_j).\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
If the variables are independent, the expectation of a product of functions of
separate variables factorises, so
$E\left(\prod_je^{t_jX_j}\right) = \prod_jE\left(e^{t_jX_j}\right)$. Conversely,
the product of the marginal m.g.f.s is the joint m.g.f. of independent variables
with those marginals, and by the uniqueness theorem for joint m.g.f.s the joint
distribution is that one.
\end{proof}

Setting all but one $t_j$ to zero gives the marginal m.g.f.s,
$M_{X_1}(t_1) = M(t_1,0,\dots,0)$, and the mixed moments come from mixed partial
derivatives, for example $E(X_1X_2) = \frac{\partial^2 M}{\partial
t_1\partial t_2}(\mathbf 0)$.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:jointmgf}
Let $f(x,y) = e^{-y}$ for $0<x<y<\infty$. Find the joint m.g.f., and use it to
find $E(X)$, $E(Y)$ and $\text{Cov}(X,Y)$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
For $t_1 + t_2<1$ and $t_2<1$,
\[M(t_1,t_2) = \int_0^\infty\int_x^\infty e^{t_1x + t_2y}e^{-y}\,dy\,dx =
\int_0^\infty e^{t_1x}\frac{e^{-(1 - t_2)x}}{1 - t_2}\,dx = \frac{1}{(1 -
t_2)(1 - t_1 - t_2)} .\]
Then $M(t,0) = \frac{1}{1 - t}$, so $X$ is exponential with mean $1$, and
$M(0,t) = \frac{1}{(1 - t)^2}$, so $Y\sim\text{GAM}(2,1)$ with $E(Y) = 2$.
Differentiating, $\frac{\partial^2M}{\partial t_1\partial t_2}(0,0) = 3$, so
$E(XY) = 3$ and $\text{Cov}(X,Y) = 3 - 1\cdot 2 = 1$. The m.g.f. does not
factorise, so $X$ and $Y$ are dependent.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Properties of the Bivariate CDF}

The joint c.d.f. of $(X_1,X_2)$ is $F(x_1,x_2) = P(X_1\leq x_1,\ X_2\leq x_2)$.
The probability that $(X_1, X_2)$ falls in a rectangle $(a,b] \times (c,d]$ is
found from its values at the four corners by inclusion and exclusion:
\[ P(a < X_1 \leq b,\ c < X_2 \leq d) = F(b,d) - F(b,c) - F(a,d) + F(a,c).\]

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.1, >=stealth]
\draw[->] (-0.5,0) -- (5,0) node[right] {$x_1$};
\draw[->] (0,-0.5) -- (0,5) node[above] {$x_2$};
\draw[dashed] (1,0) node[below] {$a$} -- (1,3);
\draw[dashed] (4,0) node[below] {$b$} -- (4,3);
\draw[dashed] (0,1) node[left] {$c$} -- (4,1);
\draw[dashed] (0,3) node[left] {$d$} -- (4,3);

\filldraw[fill=blue!18, draw=blue!45] (1,1) rectangle (4,3);

\node[circle, fill=black, inner sep=1pt, label=above right:{\small $(b,d)$}] at (4,3) {};
\node[circle, fill=black, inner sep=1pt, label=below left:{\small $(a,c)$}] at (1,1) {};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The probability of a rectangular region is found by inclusion-exclusion of the Joint CDF values at the vertices.}\end{figure}

The term $F(b,d)$ counts the whole quadrant below and to the left of $(b,d)$;
subtracting $F(b,c)$ and $F(a,d)$ removes the two strips outside the rectangle,
but removes the quadrant below and to the left of $(a,c)$ twice, so $F(a,c)$ is
added back. In particular, a function can be a joint c.d.f. only if this
combination is non-negative for every rectangle. For jointly continuous
variables, $f(x_1,x_2) = \frac{\partial^2F}{\partial x_1\partial x_2}$.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:bivariatecdf}
Let $F(x,y) = (1 - e^{-x})(1 - e^{-2y})$ for $x,y>0$. Find
$P(1<X\leq 2,\ 0<Y\leq 1)$ and the joint p.d.f.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Since $F(x,0) = F(0,y) = 0$,
\begin{align*}
P &= F(2,1) - F(2,0) - F(1,1) + F(1,0)\\
&= (1 - e^{-2})(1 - e^{-2}) - (1 - e^{-1})(1 - e^{-2})\\
&= (e^{-1} - e^{-2})(1 - e^{-2})\approx 0.201 .
\end{align*}
Differentiating, $f(x,y) = e^{-x}\cdot 2e^{-2y}$: $X$ and $Y$ are independent
exponential variables with means $1$ and $\frac12$.
\end{solution}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\section{Distribution of Order Statistics}

Suppose $X_1, X_2, \dots, X_n$ is a random sample from a continuous population.
Arranging the observations in increasing order gives the \emph{order
statistics}
\[ X_{(1)} \leq X_{(2)} \leq \dots \leq X_{(n)} ,\]
often written $Y_1, Y_2, \dots, Y_n$ to distinguish them from the raw sample.
(For a continuous population, ties have probability zero, so the inequalities
are strict with probability one.)
\begin{itemize}
\item $Y_1 = \min(X_1, \dots, X_n)$ is the smallest order statistic.
\item $Y_n = \max(X_1, \dots, X_n)$ is the largest.
\item $Y_{(n+1)/2}$, for odd $n$, is the sample median.
\end{itemize}
Order statistics are dependent even though the sample is independent: knowing
$Y_1$ is large forces every other $Y_k$ to be large.

\subsection{The Joint PDF}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:orderjoint}
If $X_1, X_2, \dots, X_n$ is a random sample from a population with continuous
p.d.f. $f(x)$, then the joint p.d.f. of the order statistics $Y_1, Y_2, \dots,
Y_n$ is
\[ g(y_1, y_2, \dots, y_n) = n! \prod_{i=1}^n f(y_i), \qquad y_1 < y_2 < \dots <
y_n ,\]
and zero otherwise.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
The map from $(x_1,\dots,x_n)$ to its sorted version is $n!$-to-one: each of the
$n!$ orderings of a sample with distinct values gives the same ordered vector.
On each of the $n!$ regions $x_{\pi(1)}<\cdots<x_{\pi(n)}$ the map is a
permutation of coordinates, with Jacobian $\pm 1$, and the joint density of the
sample, $\prod f(x_i)$, takes the same value $\prod f(y_i)$ at every
preimage. Adding the $n!$ contributions gives the result.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:orderexp}
Let $X_1, X_2, X_3$ be a random sample of size $3$ from the exponential
population with p.d.f. $f(x) = e^{-x}$, $x > 0$. Find
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] the joint p.d.f. of the order statistics $Y_1, Y_2, Y_3$;
\item[(ii)] the marginal p.d.f. of the minimum $Y_1$;
\item[(iii)] the marginal p.d.f. of the maximum $Y_3$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] By Theorem~\ref{thm:orderjoint},
\[g(y_1, y_2, y_3) = 3!\, e^{-y_1}e^{-y_2}e^{-y_3} = 6 e^{-(y_1 + y_2 + y_3)},
\qquad 0 < y_1 < y_2 < y_3 < \infty .\]

\item[(ii)] Integrate out $y_3$ from $y_2$ to $\infty$, then $y_2$ from $y_1$ to
$\infty$:
\begin{align*}
g_1(y_1) &= 6e^{-y_1} \int_{y_1}^{\infty} e^{-y_2}\int_{y_2}^{\infty} e^{-y_3}\,
dy_3 \, dy_2 = 6e^{-y_1} \int_{y_1}^{\infty} e^{-2y_2}\, dy_2\\
&= 6e^{-y_1}\cdot\frac{1}{2}e^{-2y_1} = 3e^{-3y_1},\qquad y_1 > 0 .
\end{align*}
So the minimum of three exponential lifetimes with mean $1$ is exponential with
mean $\frac13$.

\item[(iii)] Integrate out $y_1$ from $0$ to $y_2$, then $y_2$ from $0$ to
$y_3$:
\begin{align*}
g_3(y_3) & = 6e^{-y_3} \int_{0}^{y_3} e^{-y_2}\int_0^{y_2}e^{-y_1}\,dy_1\,dy_2 =
6e^{-y_3} \int_{0}^{y_3} (e^{-y_2} - e^{-2y_2})\, dy_2\\
& = 6e^{-y_3} \left( \frac{1}{2} - e^{-y_3} + \frac{1}{2}e^{-2y_3} \right) =
3e^{-y_3} - 6e^{-2y_3} + 3e^{-3y_3},\qquad y_3 > 0 .
\end{align*}
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\subsection{Derivation of Extremes: Minimum and Maximum}

The distributions of the maximum and the minimum are fundamental in reliability
theory and extreme value analysis, and they can be found directly from the
c.d.f. without integrating the joint density.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:extremes}
Let $X_1, X_2, \dots, X_n$ be independent and identically distributed continuous
random variables with p.d.f. $f$ and c.d.f. $F$. Then the maximum $Y_n$ and the
minimum $Y_1$ have c.d.f.s and p.d.f.s
\[F_{Y_n}(y) = [F(y)]^n ,\qquad g_n(y) = n[F(y)]^{n-1} f(y) ,\]
\[F_{Y_1}(y) = 1 - [1 - F(y)]^n ,\qquad g_1(y) = n[1 - F(y)]^{n-1} f(y) .\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
The maximum is at most $y$ if and only if every observation is at most $y$, so
by independence
\[F_{Y_n}(y) = P(X_1 \leq y,\ \dots,\ X_n \leq y) = \prod^n_{i = 1}P(X_i\leq y)
= [F(y)]^n .\]
The minimum exceeds $y$ if and only if every observation exceeds $y$, so
\[F_{Y_1}(y) = 1 - P(X_1 > y,\ \dots,\ X_n > y) = 1 - [1 - F(y)]^n .\]
Differentiating gives the p.d.f.s.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:uniformmax}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from the uniform distribution on
$(0,\theta)$. Find the p.d.f. and the mean of $Y_n$, and show that
$\frac{n+1}{n}Y_n$ has mean $\theta$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Here $F(y) = \frac{y}{\theta}$ on $(0,\theta)$, so $g_n(y) =
\frac{ny^{n-1}}{\theta^n}$ for $0<y<\theta$, and
\[E(Y_n) = \int_0^\theta y\,\frac{ny^{n-1}}{\theta^n}\,dy = \frac{n}{n +
1}\theta .\]
So $Y_n$ underestimates $\theta$ on average, as it must since $Y_n<\theta$, and
$\frac{n+1}{n}Y_n$ has mean exactly $\theta$; it is used as an estimator in the
chapter on estimation.
\end{solution}

\subsection{The $k^{\text{th}}$ Order Statistic}

The same reasoning gives the distribution of any order statistic. For $Y_k$ to
lie in a small interval $(y, y + dy)$, the $n$ observations must fall into three
bins:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $k-1$ observations to the \emph{left} of $y$, each with probability
$F(y)$;
\item exactly \emph{one} observation in $(y,y + dy)$, with probability about
$f(y)\,dy$;
\item the remaining $n-k$ observations to the \emph{right}, each with
probability $1-F(y)$.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.2, every node/.style={scale=0.9}]
% Draw the number line
\draw[thick, ->] (0,0) -- (10,0) node[right] {$x$};
% Draw the intervals
\draw[blue, thick] (0.5, 0.1) -- (0.5, -0.1) node[below] {$a$};
\draw[blue, thick] (9.5, 0.1) -- (9.5, -0.1) node[below] {$b$};

% The point y_k
\draw[red, ultra thick] (6, 0.4) -- (6, -0.4) node[below] {$y_k$};
\node[red, above] at (6, 0.4) {Exactly 1 obs.};
\node[red] at (6, -0.99) {($f(y_k)$)};

% Left Region
\fill[blue!10] (0.5, 0) rectangle (5.9, 0.3);
\node at (3.25, 0.5) {$k-1$ observations};
\node[blue] at (3.25, -0.4) {Prob: $[F(y_k)]^{k-1}$};

% Right Region
\fill[green!10] (6.1, 0) rectangle (9.5, 0.3);
\node at (7.8, 0.5) {$n-k$ observations};
\node[green!50!black] at (7.8, -0.4) {Prob: $[1-F(y_k)]^{n-k}$};

% Multinomial Coefficient
\node[draw, rounded corners, fill=gray!5] at (5, -1.8) {$\text{Combinations} = \frac{n!}{(k-1)! \times 1! \times (n-k)!}$};
\end{tikzpicture}\caption{The spatial partitioning of a sample for the $k^{th}$ order statistic.}
\end{figure}

The number of ways to assign the observations to the three bins is the
multinomial coefficient $\frac{n!}{(k - 1)!\,1!\,(n - k)!}$, which gives the
following theorem.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:orderk}
Let $X_1, X_2, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample of size $n$ from a continuous
population with p.d.f. $f$ and c.d.f. $F$, supported on $(a, b)$. The p.d.f. of
the $k^{\text{th}}$ order statistic $Y_k$ is
\[ g_k(y) = \frac{n!}{(k - 1)!\,(n - k)!} [F(y)]^{k-1} [1 - F(y)]^{n - k} f(y)
,\qquad a < y < b ,\]
and zero otherwise.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
By the multinomial argument above,
\begin{align*}
P(y<Y_k\leq y + h) ={}& \frac{n!}{(k - 1)!(n - k)!}[F(y)]^{k-1}[1 - F(y + h)]^{n-k}\\
&\times\left(F(y + h) - F(y)\right) + o(h),
\end{align*}
the remainder accounting for two or more observations in the interval. Dividing by $h$ and letting $h\to 0$ gives
$g_k(y)$.
\end{proof}

With $k = n$ and $k = 1$ this reduces to Theorem~\ref{thm:extremes}.

\begin{exe}
\label{exe:orderexp}
In Example~\ref{ex:orderexp} the marginal p.d.f.s of the minimum $Y_1$ and the
maximum $Y_3$ of an exponential sample of size $3$ were found by double
integration. Use Theorem~\ref{thm:orderk} to find directly the p.d.f.s of
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] the sample minimum $Y_1$;
\item[(ii)] the sample median $Y_2$;
\item[(iii)] the sample maximum $Y_3$,
\end{enumerate}
and check that the results for $Y_1$ and $Y_3$ agree.

\textbf{Answer:} With $F(y) = 1 - e^{-y}$: $g_1(y) = 3e^{-3y}$;
$g_2(y) = 6(1 - e^{-y})e^{-2y}$; $g_3(y) = 3(1 - e^{-y})^2e^{-y} = 3e^{-y} -
6e^{-2y} + 3e^{-3y}$, all for $y>0$.
\end{exe}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:ordercdf}
For a random sample of size $n$ from a population with c.d.f. $F$, discrete or
continuous, the c.d.f. of the $k^{\text{th}}$ order statistic is
\[G_k(y) = \sum^n_{j = k}\binom{n}{j}[F(y)]^j[1 - F(y)]^{n-j} .\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
$Y_k\leq y$ exactly when at least $k$ of the $n$ observations are at most $y$.
The number of observations at most $y$ is binomial with parameters $n$ and
$F(y)$, so $P(Y_k\leq y)$ is the probability that this binomial variable is at
least $k$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:ordercdf}
Let $X_1, X_2, X_3$ be a random sample from $f(x) = 2x$, $0< x < 1$. Find the
c.d.f. and the p.d.f. of the median $Y_2$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The population c.d.f. is $F(x) = x^2$ for $0<x<1$ (with $F = 0$ for $x\leq 0$
and $F = 1$ for $x\geq 1$). By Theorem~\ref{thm:ordercdf}, for $0<y<1$,
\[G_2(y) = \binom{3}{2}(y^2)^2(1 - y^2) + \binom{3}{3}(y^2)^3 = 3y^4(1 - y^2) +
y^6 = y^4(3 - 2y^2) ,\]
with $G_2 = 0$ for $y\leq 0$ and $G_2 = 1$ for $y\geq 1$. The p.d.f. can be found
in two ways:
\begin{itemize}
\item differentiating the c.d.f., $g_2(y) = \frac{d}{dy}(3y^4 - 2y^6) = 12y^3 -
12y^5 = 12y^3(1 - y^2)$;
\item from Theorem~\ref{thm:orderk} with $n = 3$, $k = 2$,
$g_2(y) = \frac{3!}{1!\,1!} (y^2)(1 - y^2)(2y) = 12y^3(1 - y^2)$,
\end{itemize}
both for $0 < y < 1$.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:medianuniform}
Find the probability that the median of a sample of size $5$ from the uniform
distribution on $(0,1)$ lies between $0.4$ and $0.6$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:orderk} with $n = 5$, $k = 3$ and $F(y) = y$,
$g_3(y) = 30y^2(1 - y)^2$, a beta density. So
\[P(0.4<Y_3<0.6) = 30\int_{0.4}^{0.6}y^2(1 - y)^2\,dy = 30\left[\frac{y^3}{3} -
\frac{y^4}{2} + \frac{y^5}{5}\right]_{0.4}^{0.6} \approx 0.365 .\]
\end{solution}

\subsection{Distribution of the Range}

The \emph{sample range} $R = Y_n - Y_1$ is a measure of dispersion used in
quality control and non-parametric statistics. Its distribution follows from the
joint distribution of the two extremes, which the same multinomial argument
gives.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:extremesjoint}
For a random sample of size $n$ from a continuous population on $(a,b)$, the
joint p.d.f. of $Y_1$ and $Y_n$ is
\[ g_{1n}(y_1, y_n) = n(n-1) [F(y_n) - F(y_1)]^{n-2} f(y_1) f(y_n) ,\qquad a <
y_1 < y_n < b .\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
One observation must be near $y_1$, one near $y_n$, and the other $n - 2$
between them, each with probability $F(y_n) - F(y_1)$. There are $n(n - 1)$
ways to choose which observations are the minimum and the maximum.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:rangeuniform}
Find the distribution of the range $R$ of a sample of size $n$ from the uniform
distribution on $(0,1)$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:extremesjoint}, $g(y_1,y_n) = n(n - 1)(y_n - y_1)^{n-2}$ for
$0<y_1<y_n<1$. Change variables to $R = Y_n - Y_1$ and $S = Y_1$, with Jacobian
$1$ (the method is justified in the next section). For fixed $r$, $s$ runs from
$0$ to $1 - r$, so
\[f_R(r) = \int_0^{1 - r}n(n - 1)r^{n-2}\,ds = n(n - 1)r^{n-2}(1 - r),\qquad
0<r<1 ,\]
a beta distribution with parameters $n - 1$ and $2$, and $E(R) = \frac{n -
1}{n + 1}$.
\end{solution}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\section{Functions of Random Variables}

Suppose $X_1, X_2, \dots , X_n$ are random variables with joint p.f. or p.d.f.
$f(x_1, x_2, \dots , x_n)$. The aim is the distribution of new variables
$Y_1, Y_2, \dots, Y_k$ that are functions of them,
\[Y_j = h_j(X_1, X_2, \dots, X_n),\qquad j = 1,\dots,k .\]
Three techniques are used:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the \textbf{c.d.f. technique}, best for a single function whose event
$\{Y\leq y\}$ is easy to describe;
\item the \textbf{transformation (Jacobian) method}, best for one-to-one
mappings, in one or several variables;
\item the \textbf{moment generating function method}, best for sums of
independent variables.
\end{enumerate}

Throughout, $\text{GAM}(\alpha,\beta)$ denotes the gamma distribution with shape
$\alpha$ and \emph{scale} $\beta$, with p.d.f.
\[f(x) = \frac{x^{\alpha - 1}e^{-x/\beta}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\beta^\alpha},\qquad
x>0 ,\]
mean $\alpha\beta$, variance $\alpha\beta^2$ and m.g.f. $(1 - \beta t)^{-\alpha}$
for $t<\frac1\beta$; $\text{EXP}(\beta) = \text{GAM}(1,\beta)$ is the exponential
distribution with mean $\beta$. Where a rate is used instead, it is said so.

\subsection{The Cumulative Distribution Function Method}

The method uses only the definition of the c.d.f. To find the distribution of
$Y = g(X_1,\dots,X_n)$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item write $F_Y(y) = P(Y \leq y) = P(g(X_1, \dots, X_n) \leq y)$;
\item evaluate this probability by summing or integrating the joint p.f. or
p.d.f. over the region $\{\mathbf x : g(\mathbf x) \leq y\}$;
\item for a continuous $Y$, differentiate: $f_Y(y) = F_Y'(y)$.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{thm}[The c.d.f. method for a monotone function]
\label{thm:cdfmonotone}
Let $X$ have p.d.f. $f_X$ and let $Y = g(X)$, where $g$ is differentiable and
strictly increasing on the support of $X$. Then
\[F_Y(y) = P(g(X)\leq y) = P\left(X \leq g^{-1}(y)\right) = F_X\left(g^{-1}(y)
\right) ,\qquad f_Y(y) = f_X\left(g^{-1}(y)\right)\frac{d}{dy}g^{-1}(y) .\]
If $g$ is strictly decreasing, $F_Y(y) = 1 - F_X\left(g^{-1}(y)\right)$ and
$f_Y(y) = -f_X\left(g^{-1}(y)\right)\frac{d}{dy}g^{-1}(y)$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
If $g$ is increasing, $g(X)\leq y$ exactly when $X\leq g^{-1}(y)$; if
decreasing, exactly when $X\geq g^{-1}(y)$. Differentiate by the chain rule.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:cdfsquare}
Let $X$ be a continuous random variable with p.d.f. $f_X$, and $Y = X^2$. Find
the p.d.f. of $Y$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The function $x^2$ is not monotone, so apply the method directly. For $y > 0$,
\[F_Y(y) = P(X^2 \leq y) = P(-\sqrt{y} \leq X \leq \sqrt{y}) = F_X(\sqrt{y}) -
F_X(-\sqrt{y}) ,\]
and $F_Y(y) = 0$ for $y\leq 0$. Differentiating by the chain rule,
\[f_Y(y) = f_X(\sqrt{y}) \cdot \frac{1}{2\sqrt{y}} + f_X(-\sqrt{y}) \cdot
\frac{1}{2\sqrt{y}} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{y}} \left[ f_X(\sqrt{y}) +
f_X(-\sqrt{y}) \right], \qquad y > 0 .\]
For $X\sim N(0,1)$ this gives $f_Y(y) = \frac{y^{-1/2}e^{-y/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}$,
the $\chi^2(1)$ density: the square of a standard normal variable is chi-square
with one degree of freedom.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:cdfexp}
Given $f_X(x) = 2e^{-2x}$ for $x > 0$, find the p.d.f. of $Y = e^X$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
For $x > 0$, $F_X(x) = \int_0^x 2e^{-2t}\, dt = 1 - e^{-2x}$. Since $x > 0$,
$Y = e^X$ takes values in $(1, \infty)$, and $e^x$ is increasing, so for $y>1$,
\[F_Y(y) = P(e^X \leq y) = P(X \leq \ln y) = 1 - e^{-2\ln y} = 1 - y^{-2} .\]
Differentiating, $f_Y(y) = 2y^{-3}$ for $y > 1$, a Pareto distribution.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:cdfloguniform}
Let $X$ be uniform on $(0, 1)$, with $f_X(x) = 1$ for $0 < x < 1$, and let
$Y = -2\ln X$. Find the p.d.f. of $Y$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
As $x$ runs from $1$ down to $0^+$, $y = -2\ln x$ runs from $0$ up to $\infty$,
so the support of $Y$ is $y > 0$. The function is decreasing, and for $y>0$
\[F_Y(y) = P(-2\ln X \leq y) = P\left(\ln X \geq -\frac{y}{2}\right) = P\left(X
\geq e^{-y/2}\right) = 1 - e^{-y/2},\]
the inequality reversing on division by $-2$. Hence
$f_Y(y) = \frac{1}{2} e^{-y/2}$ for $y > 0$.
\end{solution}

\begin{remark}
The result is the exponential distribution with mean $2$, which is also the
chi-square distribution with $2$ degrees of freedom. Since $F_X(X)$ is uniform on
$(0,1)$ for any continuous $X$ (the \emph{probability integral transform}), this
gives a way to turn uniform random numbers into observations from other
distributions, and is the basis of Fisher's method of combining $p$-values.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:probintegral}
Prove the probability integral transform: if $X$ is continuous with strictly
increasing c.d.f. $F$, then $U = F(X)$ is uniform on $(0,1)$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
For $0<u<1$, $P(U\leq u) = P(F(X)\leq u) = P\left(X\leq F^{-1}(u)\right) =
F\left(F^{-1}(u)\right) = u$, which is the uniform c.d.f.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Transformation (Change of Variable) Method}

\subsubsection{The Discrete Case}

For a discrete variable a transformation simply relabels the points of the
sample space.

\begin{thm}[Discrete case]
\label{thm:transdiscrete}
Let $X$ be a discrete random variable with p.f. $f_X(x)$. If $Y = g(X)$ is one
to one, with inverse $x = g^{-1}(y)$, then the p.f. of $Y$ is
\[ f_Y(y) = f_X\left(g^{-1}(y)\right) ,\qquad y \in g(A),\]
where $A$ is the support of $X$. If $g$ is not one to one,
$f_Y(y) = \sum_{x : g(x) = y}f_X(x)$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
$P(Y = y) = P(X\in\{x : g(x) = y\})$, which is a single term when $g$ is one to
one.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:geometricshift}
If $X \sim \text{GEO}(p)$, with $f_X(x) = pq^{x-1}$ for $x = 1, 2, 3, \dots$ and
$q = 1 - p$, find the distribution of $Y = X - 1$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The inverse is $x = y + 1$, so
\[f_Y(y) = f_X(y+1) = pq^y, \qquad y = 0, 1, 2, \dots .\]
The distribution of $X$ counts the trial on which the first success occurs; $Y$
counts the failures before it.
\end{solution}

\subsubsection{The Continuous Case}

For a continuous variable the density must be rescaled as well as relabelled.

\begin{thm}[Continuous case]
\label{thm:transcont}
Let $X$ have p.d.f. $f_X(x)$ with support $A$. If $Y = h(X)$ is a one-to-one
transformation of $A$ onto $B$ whose inverse $x = h^{-1}(y)$ has a continuous,
non-zero derivative on $B$, then
\[ f_Y(y) = f_X\left(h^{-1}(y)\right) |J| ,\qquad y\in B ,\]
where $J = \frac{dx}{dy} = \frac{d}{dy}h^{-1}(y)$ is the \emph{Jacobian} of the
transformation.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
A one-to-one differentiable function with non-zero derivative is strictly
monotone, so this is Theorem~\ref{thm:cdfmonotone}; the absolute value covers
both the increasing and the decreasing case.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:betalog}
Let $X$ have the beta distribution with parameters $\alpha$ and $\beta$,
\[ f_X(x) = \frac{1}{B(\alpha, \beta)} x^{\alpha - 1}(1 - x)^{\beta - 1}, \qquad 0
< x < 1 .\]
Find the p.d.f. of $Y = -\ln X$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
As in Example~\ref{ex:cdfloguniform}, the support of $Y$ is $(0,\infty)$. The
inverse is $x = e^{-y}$, with $J = \frac{dx}{dy} = -e^{-y}$ and $|J| = e^{-y}$.
By Theorem~\ref{thm:transcont},
\[f_Y(y) = \frac{1}{B(\alpha, \beta)} (e^{-y})^{\alpha - 1} (1 - e^{-y})^{\beta -
1} \cdot e^{-y} = \frac{1}{B(\alpha, \beta)} e^{-\alpha y} (1 - e^{-y})^{\beta -
1}, \qquad y > 0 .\]
With $\beta = 1$ this is $\alpha e^{-\alpha y}$: if $X\sim\text{BETA}(\alpha,1)$,
then $-\ln X\sim\text{EXP}\left(\frac1\alpha\right)$.
\end{solution}

\subsubsection{Bivariate Transformations}

In two dimensions, for example to find the distribution of a sum or a ratio, a
second variable is introduced so that the transformation is one to one, and the
bivariate Jacobian of the subsection on Jacobians in multivariable calculus is
used.

\begin{thm}[Bivariate transformation]
\label{thm:transbivariate}
Let $(X, Y)$ have joint p.d.f. $f_{X,Y}(x,y)$ on the support $A$. Let
$U = h_1(X,Y)$ and $V = h_2(X,Y)$ define a one-to-one transformation of $A$ onto
$B$, with inverse $x = w_1(u,v)$, $y = w_2(u,v)$ having continuous partial
derivatives and non-zero Jacobian
\[ J = \frac{\partial(x,y)}{\partial(u,v)} = \begin{vmatrix} \frac{\partial
x}{\partial u} & \frac{\partial x}{\partial v} \\[1ex] \frac{\partial y}{\partial
u} & \frac{\partial y}{\partial v} \end{vmatrix}\]
on $B$. Then the joint p.d.f. of $(U, V)$ is
\[ f_{U,V}(u,v) = f_{X,Y}(w_1(u,v), w_2(u,v))\,|J| ,\qquad (u,v)\in B .\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
For a region $C\subseteq B$, $P((U,V)\in C) = P((X,Y)\in w(C)) =
\iint_{w(C)}f_{X,Y}\,dx\,dy$, and by the change of variables formula for double
integrals this is $\iint_C f_{X,Y}(w_1,w_2)|J|\,du\,dv$.
\end{proof}

To find the distribution of a single function $U = h_1(X,Y)$, choose a
convenient second variable $V$, find the joint p.d.f. of $(U,V)$, and integrate
out $v$.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:gammabeta}
Let $X\sim\text{GAM}(a,1)$ and $Y\sim\text{GAM}(b,1)$ be independent.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] Find the joint p.d.f. of $U = X + Y$ and $V = \frac{X}{X + Y}$.
\item[(ii)] Are $U$ and $V$ independent?
\item[(iii)] Find the marginal p.d.f.s of $U$ and $V$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] By independence, the joint p.d.f. is
\[ f_{X,Y}(x,y) = \frac{1}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)} x^{a-1} y^{b-1} e^{-(x+y)},
\qquad x, y > 0 .\]
The transformation $u = x + y$, $v = \frac{x}{x+y}$ has inverse $x = uv$,
$y = u(1-v)$, and maps the quadrant $x,y>0$ onto the strip $u > 0$, $0 < v < 1$.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.3]
    % Left Plane (X,Y)
    \draw[->, thick] (-0.2,0) -- (3,0) node[right] {$x$};
    \draw[->, thick] (0,-0.2) -- (0,3) node[above] {$y$};
    \fill[blue!10] (0,0) rectangle (2.5,2.5);
    \node at (1.25, 1.25) {Support $A$};
    \node[below left] at (0,0) {$0$};
    
    % Dashed lines representing U = x+y
    \draw[dashed, red] (1,0) -- (0,1) node[midway, sloped, above, scale=0.7] {$u=1$};
    \draw[dashed, red] (2,0) -- (0,2) node[midway, sloped, above, scale=0.7] {$u=2$};
    
    % Transformation Arrow
    \draw[->, ultra thick] (3.5, 1.25) -- (5, 1.25) node[midway, above] {$S$};
    \node[midway, below, scale=0.8] at (4.25, 1.2) {$\begin{cases} u=x+y \\ v=\frac{x}{x+y} \end{cases}$};

    % Right Plane (U,V)
    \begin{scope}[shift={(6,0)}]
        \draw[->, thick] (-0.2,0) -- (3,0) node[right] {$u$};
        \draw[->, thick] (0,-0.2) -- (0,2.5) node[above] {$v$};
        
        % Rectangular Support
        \fill[green!10] (0,0) rectangle (2.5, 2);
        \draw[thick, green!60!black] (0,2) -- (2.5, 2);
        \node[left] at (0,2) {$1$};
        \node[below left] at (0,0) {$0$};
        \node at (1.25, 1) {Support $B$};
        
        % Horizontal lines corresponding to constant v
        \draw[blue, opacity=0.3] (0, 0.5) -- (2.5, 0.5);
        \draw[blue, opacity=0.3] (0, 1.5) -- (2.5, 1.5);
    \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The transformation maps the $x,y > 0$ quadrant to a rectangular strip where $u > 0$ and $0 < v < 1$.}
\end{figure}

The Jacobian is
\[ J = \begin{vmatrix} v & u \\ 1-v & -u \end{vmatrix} = -uv - u(1-v) = -u
,\qquad |J| = u ,\]
so by Theorem~\ref{thm:transbivariate}
\begin{align*}
f_{U,V}(u,v) &= \frac{(uv)^{a-1} (u(1-v))^{b-1} e^{-u}}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}\cdot u\\
&= \frac{u^{a+b-1} e^{-u}\, v^{a-1} (1-v)^{b-1}}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)} ,\qquad u>0,\ 0<v<1 .
\end{align*}

\item[(ii)] The joint p.d.f. factorises as
\[f_{U,V}(u,v) = \frac{u^{a + b - 1} e^{-u}}{\Gamma(a + b)} \times \frac{\Gamma(a +
b)\, v^{a - 1}(1 - v)^{b - 1}}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)} ,\]
a function of $u$ times a function of $v$, on the product set
$(0,\infty)\times(0,1)$. By the factorisation criterion, $U$ and $V$ are
independent.

\item[(iii)] The two factors are themselves densities: integrating out $v$ uses
$\int_0^1v^{a-1}(1 - v)^{b-1}\,dv = B(a,b) = \frac{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a +
b)}$, and integrating out $u$ uses $\int_0^\infty u^{a+b-1}e^{-u}\,du = \Gamma(a +
b)$. So
\[f_U(u) = \frac{u^{a + b - 1} \, e^{-u}}{\Gamma(a + b)},\ u > 0,\qquad f_V(v) =
\frac{v^{a - 1}\, (1 - v)^{b - 1}}{B(a,b)},\ 0 < v < 1 ,\]
that is, $U\sim\text{GAM}(a + b ,1)$ and $V \sim\text{BETA}(a,b)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\begin{exe}
\label{exe:gammabetamoments}
For $U$ and $V$ of Example~\ref{ex:gammabeta}, show that
\begin{enumerate}
\item $E(V) = \frac{a}{a+b}$;
\item $\text{Var}(V) = \frac{ab}{(a+b)^2(a+b+1)}$;
\item $E(U) = a+b$ and $\text{Var}(U) = a+b$.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Answer:} For (1) and (2), $E(V^k) = \frac{B(a + k,b)}{B(a,b)}$, giving
$E(V) = \frac{a}{a + b}$ and $E(V^2) = \frac{a(a + 1)}{(a + b)(a + b + 1)}$. For
(3), use the gamma moments with $\alpha = a + b$, $\beta = 1$.
\end{exe}

\subsubsection{Discrete Bivariate Transformations}

In the discrete case the transformation is a point-to-point mapping, and the
work lies in identifying the new support correctly.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:transdiscbiv}
Let $f(x,y)$ be the joint p.f. of discrete random variables $X$ and $Y$, and let
$U = h_1(X,Y)$, $V = h_2(X,Y)$ define a one-to-one transformation of
$A = \{(x,y) : f(x,y) > 0\}$ onto $B$. Then the joint p.f. of $U$ and $V$ is
\[g(u,v) = f(w_1(u,v), w_2(u,v)) ,\qquad (u,v)\in B ,\]
where $x = w_1(u,v)$ and $y = w_2(u,v)$ is the inverse transformation.
\end{thm}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:discreteproduct}
Let $X$ and $Y$ have joint p.f. $f(x,y) = \frac{1}{36}xy$ for $x = 1, 2, 3$ and
$y = 1, 2, 3$. Find the joint p.f. of $U = XY$ and $V = Y$, and the marginal p.f.
of $U$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The inverse is $y = v$, $x = \frac{u}{v}$, so
\[g(u,v) = \frac{1}{36} \cdot\frac{u}{v}\cdot v = \frac{u}{36}\]
on the support $B = \{(u,v) : v \in \{1,2,3\},\ \frac{u}{v} \in \{1,2,3\}\}$:
for $v = 1$, $u\in\{1,2,3\}$; for $v = 2$, $u\in\{2,4,6\}$; for $v = 3$,
$u\in\{3,6,9\}$. The joint and marginal p.f.s are shown in the table.
\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{|c|ccc|c|}
\hline
$u \setminus v$ & $1$ & $2$ & $3$ & $g_U(u)$ \\ \hline
$1$ & $\frac{1}{36}$ & $0$ & $0$ & $\frac{1}{36}$ \\
$2$ & $\frac{2}{36}$ & $\frac{2}{36}$ & $0$ & $\frac{4}{36}$ \\
$3$ & $\frac{3}{36}$ & $0$ & $\frac{3}{36}$ & $\frac{6}{36}$ \\
$4$ & $0$ & $\frac{4}{36}$ & $0$ & $\frac{4}{36}$ \\
$6$ & $0$ & $\frac{6}{36}$ & $\frac{6}{36}$ & $\frac{12}{36}$ \\
$9$ & $0$ & $0$ & $\frac{9}{36}$ & $\frac{9}{36}$ \\ \hline
$g_V(v)$ & $\frac{6}{36}$ & $\frac{12}{36}$ & $\frac{18}{36}$ & $1$ \\ \hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Joint probability table for $U = XY$ and $V = Y$.}
\label{tab:discreteproduct}
\end{table}
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:poissonsum}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent Poisson random variables with means $\mu$ and
$\lambda$. Find
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] the joint p.f. of $U = X + Y$ and $V = Y$;
\item[(ii)] the marginal p.f. of $U$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] The joint p.f. of $X$ and $Y$ is
\[f(x,y) = \frac{e^{-(\mu + \lambda)}\, \mu^x\, \lambda^y}{x!\, y!} ,\qquad x,
y = 0, 1, 2, \dots .\]
The inverse of $u = x + y$, $v = y$ is $y = v$, $x = u - v$, and $x\geq 0$ means
$v\leq u$, so
\[g(u,v) = \frac{e^{-(\mu + \lambda)}\, \mu^{u-v}\, \lambda^v}{(u - v)!\, v!}
,\qquad u = 0, 1, 2, \dots ;\ v = 0, 1, \dots, u .\]

\item[(ii)] Summing over $v$ and using the binomial theorem,
\[g_U(u) = \frac{e^{-(\mu + \lambda)}}{u!}\sum_{v = 0}^u\binom{u}{v}\mu^{u -
v}\lambda^v = \frac{e^{-(\mu + \lambda)}\,(\mu + \lambda)^u}{u!} ,\qquad u = 0, 1,
2, \dots ,\]
so $U\sim\text{POI}(\mu + \lambda)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:poissonconditional}
For $X$ and $Y$ as in Example~\ref{ex:poissonsum}, find the conditional
distribution of $Y$ given $X + Y = u$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Dividing the joint p.f. by the marginal,
\[P(V = v\mid U = u) = \frac{g(u,v)}{g_U(u)} = \binom{u}{v}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\mu
+ \lambda}\right)^v\left(\frac{\mu}{\mu + \lambda}\right)^{u - v},\qquad v =
0,\dots,u ,\]
the binomial distribution with $u$ trials and success probability
$\frac{\lambda}{\mu + \lambda}$. Given the total number of events, each one comes
from the second source with probability proportional to its rate.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Distributions of Sum and Difference of Random Variables}

The distribution of $Z = X + Y$ or $W = X - Y$ is found by adding up the joint
density along the lines $x + y = z$ or $x - y = w$.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:sumdiff}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be jointly continuous random variables with joint p.d.f.
$f(x,y)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] The p.d.f. of $Z = X + Y$ is
\[f_Z(z) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty f(x,z-x)\,dx = \int_{-\infty}^\infty f(z-y,y)\,
dy .\]
\item[(b)] The p.d.f. of $W = X - Y$ is
\[f_W(w) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty f(x,x - w)\,dx = \int_{-\infty}^\infty
f(w+y,y)\, dy .\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
(a) The c.d.f. is the integral of $f$ over the half-plane $x + y \leq z$.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9]
    % 1. Define the shaded region FIRST so it stays in the background
    % We use opacity=0.4 to ensure the grid/axes are visible through it
    \fill[blue!20, opacity=0.6] (-1,-1) -- (-1,2.5) -- (2.5,-1) -- cycle;

    % 2. Draw the Axes on top of the shading
    \draw[->, thick] (-1.5,0) -- (4,0) node[right] {$x$};
    \draw[->, thick] (0,-1.5) -- (0,4) node[above] {$y$};
    
    % 3. Draw the Boundary Line x + y = z
    \draw[ultra thick, blue] (-0.5, 3) -- (3, -0.5) node[right, black] {$x + y = z$};
    
    % 4. Add Intercepts and Dots
    \filldraw[black] (0,2.5) circle (1.5pt) node[left] {$z$};
    \filldraw[black] (2.5,0) circle (1.5pt) node[above] {$z$};
    
    % 5. Text Indicators
    \node[blue!70!black, font=\small] at (0.7, 0.7) {$X + Y \leq z$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Integration region for the sum $Z = X + Y$.}
\end{figure}

Integrating $x$ first,
\[F_Z(z) = \iint\limits_{x+y \leq z} f(x,y)\,dx\, dy = \int_{-\infty}^\infty
\int^{z-y}_{-\infty}f(x,y)\, dx\,dy .\]
Differentiating under the integral sign, by the fundamental theorem of calculus,
\[f_Z(z) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty \frac{\partial}{\partial z}\left[
\int^{z-y}_{-\infty}f(x,y)\, dx\right]dy = \int_{-\infty}^\infty f(z-y,y)\,dy ,\]
and integrating $y$ first instead gives the other form.

(b) The c.d.f. is the integral over the half-plane $x - y\leq w$, that is
$y\geq x - w$.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9]
    % 1. Shaded Region (X - Y <= w  =>  y >= x - w)
    % We define a large polygon that covers the top-left half-plane
    \fill[green!20, opacity=0.6] (-1.5, 2) -- (4, 2) -- (4, 1) -- (-0.5, -3.5) -- (-1.5, -3.5) -- cycle;
    
    % 2. Axes (Drawn over the fill)
    \draw[-Stealth, thick] (-1.5,0) -- (4,0) node[right] {$x$};
    \draw[-Stealth, thick] (0,-3.5) -- (0,2.5) node[above] {$y$};
    
    % 3. Boundary Line y = x - w
    \draw[ultra thick, green!60!black] (-1, -3) -- (3.5, 1.5) node[right, black] {$x - y = w$};
    
    % 4. Intercepts
    \filldraw[black] (2,0) circle (1.5pt) node[below right] {$w$};
    \filldraw[black] (0,-2) circle (1.5pt) node[left] {$-w$};
    
    % 5. Indicator Text
    \node[green!40!black, font=\small] at (-0.5, 1) {$X - Y \leq w$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Integration region for the difference $W = X - Y$, with visible axes.}
\end{figure}

So $F_W(w) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty\int_{x - w}^\infty f(x,y)\,dy\,dx$, and
differentiating with respect to $w$, the lower limit $x - w$ contributing with a
minus sign twice,
\[f_W(w) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty f(x, x - w)\,dx .\]
The substitution $x = w + y$ gives the other form.
\end{proof}

\begin{coro}[The convolution formula]
\label{cor:convolution}
If $X$ and $Y$ are independent continuous random variables, then
\[f_Z(z) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty f_X(x)f_Y(z-x)\,dx = \int_{-\infty}^\infty
f_X(z-y)f_Y(y)\,dy ,\qquad f_W(w) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty f_X(w + y)f_Y(y)\,dy
.\]
\end{coro}

The integrand is zero outside the supports, and finding the actual limits of
integration is the main work in applications.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:expsum}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent exponential random variables, each with mean
$\lambda$. Find the distribution of $X+Y$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Here $f_X(x) = \frac{1}{\lambda}e^{-x/\lambda}$ for $x>0$ and likewise for $Y$.
The integrand $f_X(x)f_Y(z - x)$ is non-zero only when $x>0$ and $z - x>0$, so for
$z>0$,
\[f_Z(z) = \int_0^z \frac{1}{\lambda} e^{-x/\lambda} \cdot \frac{1}{\lambda}
e^{-(z-x)/\lambda}\, dx = \int^z_0 \frac{1}{\lambda^2} e^{-z/\lambda}\, dx =
\frac{z}{\lambda^2} e^{-z/\lambda} ,\qquad z>0 .\]
This is $\frac{z^{2-1}e^{-z/\lambda}}{\Gamma(2)\lambda^2}$, so
$X + Y \sim \text{GAM}(2,\lambda)$.
\end{solution}

More generally, if $X_1,\dots,X_k$ are independent with
$X_i\sim\text{GAM}(\alpha_i,\beta)$, the same scale $\beta$, then
$\sum_{i=1}^kX_i\sim\text{GAM}\left(\sum_{i=1}^k\alpha_i,\beta\right)$; this is
proved most easily by the m.g.f. method below.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:normaldiff}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent standard normal random variables. Find the
distribution of $W = X-Y$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
By Corollary~\ref{cor:convolution},
\[f_W(w) = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int^{\infty}_{-\infty} e^{-\frac{1}{2}(w + y)^2}
e^{-\frac{1}{2}y^2}\, dy = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int^{\infty}_{-\infty} e^{-\frac{1}{2}(w^2
+ 2wy + 2y^2)}\, dy .\]
Completing the square, $w^2 + 2wy + 2y^2 = 2\left(y + \frac{w}{2}\right)^2 +
\frac{w^2}{2}$, so
\[f_W(w) = \frac{e^{-w^2/4}}{2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}e^{-\left(y +
\frac{w}{2}\right)^2}\,dy = \frac{e^{-w^2/4}}{2\pi}\sqrt\pi =
\frac{e^{-w^2/4}}{\sqrt{4\pi}} ,\qquad -\infty < w < \infty .\]
This is the $N(0,2)$ density: the difference of two independent standard normals
is normal with variance $2$.
\end{solution}

\begin{exe}
\label{exe:gammasum}
Let $X\sim\text{GAM}(\alpha_1, \beta)$ and $Y\sim\text{GAM}(\alpha_2, \beta)$ be
independent. Use the convolution formula to show that
$X + Y \sim\text{GAM}(\alpha_1 + \alpha_2, \beta)$.

\textbf{Hint:} After taking out the constants, the integral is
$\int_0^zx^{\alpha_1 - 1}(z - x)^{\alpha_2 - 1}\,dx = z^{\alpha_1 + \alpha_2 -
1}B(\alpha_1,\alpha_2)$, by the substitution $x = zt$.
\end{exe}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:uniformsum}
Find the p.d.f. of the sum of two independent uniform variables on $(0,1)$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The integrand $f_X(x)f_Y(z - x)$ is $1$ when $0<x<1$ and $0<z - x<1$, that is
$\max(0,z - 1)<x<\min(1,z)$, and $0$ otherwise. So
\[f_Z(z) =
\begin{cases}
z, & 0<z\leq 1,\\
2 - z, & 1<z<2 ,
\end{cases}\]
the triangular distribution on $(0,2)$. The density of a sum is smoother than
those of its terms.
\end{solution}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:sumdiscrete}
If $X$ and $Y$ are discrete random variables with joint p.f. $f(x,y)$, then
$Z = X + Y$ and $W = X - Y$ have p.f.s
\begin{gather*}
P(Z = z) = \sum_x f(x,z - x) = \sum_y f(z - y,y),\\
P(W = w) = \sum_x f(x, x - w) = \sum_y f(w + y,y) .
\end{gather*}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
$\{Z = z\}$ is the disjoint union over $x$ of the events
$\{X = x,\ Y = z - x\}$, and similarly for $W$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:discretesum}
The joint p.f. of $X$ and $Y$ is given in the table below. Find the distributions
of $Z = X + Y$ and $W = X - Y$.
\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{c|cccc|c}
$x\setminus y$ & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & $f_X(x)$\\
\hline
1 & 0.10 & 0.05 & 0.02 & 0.02 & 0.19\\
2 & 0.05 & 0.20 & 0.05 & 0.20 & 0.50\\
3 & 0.02 & 0.05 & 0.02 & 0.04 & 0.13\\
4 & 0.02 & 0.02 & 0.04 & 0.10 & 0.18\\
\hline
$f_Y(y)$ & 0.19 & 0.32 & 0.13 & 0.36 & 1\\
\end{tabular}
\caption{The joint p.f. of $X$ and $Y$.}
\label{tab:discretesum}
\end{table}
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:sumdiscrete}, each probability is the sum of the entries on a
diagonal of the table: for $Z = z$ the cells with $x + y = z$, and for $W = w$ the
cells with $x - y = w$. For example
$P(Z = 4) = f(1,3) + f(2,2) + f(3,1) = 0.02 + 0.20 + 0.02 = 0.24$. The results
are
\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{|c|ccccccc|}
\hline
$z$ & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 & 7 & 8\\
\hline
$P(Z = z)$ & 0.10 & 0.10 & 0.24 & 0.14 & 0.24 & 0.08 & 0.10\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}
and
\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{|c|ccccccc|}
\hline
$w$ & $-3$ & $-2$ & $-1$ & 0 & 1 & 2 & 3 \\
\hline
$P(W = w)$ & 0.02 & 0.22 & 0.14 & 0.42 & 0.14 & 0.04 & 0.02\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}
Each set of probabilities sums to $1$.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Distributions of Products and Quotients of Random Variables}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:prodquot}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be jointly continuous random variables with joint p.d.f.
$f(x,y)$, where $P(X = 0) = 0$. Then $Z=\frac{Y}{X}$ and $W = XY$ have p.d.f.s
\[f_Z(z) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty |x|\,f(x,xz)\,dx ,\qquad f_W(w) =
\int_{-\infty}^\infty\frac{1}{|x|}f\left(x,\frac{w}{x}\right)dx .\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
For the quotient, take $U = X$ and $Z = \frac{Y}{X}$, with inverse $x = u$,
$y = uz$ and Jacobian $\begin{vmatrix}1 & 0\\ z & u\end{vmatrix} = u$. By
Theorem~\ref{thm:transbivariate} the joint p.d.f. of $(U,Z)$ is
$f(u,uz)|u|$, and integrating out $u$ gives $f_Z$. For the product, take
$U = X$, $W = XY$, with inverse $x = u$, $y = \frac{w}{u}$ and Jacobian
$\frac1u$, and integrate out $u$ again.
\end{proof}

\begin{coro}
\label{cor:quotientindep}
If $X$ and $Y$ are independent continuous random variables, then
$Z = \frac{Y}{X}$ has p.d.f.
\[f_Z(z) = \int^{\infty}_{-\infty}|x|\, f_X(x)\,f_Y(zx)\, dx .\]
\end{coro}

The formula can also be derived from the c.d.f. directly, splitting according to
the sign of $X$: $F_Z(z) = P(Y\leq zX,\ X>0) + P(Y\geq zX,\ X<0)$, and
differentiating each part under the integral sign produces the factor $x$ on
$x>0$ and $-x$ on $x<0$, which together give $|x|$.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:expratio}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent, each with p.d.f. $\lambda e^{-\lambda x}$ for
$x > 0$. Find the p.d.f. of $Z = \frac{Y}{X}$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
By Corollary~\ref{cor:quotientindep}, for $z>0$,
\[f_Z(z) = \int^{\infty}_0x\,\lambda e^{-\lambda x}\,\lambda e^{-\lambda zx}\,
dx = \lambda^2\int^{\infty}_0xe^{-\lambda(1 + z)x}\, dx = \frac{\lambda^2}{\lambda^2
(1 + z)^2} = \frac{1}{(1+z)^2} ,\]
using $\int_0^\infty xe^{-cx}\,dx = \frac{1}{c^2}$. The parameter $\lambda$ has
disappeared, as it must: the ratio of two lifetimes does not depend on the unit
of time.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:cauchy}
Show that the ratio of two independent standard normal variables has the Cauchy
distribution, with p.d.f. $\frac{1}{\pi(1 + z^2)}$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
By Corollary~\ref{cor:quotientindep}, using the symmetry of the integrand in $x$,
\[f_Z(z) = 2\int_0^\infty x\frac{e^{-x^2/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{e^{-z^2x^2/2}}
{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,dx = \frac{1}{\pi}\int_0^\infty xe^{-(1 + z^2)x^2/2}\,dx =
\frac{1}{\pi(1 + z^2)} .\]
The Cauchy distribution has no mean: $\int|z|f_Z(z)\,dz$ diverges.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Moment Generating Function Method}

The method rests on two facts: the m.g.f. of a sum of independent variables is
the product of their m.g.f.s, and an m.g.f. determines its distribution
(Theorem~\ref{thm:mgfprops}).

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:mgfsum}
If $X_1, X_2, \dots, X_n$ are independent random variables with m.g.f.s
$M_{X_i}(t)$, then the m.g.f. of $Y = \sum^n_{i = 1}X_i$ is
\[M_{Y}(t) = M_{X_1}(t)\, M_{X_2}(t)\cdots M_{X_n}(t) .\]
In particular, if the $X_i$ are independent and identically distributed with
m.g.f. $M_X(t)$, then $M_Y(t) = [M_X(t)]^n$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
By independence the expectation of the product factorises:
\[M_Y(t) = E\left(e^{tX_1}e^{tX_2}\cdots e^{tX_n}\right) =
E\left(e^{tX_1}\right) E\left(e^{tX_2}\right) \cdots E\left(e^{tX_n}\right) .\]
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Special Results}

The following are used repeatedly in sampling theory, and each follows from
Theorem~\ref{thm:mgfsum} and the m.g.f.s of the distributions involved.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $X\sim \text{GAM}(\alpha,\beta)$, then
$\frac{2X}{\beta} \sim \chi^2(2\alpha)$.
\item If $X_i \sim \text{GAM}(\alpha_i,\beta)$, $i = 1, \dots, n$, independently,
then $\sum^n_{i = 1} X_i \sim\text{GAM}\left(\sum^n_{i = 1} \alpha_i,
\beta\right)$.
\item If $X_i \sim \text{EXP}(\beta)$, $i = 1, \dots , n$, independently, then
$\sum^n_{i = 1}X_i \sim \text{GAM}(n,\beta)$.
\item If $X_i \sim N(\mu,\sigma^2)$, $i = 1, \dots , n$, independently, then
$\sum^n_{i = 1}\left(\frac{X_i - \mu}{\sigma}\right)^2\sim \chi^2(n)$.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{proof}
For 1, $M_{2X/\beta}(t) = M_X\left(\frac{2t}{\beta}\right) = (1 -
2t)^{-\alpha}$, the m.g.f. of $\chi^2(2\alpha)$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:chisquare}
below). For 2, $\prod_i(1 - \beta t)^{-\alpha_i} = (1 - \beta t)^{-\sum\alpha_i}$,
and 3 is the case $\alpha_i = 1$. For 4, each
$Z_i = \frac{X_i - \mu}{\sigma}$ is standard normal, $Z_i^2\sim\chi^2(1) =
\text{GAM}\left(\frac12,2\right)$ by Example~\ref{ex:cdfsquare}, and the sum of
$n$ independent such variables is $\text{GAM}\left(\frac n2,2\right) =
\chi^2(n)$ by 2.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:poissonmgf}
Let $X_1, X_2, \dots, X_n$ be independent with $X_i \sim\text{POI}(\mu_i)$. Show
that $Y = \sum^n_{i = 1} X_i \sim\text{POI}\left(\sum^n_{i = 1} \mu_i\right)$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The m.g.f. of $\text{POI}(\mu_i)$ is
$M_{X_i}(t) = \sum_x e^{tx}\frac{e^{-\mu_i}\mu_i^x}{x!} = e^{\mu_i(e^t - 1)}$, so
by Theorem~\ref{thm:mgfsum}
\[M_Y(t) = \prod^n_{i =1} e^{\mu_i(e^t-1)} = e^{\left(\sum_i\mu_i\right)(e^t -
1)},\]
which is the m.g.f. of $\text{POI}\left(\sum_i\mu_i\right)$. By uniqueness, $Y$
has that distribution. This repeats Example~\ref{ex:poissonsum} in two lines.
\end{solution}

\begin{exe}
\label{exe:binnb}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Let $X_1, \dots , X_n$ be independent with $X_i\sim \text{BIN}(n_i,p)$.
Show that $\sum_i X_i \sim\text{BIN}\left(\sum_i n_i,p\right)$.
\item Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be independent with $X_i \sim\text{NB}(k_i,p)$. Show
that $\sum_i X_i \sim\text{NB}\left(\sum_i k_i,p\right)$.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Answer:} The m.g.f.s are $(q + pe^t)^{n_i}$ and
$\left(\frac{pe^t}{1 - qe^t}\right)^{k_i}$ with $q = 1 - p$; the products have the
same form with the exponents added. The common $p$ is essential.
\end{exe}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:normallinear}
Let $X_1, \dots , X_n$ be independent with $X_i \sim N(\mu_i,\sigma_i^2)$, and let
$a_1,\dots,a_n$ be constants. Then
\[\sum^n_{i = 1}a_iX_i \sim N\left(\sum^n_{i = 1} a_i\mu_i,\ \sum^n_{i =
1}a_i^2\sigma^2_i\right).\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
With $M_{X_i}(t) = e^{\mu_i t + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2_it^2}$ and
$Y = \sum_i a_i X_i$,
\[M_Y(t) = \prod^n_{i = 1}E\left(e^{(a_it)X_i}\right) = \prod^n_{i =
1}e^{\mu_ia_it + \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2_ia^2_it^2} = e^{\left(\sum_i\mu_ia_i\right)t +
\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_i\sigma^2_ia^2_i\right)t^2},\]
the m.g.f. of the stated normal distribution.
\end{proof}

\begin{coro}
\label{cor:samplemean}
If $X_1,\dots,X_n$ are independent and $N(\mu,\sigma^2)$, then
$\sum^n_{i = 1} X_i \sim N(n\mu, n\sigma^2)$ and
$\bar X = \frac{1}{n}\sum^n_{i = 1}X_i \sim N\left(\mu, \frac{\sigma^2}{n}\right)$.
\end{coro}

\begin{proof}
Take $a_i = 1$, and then $a_i = \frac1n$, in Theorem~\ref{thm:normallinear}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:chisquare}
If $X \sim \chi^2(n)$, with p.d.f.
\[f(x) = \frac{1}{2^{n/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}\, x^{\frac{n}{2}-1}\,
e^{-x/2} ,\qquad x > 0 ,\]
then
\[M_X(t) = (1-2t)^{-n/2},\ t<\frac12 ,\qquad E(X) = n ,\qquad \text{Var}(X) =
2n .\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
For $t<\frac12$, substituting $y = \left(\frac{1}{2}-t\right)x$,
\[M_X(t) = \frac{1}{2^{n/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}\int_0^{\infty}x^{\frac{n}
{2}-1}\, e^{-\left(\frac{1}{2}-t\right)x}\, dx = \frac{1}{2^{n/2}\Gamma\left(
\frac{n}{2}\right)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}{\left(\frac{1}{2}-t
\right)^{n/2}} = (1-2t)^{-n/2} .\]
Then $M'_X(t) = n(1 - 2t)^{-\frac{n}{2}-1}$ and
$M''_X(t) = n(n + 2)(1 - 2t)^{-\frac{n}{2}-2}$, so $E(X) = n$,
$E(X^2) = n^2 + 2n$ and $\text{Var}(X) = 2n$.
\end{proof}

The chi-square distribution with $n$ degrees of freedom is therefore
$\text{GAM}\left(\frac n2,2\right)$.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mgfsamplemean}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{EXP}(\beta)$. Find the
distribution of $\frac{2n\bar X}{\beta}$ and use it to find $c$ with
$P(\bar X>c\beta) = 0.05$ when $n = 10$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
By special result 3, $\sum X_i = n\bar X\sim\text{GAM}(n,\beta)$, and by result 1,
$\frac{2n\bar X}{\beta}\sim\chi^2(2n)$. With $n = 10$, $P(\bar X>c\beta) =
P\left(\chi^2(20)>20c\right) = 0.05$ when $20c = 31.41$, the upper $5\%$ point of
$\chi^2(20)$, so $c = 1.571$.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Distribution of $t$, $\chi^2$ and $F$ Random Variables}

The three distributions of normal sampling theory are all built from independent
standard normal variables.

\begin{definition}[Chi-square distribution]
\label{def:chisquare}
A random variable $X$ with p.d.f.
\[f_X(x) = \frac{x^{\frac{k}{2}-1}e^{-x/2}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)
2^{k/2}} ,\qquad x>0,\]
has the \emph{chi-square distribution with $k$ degrees of freedom}, written
$X\sim\chi^2(k)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Comparing with the gamma p.d.f., $\chi^2(k) = \text{GAM}\left(\frac k2,
2\right)$: shape $\frac k2$ and scale $2$. (In the \emph{rate} form, with
$\lambda = \frac1\beta$, it is the gamma with $\alpha = \frac k2$ and
$\lambda = \frac12$.)
\item Hence, from the gamma moments $\alpha\beta$ and $\alpha\beta^2$, a
chi-square variable has mean $k$ and variance $2k$: the mean equals the degrees
of freedom and the variance is twice them, as in Theorem~\ref{thm:chisquare}.
\item Its m.g.f. is $(1 - 2t)^{-k/2}$ for $t<\frac12$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:chisquaresum}
If $X_1, \dots, X_k$ are independent with $X_j\sim N(\mu_j, \sigma^2_j)$, then
\[W = \sum^k_{j=1}\left(\frac{X_j - \mu_j}{\sigma_j}\right)^2 \sim\chi^2(k).\]
That is, a chi-square variable with $k$ degrees of freedom is a sum of squares of
$k$ independent standard normal variables.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
The variables $Z_j = \frac{X_j - \mu_j}{\sigma_j}$ are independent standard
normal, so by Theorem~\ref{thm:mgfsum}
$M_W(t) = \left[E\left(e^{tZ^2}\right)\right]^k$ with $Z\sim N(0,1)$. For
$t<\frac12$, substituting $u = \sqrt{1 - 2t}\,z$,
\[E\left(e^{tZ^2}\right) = \int^{\infty}_{-\infty} \frac{1}{\sqrt{2
\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(1 - 2t)z^2}\, dz = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - 2t}}\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2} u^2} \, du = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - 2t}} .\]
So $M_W(t) = (1 - 2t)^{-k/2}$, and by uniqueness $W\sim\chi^2(k)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{coro}
\label{cor:chisquaresample}
If $X_1, X_2,\dots, X_n$ is a random sample from $N(\mu, \sigma^2)$, then
$\sum^n_{j=1} \left( \frac{X_j - \mu}{\sigma}\right)^2 \sim \chi^2(n)$.
\end{coro}

When $\mu$ is replaced by $\bar X$, one degree of freedom is lost:
$\frac{(n - 1)S^2}{\sigma^2} = \sum\left(\frac{X_j - \bar X}{\sigma}\right)^2\sim
\chi^2(n - 1)$, and $\bar X$ and $S^2$ are independent. This fact, proved in
texts on linear models by an orthogonal change of variables, is used below and in
the chapters on estimation and testing.

\begin{definition}[$F$ distribution]
\label{def:fdist}
A random variable $X$ with p.d.f.
\[f_X(x) = \frac{\Gamma \left(\frac{m + n}{2}\right) \left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{m/2}
x^{\frac{m}{2}-1}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{m}{2}\right)\Gamma \left(\frac{n}{2}\right)
\left(1 + \frac{m}{n}x\right)^{\frac{m+n}{2}}} ,\qquad x>0,\]
has the \emph{$F$ distribution with $m$ and $n$ degrees of freedom} (in the
numerator and the denominator), written $X\sim F(m,n)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:fratio}
If $U$ and $V$ are independent with $U\sim\chi^2(m)$ and $V\sim\chi^2(n)$, then
\[X = \frac{U/m}{V/n}\sim F(m,n) .\]
The $F$ distribution is the ratio of two independent chi-square variables, each
divided by its degrees of freedom.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
The plan is to introduce a second variable $Z = V$, find the joint p.d.f. of
$(X,Z)$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:transbivariate}, and integrate out $z$. The joint
p.d.f. of $U$ and $V$ is
\[f_{U,V}(u,v) = \frac{u^{\frac{m}{2}-1}v^{\frac{n}{2}-1}
e^{-(u+v)/2}}{2^{\frac{m + n}{2}}\, \Gamma \left(\frac{m}{2}\right) \,
\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)} ,\qquad u,v>0 .\]
The inverse of $x = \frac{u/m}{v/n}$, $z = v$ is $u = \frac{m}{n}xz$, $v = z$, with
Jacobian
\[J = \begin{vmatrix}
\frac{\partial u}{\partial x} & \frac{\partial u}{\partial z}\\[1ex]
\frac{\partial v}{\partial x} & \frac{\partial v}{\partial z}
\end{vmatrix} = \begin{vmatrix}
\frac{m}{n}z & \frac{m}{n}x\\
0 & 1
\end{vmatrix} = \frac{m}{n}z .\]
So for $x,z>0$,
\[f_{X,Z}(x,z) = \frac{\left(\frac{m}{n}xz\right)^{\frac{m}{2}-1}
z^{\frac{n}{2}-1} e^{-\frac{z}{2}\left(1 + \frac{m}{n}x\right)}}{2^{\frac{m+n}{2}}
\Gamma \left(\frac{m}{2}\right)\Gamma \left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}\cdot\frac{m}{n}z =
\frac{\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{m/2}x^{\frac{m}{2}-1}\, z^{\frac{m+n}{2}-1}\,
e^{-\frac{z}{2}\left(1 + \frac{m}{n}x\right)}}{2^{\frac{m+n}{2}} \Gamma
\left(\frac{m}{2}\right)\Gamma \left(\frac{n}{2}\right)} .\]
Integrating out $z$ with $\int_0^\infty z^{a-1}e^{-cz}\,dz = \frac{\Gamma(a)}{c^a}$,
where $a = \frac{m+n}{2}$ and $c = \frac12\left(1 + \frac mnx\right)$,
\[f_X(x) = \frac{\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{m/2}x^{\frac{m}{2}-1}}{2^{\frac{m+n}{2}}
\Gamma \left(\frac{m}{2}\right)\Gamma \left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma
\left(\frac{m + n}{2}\right)2^{\frac{m+n}{2}}}{\left(1 + \frac{m}{n}x\right)^{
\frac{m+n}{2}}},\]
which is the $F(m,n)$ density.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[$t$ distribution]
\label{def:tdist}
Let $Z\sim N(0,1)$ and $U\sim\chi^2(m)$ be independent. The distribution of
\[ T = \frac{Z}{\sqrt{U/m}}\]
is the \emph{$t$ distribution with $m$ degrees of freedom}, written $T\sim t(m)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
For a random sample from $N(\mu,\sigma^2)$, with $S^2$ the sample variance,
\[T = \frac{\bar X - \mu}{S/\sqrt n} = \frac{\dfrac{\bar X - \mu}{\sigma /
\sqrt{n}}}{\sqrt{\dfrac{(n-1)S^2/\sigma^2}{n-1}}} ,\]
a standard normal variable divided by the square root of an independent
$\chi^2(n - 1)$ variable over its degrees of freedom. So $T\sim t(n - 1)$, the
basis of the $t$ test and the $t$ interval. Also $T^2\sim F(1,m)$ when
$T\sim t(m)$, since $Z^2\sim\chi^2(1)$.
\end{remark}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:tdensity}
The p.d.f. of the $t$ distribution with $m$ degrees of freedom is
\[f_T(y) = \frac{\Gamma \left(\frac{m + 1}{2}\right)}{\sqrt{m\pi}\,
\Gamma\left(\frac{m}{2}\right)\left(1 + \frac{y^2}{m}\right)^{\frac{m+1}{2}}}
,\qquad -\infty<y<\infty .\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
The joint p.d.f. of $Z$ and $U$ is
\[f_{Z,U}(z,u) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-z^2/2}\cdot\frac{u^{\frac{m}{2}-1}
e^{-u/2}}{\Gamma \left(\frac{m}{2}\right) 2^{m/2}} ,\qquad -\infty<z<\infty,\
u>0 .\]
Transform to $X = U$ and $Y = \frac{Z}{\sqrt{U/m}}$, with inverse $u = x$,
$z = y\sqrt{\frac{x}{m}}$ and Jacobian
\[J = \begin{vmatrix}
\frac{\partial u}{\partial x} & \frac{\partial u}{\partial y}\\[1ex]
\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} & \frac{\partial z}{\partial y}
\end{vmatrix} = \begin{vmatrix}
1 & 0 \\
\frac{y}{2\sqrt{mx}} & \sqrt{\frac{x}{m}}
\end{vmatrix} = \sqrt{\frac{x}{m}} .\]
So, for $x>0$ and all $y$,
\[f_{X,Y}(x,y) = \frac{x^{\frac{m}{2}-1}\, e^{-\frac{x}{2}\left(1 +
\frac{y^2}{m}\right)}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\, 2^{m/2}\, \Gamma \left(\frac{m}{2}\right)}
\sqrt{\frac{x}{m}} = \frac{x^{\frac{m+1}{2} - 1}e^{-\frac{x}{2}\left(1 +
\frac{y^2}{m}\right)}}{\sqrt{2\pi m}\, 2^{m/2}\, \Gamma \left(\frac{m}{2}\right)} .\]
Integrating out $x$ with the same gamma integral, $a = \frac{m+1}{2}$ and
$c = \frac12\left(1 + \frac{y^2}{m}\right)$,
\[f_Y(y) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi m}\, 2^{m/2}\, \Gamma \left(\frac{m}{2}\right)}\cdot
\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{m+1}{2}\right)2^{\frac{m+1}{2}}}{\left(1 +
\frac{y^2}{m}\right)^{\frac{m+1}{2}}} = \frac{\Gamma \left(\frac{m +
1}{2}\right)}{\sqrt{m \pi}\, \Gamma \left(\frac{m}{2}\right)\left(1 +
\frac{y^2}{m}\right)^{\frac{m+1}{2}}} ,\]
since $\frac{2^{\frac{m+1}{2}}}{\sqrt{2}\,2^{m/2}} = 1$.
\end{proof}

The $t$ density is symmetric about $0$ and has heavier tails than the normal; as
$m\to\infty$ it tends to the standard normal density, since
$\left(1 + \frac{y^2}{m}\right)^{-\frac{m+1}{2}}\to e^{-y^2/2}$. With $m = 1$ it
is the Cauchy distribution of Example~\ref{ex:cauchy}.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:tmoments}
Show that a $t(m)$ variable has mean $0$ for $m>1$ and variance
$\frac{m}{m - 2}$ for $m>2$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Write $T = Z\sqrt{m/U}$ with $Z$ and $U$ independent. Then $E(T) = E(Z)E\left(
\sqrt{m/U}\right) = 0$ when the second factor is finite, which needs $m>1$. For
the variance, $E(T^2) = E(Z^2)\,m\,E\left(U^{-1}\right)$, and for
$U\sim\chi^2(m)$,
\[E\left(U^{-1}\right) = \int_0^\infty\frac{u^{\frac m2 - 2}e^{-u/2}}{\Gamma\left(
\frac m2\right)2^{m/2}}\,du = \frac{\Gamma\left(\frac m2 - 1\right)2^{\frac m2 -
1}}{\Gamma\left(\frac m2\right)2^{m/2}} = \frac{1}{m - 2},\qquad m>2 .\]
So $\text{Var}(T) = \frac{m}{m - 2}$, larger than the normal variance $1$ and
tending to it as $m$ grows.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:fvariances}
Independent random samples of sizes $m$ and $n$ are taken from
$N(\mu_1,\sigma^2)$ and $N(\mu_2,\sigma^2)$, with sample variances $S_1^2$ and
$S_2^2$. Find the distribution of $\frac{S_1^2}{S_2^2}$, and find
$P\left(S_1^2/S_2^2>3.02\right)$ when $m = n = 10$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Since $\frac{(m - 1)S_1^2}{\sigma^2}\sim\chi^2(m - 1)$ and
$\frac{(n - 1)S_2^2}{\sigma^2}\sim\chi^2(n - 1)$ independently,
Theorem~\ref{thm:fratio} gives
\[\frac{S_1^2}{S_2^2} = \frac{\frac{(m-1)S_1^2}{\sigma^2}\big/(m - 1)}{\frac{(n -
1)S_2^2}{\sigma^2}\big/(n - 1)}\sim F(m - 1, n - 1) ,\]
the common $\sigma^2$ cancelling. With $m = n = 10$ this is $F(9,9)$, whose upper
$5\%$ point is $3.18$ and upper $10\%$ point is $2.44$, so the probability lies
between $0.05$ and $0.10$; more precisely it is about $0.054$. This ratio is the
test statistic for equality of two variances.
\end{solution}


\section{Convergence and Limit Theorems}

Everything so far has been exact. Given the distribution of $X$ we found the exact
distribution of $Y=g(X)$, of a sum, of an order statistic. That is the right
ambition, and it works often enough to be worth the effort --- but not always. The
exact distribution of $\bar X$ for a sample from a beta distribution has no
tractable closed form, and neither does the exact distribution of most statistics
anyone actually computes.

What rescues the situation is that these quantities become \emph{predictable} as
the sample grows. The sample mean settles onto the population mean, and the way it
scatters around that mean settles onto a normal shape --- whatever the population
was. This section makes both statements precise. They are the bridge between the
distribution theory of this chapter and the estimation theory of the next: an
estimator is judged by what it does as $n$ grows, so we need the language for
that first.

\subsection{Chebyshev's Inequality}

Everything in this section rests on one inequality, and it is worth seeing how
little it assumes.

\begin{thm}[Chebyshev's inequality]
Let $X$ be a random variable with mean $\mu$ and finite variance $\sigma^2$. Then
for every $\varepsilon>0$,
\[P\left(\left|X-\mu\right|\geq\varepsilon\right)\leq
\frac{\sigma^2}{\varepsilon^2}.\]
Equivalently, writing $\varepsilon=k\sigma$,
\[P\left(\left|X-\mu\right|\geq k\sigma\right)\leq\frac{1}{k^2}.\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Take the continuous case; the discrete case replaces every integral by a sum.
By definition
\[\sigma^2=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(x-\mu)^2f(x)\,dx
\geq\int_{\left|x-\mu\right|\geq\varepsilon}(x-\mu)^2f(x)\,dx,\]
because the discarded region contributes a non-negative amount. On the region that
remains, $(x-\mu)^2\geq\varepsilon^2$, so
\[\sigma^2\geq\varepsilon^2\int_{\left|x-\mu\right|\geq\varepsilon}f(x)\,dx
=\varepsilon^2\,P\left(\left|X-\mu\right|\geq\varepsilon\right).\]
Dividing by $\varepsilon^2$ gives the result.
\end{proof}

\begin{note}
Notice what was \emph{not} assumed: no shape, no symmetry, no named distribution
--- only that the variance exists. That is the inequality's whole point, and also
why it is so loose. It says at most $\frac14$ of any distribution lies beyond two
standard deviations; for a normal distribution the true figure is about $0.046$.
A bound that holds for every distribution cannot be tight for any particular one.
\end{note}

\begin{example}
A production process has mean weight $\mu=500$\,g and standard deviation
$\sigma=8$\,g, and nothing further is known about the distribution. What can be
said about the proportion of items weighing between $480$\,g and $520$\,g?
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The interval is $\mu\pm20$, that is $\mu\pm k\sigma$ with
$k=\frac{20}{8}=2.5$. Chebyshev gives
\[P\left(\left|X-500\right|\geq20\right)\leq\frac{1}{2.5^2}=0.16,\]
\[\therefore\quad P\left(480<X<520\right)\geq1-0.16=0.84.\]
At least $84\%$ of items are within the stated range --- guaranteed, whatever the
shape of the distribution. If the weights were known to be normal the answer would
be $0.9876$, but that would be an extra assumption, and the whole value of the
bound here is that it needs none.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Convergence in Probability}

\begin{definition}
A sequence of random variables $X_1,X_2,\ldots$ \emph{converges in probability} to
a constant $c$, written $X_n\overset{P}{\longrightarrow}c$, if for every
$\varepsilon>0$
\[\lim_{n\to\infty}P\left(\left|X_n-c\right|\geq\varepsilon\right)=0,
\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad
\lim_{n\to\infty}P\left(\left|X_n-c\right|<\varepsilon\right)=1.\]
\end{definition}

Read it as: fix any tolerance you like, however small; eventually it becomes
overwhelmingly likely that $X_n$ is within that tolerance of $c$. It does
\emph{not} say $X_n$ is ever equal to $c$, and it does not say the sequence stops
moving.

Chebyshev turns this into something checkable. If $E(X_n)\to c$ and
$\text{Var}(X_n)\to0$, then
\[P\left(\left|X_n-c\right|\geq\varepsilon\right)
\leq\frac{E\left[(X_n-c)^2\right]}{\varepsilon^2}\longrightarrow0,\]
so $X_n\overset{P}{\longrightarrow}c$. That is the standard route, and the one
used throughout the next chapter: an estimator whose mean square error tends to
zero is \textbf{consistent}, which is exactly this definition applied to
$T_n$ and $\tau(\theta)$ (see section~\ref{sec:consistency}).

\subsection{The Weak Law of Large Numbers}

\begin{thm}[Weak law of large numbers]
Let $X_1,X_2,\ldots,X_n$ be independent and identically distributed with mean
$\mu$ and finite variance $\sigma^2$. Then
\[\bar X_n=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^nX_i\ \overset{P}{\longrightarrow}\ \mu.\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
$E\left(\bar X_n\right)=\mu$ and
$\text{Var}\left(\bar X_n\right)=\frac{\sigma^2}{n}$. By Chebyshev, for any
$\varepsilon>0$,
\[P\left(\left|\bar X_n-\mu\right|\geq\varepsilon\right)
\leq\frac{\sigma^2}{n\varepsilon^2}\ \longrightarrow\ 0
\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.\]
\end{proof}

\begin{note}
The law says the sample mean approaches the population mean. It does not say the
\emph{sum} approaches $n\mu$ --- $\sum X_i$ has standard deviation
$\sigma\sqrt n$, which grows without bound. Dividing by $n$ is what makes the
scatter shrink, and it shrinks like $\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n}$: to halve the
uncertainty you need four times the data. That single fact governs the cost of
nearly every survey ever designed.
\end{note}

\subsection{Convergence in Distribution}

The law of large numbers says where $\bar X_n$ ends up. It says nothing about the
\emph{shape} of its scatter on the way, and that is what the rest of this section
is for. A different kind of convergence is needed, because the limit is a
distribution rather than a number.

\begin{definition}
A sequence $Y_1,Y_2,\ldots$ with distribution functions $F_n$ \emph{converges in
distribution} to a random variable $Y$ with distribution function $F$, written
$Y_n\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}Y$, if
\[\lim_{n\to\infty}F_n(y)=F(y)\]
at every point $y$ where $F$ is continuous. $F$ is then called the \emph{limiting
distribution} of the sequence.
\end{definition}

Convergence in probability to a constant implies convergence in distribution to
that constant; the reverse fails, since a limiting \emph{shape} says nothing about
where any particular $Y_n$ has landed.

\subsection{Finding a Limiting Distribution by Moment Generating Functions}

Distribution functions are awkward to take limits of. Moment generating functions
are not, and they determine the distribution, so the following is the practical
tool.

\begin{thm}[Continuity theorem]
Let $Y_n$ have moment generating function $M_n(t)$, defined for $|t|<h$ for every
$n$. If
\[\lim_{n\to\infty}M_n(t)=M(t)\qquad\text{for all }|t|<h,\]
and $M(t)$ is the moment generating function of a random variable $Y$, then
$Y_n\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}Y$.
\end{thm}

So a limiting distribution is found by taking the limit of a moment generating
function and recognising the answer. Two limits do most of the work:
\[\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(1+\frac{x}{n}\right)^n=e^x,\qquad
\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(1+\frac{x}{n}+\frac{\varphi(n)}{n}\right)^n=e^x
\quad\text{whenever }\varphi(n)\to0.\]
The second is the one that matters in practice: a Taylor expansion always leaves a
remainder, and this says a remainder that dies faster than $\frac1n$ may be
discarded.

\begin{example}
Let $Z_n\sim\text{POI}(n)$. Find the limiting distribution of
\[Y_n=\frac{Z_n-n}{\sqrt n}.\]
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
A Poisson variable with mean $n$ has $M_{Z_n}(t)=e^{n\left(e^t-1\right)}$, so
\[M_{Y_n}(t)=E\left[e^{t\frac{Z_n-n}{\sqrt n}}\right]
=e^{-t\sqrt n}\,M_{Z_n}\!\left(\frac{t}{\sqrt n}\right)
=\exp\left[-t\sqrt n+n\left(e^{\frac{t}{\sqrt n}}-1\right)\right].\]
Expand the exponential inside:
\[e^{\frac{t}{\sqrt n}}-1=\frac{t}{\sqrt n}+\frac{t^2}{2n}
+\frac{t^3}{6n^{\frac32}}+\cdots,\]
so
\[n\left(e^{\frac{t}{\sqrt n}}-1\right)=t\sqrt n+\frac{t^2}{2}
+\frac{t^3}{6\sqrt n}+\cdots\]
The $t\sqrt n$ cancels against $-t\sqrt n$, and every remaining term after
$\frac{t^2}{2}$ carries a power of $\frac{1}{\sqrt n}$:
\[\ln M_{Y_n}(t)=\frac{t^2}{2}+O\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt n}\right)
\ \longrightarrow\ \frac{t^2}{2},\]
\[\therefore\quad M_{Y_n}(t)\longrightarrow e^{\frac{t^2}{2}},\]
which is the moment generating function of the standard normal. Hence
\[\frac{Z_n-n}{\sqrt n}\ \overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\ N(0,1).\]
\end{solution}

\begin{note}
The cancellation of $t\sqrt n$ is not a lucky accident --- it is what subtracting
the mean is for. Dividing by $\sqrt n$ then leaves the $t^2$ term at exactly order
one. Centre by the mean, scale by the standard deviation, and a normal limit is
what falls out; that is the pattern of the next result, in general.
\end{note}

\subsection{The Central Limit Theorem}

\begin{thm}[Central limit theorem]
Let $X_1,X_2,\ldots,X_n$ be independent and identically distributed with mean
$\mu$ and finite variance $\sigma^2>0$. Then
\[Z_n=\frac{\bar X_n-\mu}{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n}}
=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^nX_i-n\mu}{\sigma\sqrt n}
\ \overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\ N(0,1).\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}[\textit{Sketch}.]
Write $W_i=\frac{X_i-\mu}{\sigma}$, so the $W_i$ are independent with mean $0$ and
variance $1$, and $Z_n=\frac{1}{\sqrt n}\sum W_i$. If $W$ has moment generating
function $m(t)$ then $m(0)=1$, $m'(0)=0$ and $m''(0)=1$, so
\[m(t)=1+\frac{t^2}{2}+\varphi(t),\qquad
\frac{\varphi(t)}{t^2}\to0\ \text{ as }t\to0.\]
By independence,
\[M_{Z_n}(t)=\left[m\!\left(\frac{t}{\sqrt n}\right)\right]^n
=\left[1+\frac{t^2}{2n}+\varphi\!\left(\frac{t}{\sqrt n}\right)\right]^n,\]
and the remainder is of smaller order than $\frac1n$, so the second limit quoted
above applies:
\[M_{Z_n}(t)\longrightarrow e^{\frac{t^2}{2}}.\]
\end{proof}

\begin{note}
Three things the theorem does \emph{not} say, each a common misreading.

It is not a statement about the observations. The $X_i$ do not become normal, and
never do; a sample from a skewed population stays skewed however large it is. It
is $\bar X$ whose distribution becomes normal.

It is not a statement about any fixed $n$. ``$n\geq30$'' is a rule of thumb, not
part of the theorem. Thirty is generous for a symmetric population and nowhere
near enough for a badly skewed one.

It needs a finite variance. Average a sample from a Cauchy distribution and
$\bar X$ has exactly the same Cauchy distribution as a single observation, no
matter how large $n$ is --- averaging buys nothing at all, because the variance
does not exist.
\end{note}

\subsection{Normal Approximations in Practice}

For a discrete variable the approximating normal density is continuous, so a
probability at a single point would come out as zero. The repair is the
\textbf{continuity correction}: give each integer the unit interval around it,
replacing $P(Y=k)$ by $P\left(k-\frac12<Y<k+\frac12\right)$ and
$P(Y\leq k)$ by $P\left(Y<k+\frac12\right)$.

\begin{example}
Let $Y\sim BIN(80,0.4)$. Use a normal approximation to find (a) $P(Y=32)$ and
(b) $P(Y\leq30)$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Here $\mu=np=32$ and $\sigma^2=np(1-p)=80(0.4)(0.6)=19.2$, so
$\sigma=4.3818$.

\textbf{(a).} With the continuity correction,
\[P(Y=32)\approx P\left(31.5<Y<32.5\right)
=P\left(\frac{-0.5}{4.3818}<Z<\frac{0.5}{4.3818}\right)
=P(-0.114<Z<0.114),\]
\[P(Y=32)\approx0.5454-0.4546=0.0908,\]
against an exact binomial probability of $0.0908$.

Keep the third decimal place in $z$ here. Rounding to $z=\pm0.11$ gives $0.0876$,
which is out by nearly $4\%$ --- the interval is so narrow that its width, and so
the answer, is proportional to $z$ itself.

\textbf{(b).}
\[P(Y\leq30)\approx P\left(Y<30.5\right)
=P\left(Z<\frac{30.5-32}{4.3818}\right)=P(Z<-0.34)=0.3669,\]
against an exact value of $0.3687$.
\end{solution}

\begin{note}
Without the correction, (a) would have given $P(Y=32)\approx0$ and (b)
$P\left(Z<\frac{30-32}{4.3818}\right)=P(Z<-0.46)=0.3228$ --- an error of nearly
five percentage points on a probability of about $0.37$. The correction costs one
line and is worth it every time the variable is discrete.
\end{note}

\section{Practice problems: distributions and transformations}

Problems on moments, joint distributions and transformations. Work each one
before opening the solution.

\begin{problem}
Prove that
\[E\left(X^{(j)}\right)=(-k)^{(j)}\left(\frac{p-1}{p}\right)^j,\qquad j=1,2,3,\ldots\]
if $X\sim NB(k,p)$ with probability function
\[f(x)=\binom{-k}{x}p^k(-q)^x,\qquad x=0,1,2,\ldots\]
and hence find $\text{Var}(X)$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Throughout, $a^{(j)}$ is the falling factorial $a(a-1)\cdots(a-j+1)$, so
$X^{(j)}=X(X-1)\cdots(X-j+1)$ and $E\left(X^{(j)}\right)$ is the $j$th
\emph{factorial} moment. Note also that $q=1-p$, so $-q=p-1$.

Start from the negative binomial series, valid for $|z|<1$:
\[\sum_{x=0}^\infty\binom{-k}{x}z^x=(1+z)^{-k}.\]
Differentiating $j$ times term by term,
\begin{align*}
\sum_{x=0}^\infty x^{(j)}\binom{-k}{x}z^{x-j}
&=\frac{d^j}{dz^j}(1+z)^{-k}
=(-k)(-k-1)\cdots(-k-j+1)(1+z)^{-k-j}\\
&=(-k)^{(j)}(1+z)^{-k-j},
\end{align*}
and multiplying through by $z^j$,
\[\sum_{x=0}^\infty x^{(j)}\binom{-k}{x}z^x=(-k)^{(j)}z^j(1+z)^{-k-j}.\]
Now put $z=-q$ and multiply by $p^k$, which is exactly the expectation asked for:
\begin{align*}
E\left(X^{(j)}\right)&=p^k(-k)^{(j)}(-q)^j(1-q)^{-k-j}
=p^k(-k)^{(j)}(-q)^jp^{-k-j}\\
&=(-k)^{(j)}\left(\frac{-q}{p}\right)^j
=(-k)^{(j)}\left(\frac{p-1}{p}\right)^j.\qquad\blacksquare
\end{align*}
(Taking $j=0$ gives $p^kp^{-k}=1$, so the probabilities do sum to one.)

For the variance, read off the first two factorial moments:
\[E(X)=(-k)\left(\frac{-q}{p}\right)=\frac{kq}{p},\qquad
E\left[X(X-1)\right]=(-k)(-k-1)\left(\frac{-q}{p}\right)^2
=\frac{k(k+1)q^2}{p^2}.\]
Since $E\left(X^2\right)=E\left[X(X-1)\right]+E(X)$,
\[\text{Var}(X)=\frac{k(k+1)q^2}{p^2}+\frac{kq}{p}-\frac{k^2q^2}{p^2}
=\frac{kq^2}{p^2}+\frac{kq}{p}
=\frac{kq}{p^2}\left(q+p\right)
\]
\[\therefore\quad\text{Var}(X)=\frac{kq}{p^2}.\]

\begin{note}
Factorial moments exist because $E\left[X(X-1)\cdots\right]$ is easier to sum than
$E\left(X^j\right)$ for the discrete distributions: the falling factorial cancels
against the factorials inside the probability function. Converting back is
mechanical --- $E\left(X^2\right)=E\left[X(X-1)\right]+E(X)$, and so on.

The corresponding gamma result, $E\left(X^k\right)=\beta^k(k+\alpha-1)^{(k)}$,
is worked in the first of the review problems at the end of the notes, so it is
not repeated here.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be continuous random variables with joint p.d.f.
\[f(x,y)=k(x+y),\qquad 0\leq x\leq y\leq1.\]
Find the
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] value of the constant $k$;
\item[(b).] joint c.d.f. of $X$ and $Y$, $F(x,y)$;
\item[(c).] marginal c.d.f. of $X$ and marginal c.d.f. of $Y$;
\item[(d).] marginal p.d.f. of $X$ and marginal p.d.f. of $Y$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The support is the triangle $0\leq x\leq y\leq1$ --- \emph{not} the unit square.
Every integral below has to respect that, and it is where most of the marks are.

\textbf{(a).} Integrating $x$ from $0$ to $y$, then $y$ from $0$ to $1$,
\[1=k\int_0^1\!\!\int_0^y(x+y)\,dx\,dy
=k\int_0^1\left[\frac{x^2}{2}+xy\right]_0^y dy
=k\int_0^1\frac{3y^2}{2}\,dy
=\frac{k}{2},\]
\[\therefore\quad k=2.\]

\textbf{(b).} The joint c.d.f. splits into cases according to whether the corner
