$(x,y)$ lies above or below the line $u=v$.

For $0\leq x\leq y\leq1$ the region $\{U\leq x,\ V\leq y\}$ meets the support in
$\{0\leq u\leq x,\ u\leq v\leq y\}$, so
\[F(x,y)=\int_0^x\!\!\int_u^y2(u+v)\,dv\,du
=\int_0^x\left[2uv+v^2\right]_u^y du
=\int_0^x\left(2uy+y^2-3u^2\right)du,\]
\[F(x,y)=x^2y+xy^2-x^3.\]

For $0\leq y\leq x$ with $y\leq1$ the constraint $U\leq x$ is not binding, because
the support already forces $U\leq V\leq y\leq x$. So $F(x,y)=P(V\leq y)=y^3$.
Collecting the cases,
\[F(x,y)=\begin{cases}
0, & x<0\ \text{or}\ y<0,\\[2pt]
x^2y+xy^2-x^3, & 0\leq x\leq y\leq1,\\[2pt]
y^3, & 0\leq y\leq x,\ y\leq1,\\[2pt]
x^2+x-x^3, & 0\leq x\leq1,\ y>1,\\[2pt]
1, & x>1,\ y>1.
\end{cases}\]
The two middle rows agree on $x=y$, where both give $y^3$, so $F$ is continuous
across the diagonal --- worth checking, because a mistake in the limits almost
always shows up as a jump there.

\textbf{(c).} Let $y\to1$ and $x\to1$ in turn:
\[F_X(x)=F(x,1)=x^2+x-x^3,\quad 0\leq x\leq1;\qquad
F_Y(y)=F(1,y)=y^3,\quad 0\leq y\leq1,\]
each $0$ below its range and $1$ above.

\textbf{(d).} Differentiating the marginal c.d.f.s,
\[f_X(x)=2x+1-3x^2,\quad 0\leq x\leq1;\qquad f_Y(y)=3y^2,\quad 0\leq y\leq1.\]
The same answers come from integrating the joint density directly,
\[f_X(x)=\int_x^12(x+y)\,dy=2x+1-3x^2,\qquad
f_Y(y)=\int_0^y2(x+y)\,dx=3y^2,\]
and both integrate to $1$ over $[0,1]$.

\begin{note}
The limits are the whole question. $f_X$ integrates $y$ from $x$ to $1$, not $0$
to $1$, because on the support $Y$ is never below $X$; $f_Y$ integrates $x$ from
$0$ to $y$ for the same reason. Sketch the triangle before writing anything down.

Note also that $X$ and $Y$ are \emph{not} independent here: $f_X(x)f_Y(y)$ is not
$2(x+y)$, and in any case a triangular support rules independence out on its own.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Given that $X$ is a continuous random variable with p.d.f.
\[f(x)=kx^{-(\theta+1)},\qquad x>\alpha;\ \theta>0,\ \alpha>0,\]
find the
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] value of the constant $k$;
\item[(b).] p.d.f. of $Y=\theta\ln\left(\frac{X}{\alpha}\right)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a).} Since $\theta>0$ the integral converges at infinity:
\[1=\int_\alpha^\infty kx^{-(\theta+1)}dx
=k\left[\frac{x^{-\theta}}{-\theta}\right]_\alpha^\infty
=\frac{k\alpha^{-\theta}}{\theta}
\qquad\implies\qquad k=\theta\alpha^\theta.\]
This is the Pareto distribution with scale $\alpha$ and shape $\theta$.

\textbf{(b).} The transformation $y=\theta\ln\left(\frac{x}{\alpha}\right)$ is
strictly increasing, mapping $x>\alpha$ onto $y>0$, with inverse and derivative
\[x=\alpha e^{\frac{y}{\theta}},\qquad
\frac{dx}{dy}=\frac{\alpha}{\theta}e^{\frac{y}{\theta}}.\]
Hence
\[f_Y(y)=f_X\!\left(\alpha e^{\frac{y}{\theta}}\right)\left|\frac{dx}{dy}\right|
=\theta\alpha^\theta\left(\alpha e^{\frac{y}{\theta}}\right)^{-(\theta+1)}
\cdot\frac{\alpha}{\theta}e^{\frac{y}{\theta}}
=\alpha^{\theta-\theta-1+1}\,
e^{-\frac{(\theta+1)y}{\theta}+\frac{y}{\theta}}
=e^{-y},\]
\[\therefore\quad Y\sim\text{EXP}(1),\qquad y>0.\]

\begin{note}
Every power of $\alpha$ and $\theta$ cancels: the answer does not depend on either
parameter. That is the point of the question. A Pareto variable is an exponential
one in disguise, which is why $\ln X$ turns Pareto data into something a
$t$-test or a regression can handle.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Let $X$ have p.d.f. $f(x)=\frac{x^2}{9}$, $0<x<3$. Find the p.d.f. of
$Y=X^3$.
\item[(b).] Let $X\sim\text{UNIF}(0,1)$. Find the p.d.f. of
(i) $Y=e^{-X}$ and (ii) $V=X(1-X)$.
\item[(c).] Let $X$ have p.d.f. $f(x)=\frac{1}{24}x^2$, $-2<x<4$. Find the p.d.f.
of $Y=X^2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a).} $y=x^3$ is increasing, taking $0<x<3$ onto $0<y<27$, with
$x=y^{\frac13}$ and $\frac{dx}{dy}=\frac13y^{-\frac23}$. So
\[f_Y(y)=\frac{\left(y^{\frac13}\right)^2}{9}\cdot\frac{1}{3}y^{-\frac23}
=\frac{y^{\frac23}}{9}\cdot\frac{y^{-\frac23}}{3}=\frac{1}{27},
\qquad 0<y<27,\]
\[\therefore\quad Y\sim\text{UNIF}(0,27).\]

\textbf{(b)(i).} $y=e^{-x}$ is \emph{decreasing}, taking $0<x<1$ onto
$e^{-1}<y<1$, with $x=-\ln y$ and $\left|\frac{dx}{dy}\right|=\frac{1}{y}$. Since
$f_X\equiv1$,
\[f_Y(y)=\frac{1}{y},\qquad \frac{1}{e}<y<1,\]
and $\int_{1/e}^1\frac{dy}{y}=\ln1-\ln e^{-1}=1$, as it must.

\textbf{(ii).} Here $v=x(1-x)$ is \textbf{not} monotone on $(0,1)$: it rises to
$\frac14$ at $x=\frac12$ and falls back. The change-of-variable formula does not
apply directly, so work with the distribution function.

Solving $x^2-x+v=0$ gives the two roots $x=\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1-4v}}{2}$, and
$X(1-X)\leq v$ holds outside them:
\[F_V(v)=P\left(X\leq\frac{1-\sqrt{1-4v}}{2}\right)
+P\left(X\geq\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4v}}{2}\right).\]
As $X$ is uniform on $(0,1)$ these probabilities are the lengths themselves:
\[F_V(v)=\frac{1-\sqrt{1-4v}}{2}+1-\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4v}}{2}=1-\sqrt{1-4v}.\]
Differentiating,
\[f_V(v)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{1-4v}},\qquad 0<v<\frac14,\]
which integrates to $\left[-\sqrt{1-4v}\right]_0^{1/4}=1$.

\textbf{(c).} Again $y=x^2$ is not monotone on $(-2,4)$, and this time the two
branches cover \emph{different} ranges. For $0<y<4$ both $x=\pm\sqrt y$ lie in
$(-2,4)$; for $4<y<16$ only $x=+\sqrt y$ does, since $-\sqrt y<-2$. With
$f_X(x)=\frac{x^2}{24}$ and $\left|\frac{dx}{dy}\right|=\frac{1}{2\sqrt y}$,
\[f_Y(y)=\begin{cases}
\dfrac{2\cdot\frac{y}{24}}{2\sqrt y}=\dfrac{\sqrt y}{24}, & 0<y<4,\\[10pt]
\dfrac{\frac{y}{24}}{2\sqrt y}=\dfrac{\sqrt y}{48}, & 4<y<16.
\end{cases}\]
As a check,
\[\int_0^4\frac{\sqrt y}{24}\,dy+\int_4^{16}\frac{\sqrt y}{48}\,dy
=\frac{16}{72}+\frac{112}{144}=\frac{2}{9}+\frac{7}{9}=1.\]

\begin{note}
Parts (b)(ii) and (c) are the whole reason this question is set. A transformation
that is not one-to-one has to go through the distribution function, and the
support of $Y$ has to be worked out branch by branch --- in (c) the density
\emph{halves} at $y=4$, because above that point only one branch survives.
Writing $f_Y(y)=\frac{\sqrt y}{24}$ across the whole of $0<y<16$ is the standard
mistake, and it integrates to $\frac{16}{9}$, not $1$.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Let $X$ have the binomial p.f.
$f(x)=\binom{3}{x}\left(\frac23\right)^x\left(\frac13\right)^{3-x}$, $x=0,1,2,3$.
Find the p.f. of $Y=X^2$.
\item[(b).] Let $X$ have p.f. $f(x)=\frac13$, $x=1,2,3$. Find the p.f. of
$Y=2X+1$.
\item[(c).] Suppose $X$ has p.f. $f(x)=\left(\frac12\right)^x$, $x=1,2,3,\ldots$
Find the p.f. of $Y=X^3$.
\item[(d).] If $X\sim BIN(n,p)$, find the p.f. of $Y=n-X$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
For a discrete variable there is no Jacobian. When the map is one-to-one the
probabilities simply move with the values: $f_Y(y)=f_X\left(w(y)\right)$, where
$w$ is the inverse.

\textbf{(a).} On $x=0,1,2,3$ the map $y=x^2$ is one-to-one (no negative values to
collide), so $Y$ takes $0,1,4,9$ with $x=\sqrt y$:
\[f_Y(y)=\binom{3}{\sqrt y}\left(\frac23\right)^{\sqrt y}
\left(\frac13\right)^{3-\sqrt y},\qquad y=0,1,4,9,\]
that is
\[f_Y(0)=\frac{1}{27},\quad f_Y(1)=\frac{6}{27},\quad
f_Y(4)=\frac{12}{27},\quad f_Y(9)=\frac{8}{27},\]
which total $\frac{27}{27}=1$.

\textbf{(b).} $y=2x+1$ carries $x=1,2,3$ to $y=3,5,7$, and the probabilities go
with them:
\[f_Y(y)=\frac13,\qquad y=3,5,7.\]

\textbf{(c).} $y=x^3$ carries $x=1,2,3,\ldots$ to the cubes $y=1,8,27,\ldots$,
with $x=y^{\frac13}$:
\[f_Y(y)=\left(\frac12\right)^{y^{1/3}},\qquad y=1,8,27,64,\ldots\]

\textbf{(d).} $Y=n-X$ takes the values $y=0,1,\ldots,n$, and
\[f_Y(y)=P(X=n-y)=\binom{n}{n-y}p^{n-y}q^{y}=\binom{n}{y}q^{y}p^{n-y},\]
using $\binom{n}{n-y}=\binom{n}{y}$.
\[\therefore\quad Y\sim BIN(n,q)=BIN(n,1-p).\]
This is obvious once stated: if $X$ counts the successes in $n$ trials then
$n-X$ counts the failures, and a failure has probability $q$.

\begin{note}
Part (c) is printed with the support $x=1,2,3$, which cannot be right:
$\frac12+\frac14+\frac18=\frac78$, not $1$. The intended support is
$x=1,2,3,\ldots$, and it is taken that way above. Part (b) is genuinely finite,
since $\frac13$ three times does sum to $1$ --- which is presumably how the
misprint survived.
\end{note}
\end{solution}


\subsection{Further exercises}

The remaining problems are written for these notes rather than taken from a past
paper. They cover the same ground: transformations, sums and ratios, sampling
distributions and order statistics.

\begin{problem}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent $\text{EXP}(1)$ variables. Find the
p.d.f. of $U=\frac{X}{Y}$.
\item[(b).] Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent $N\left(0,\sigma^2\right)$ variables.
Show that $T=\frac{X}{Y}$ has the Cauchy density
$f_T(t)=\frac{1}{\pi\left(1+t^2\right)}$, whatever the value of $\sigma$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a).} Put $U=\frac{X}{Y}$ and $V=Y$, so $x=uv$, $y=v$ and
\[|J|=\left|\begin{matrix}v & u\\0 & 1\end{matrix}\right|=v.\]
Then for $u>0$, $v>0$,
\[f_{U,V}(u,v)=e^{-uv}e^{-v}\cdot v=v\,e^{-v(1+u)},\]
\[f_U(u)=\int_0^\infty v\,e^{-v(1+u)}dv=\frac{1}{(1+u)^2},\qquad u>0,\]
the integral being a gamma integral with shape $2$ and scale $\frac{1}{1+u}$.
As a check, $\int_0^\infty(1+u)^{-2}du=1$.

\textbf{(b).} By symmetry it is enough to work with the density directly. With
$T=\frac{X}{Y}$ and $V=Y$ the same Jacobian gives $|J|=|v|$, and
\[f_{T,V}(t,v)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma^2}
\exp\left[-\frac{(tv)^2+v^2}{2\sigma^2}\right]|v|,\]
so, using the symmetry in $v$ to fold the integral onto $v>0$,
\[f_T(t)=2\int_0^\infty\frac{v}{2\pi\sigma^2}
\exp\left[-\frac{v^2\left(1+t^2\right)}{2\sigma^2}\right]dv.\]
Substituting $w=v^2$, $dw=2v\,dv$,
\[f_T(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma^2}\int_0^\infty
\exp\left[-\frac{w\left(1+t^2\right)}{2\sigma^2}\right]dw
=\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma^2}\cdot\frac{2\sigma^2}{1+t^2}
=\frac{1}{\pi\left(1+t^2\right)}.\]
Every $\sigma$ has cancelled.

\begin{note}
The Cauchy distribution has no mean, so it has no variance either --- the
integral $\int t\,f_T(t)\,dt$ diverges at both ends. It is the standard warning
that a ratio of well-behaved variables need not be well behaved: both $X$ and $Y$
have every moment, and their ratio has none.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent $\text{UNIF}(0,1)$ variables. Find the p.d.f. of
their product $V=XY$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Put $U=X$ and $V=XY$, so $x=u$, $y=\frac{v}{u}$ and $|J|=\frac{1}{u}$. Both
original variables lie in $(0,1)$, so the image is $0<v<u<1$, and there
\[f_{U,V}(u,v)=1\cdot\frac{1}{u}=\frac{1}{u}.\]
Integrating $u$ out over $v<u<1$,
\[f_V(v)=\int_v^1\frac{du}{u}=\left[\ln u\right]_v^1=-\ln v,\qquad 0<v<1.\]
As a check, $\int_0^1(-\ln v)\,dv=\left[v-v\ln v\right]_0^1=1$.

\begin{note}
The density is unbounded as $v\to0^+$, yet integrates to $1$. A density may be
infinite at a point without anything going wrong --- it is the area that must be
finite, not the height. Note also the shape: multiplying two numbers below $1$
usually gives something much smaller than either, which is why the mass piles up
near zero.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X\sim\text{GAM}(\alpha,1)$ and $Y\sim\text{GAM}(\beta,1)$ be independent.
Define
\[U=X+Y,\qquad V=\frac{X}{X+Y}.\]
Show that $U\sim\text{GAM}(\alpha+\beta,1)$, that $V\sim\text{BETA}
(\alpha,\beta)$, and that $U$ and $V$ are independent.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Inverting, $x=uv$ and $y=u(1-v)$, with
\[|J|=\left|\begin{matrix}v & u\\ 1-v & -u\end{matrix}\right|
=\left|-uv-u(1-v)\right|=u.\]
The image of $\{x>0,y>0\}$ is $\{u>0,\ 0<v<1\}$. Substituting,
\[f_{U,V}(u,v)=\frac{(uv)^{\alpha-1}e^{-uv}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\cdot
\frac{\left[u(1-v)\right]^{\beta-1}e^{-u(1-v)}}{\Gamma(\beta)}\cdot u
=\frac{u^{\alpha+\beta-1}e^{-u}\,v^{\alpha-1}(1-v)^{\beta-1}}
{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)}.\]
Multiply and divide by $\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)$ to split it:
\[f_{U,V}(u,v)=
\underbrace{\frac{u^{\alpha+\beta-1}e^{-u}}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}}
_{\text{GAM}(\alpha+\beta,\,1)}\cdot
\underbrace{\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)}
v^{\alpha-1}(1-v)^{\beta-1}}_{\text{BETA}(\alpha,\,\beta)}.\]
The joint density is a function of $u$ alone times a function of $v$ alone, over a
rectangular region, so $U$ and $V$ are independent with the stated marginals.
$\blacksquare$

\begin{note}
Independence here is genuinely surprising: $V$ is built out of $X$ and $U$, so it
is not obvious that knowing the total tells you nothing about the split. It is
particular to the gamma family, and it is the reason the beta distribution turns
up wherever proportions of exponential or gamma quantities are modelled.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Let $X\sim\text{GAM}(\alpha,\beta)$. Show that
$Y=\frac{2X}{\beta}\sim\chi^2(2\alpha)$.
\item[(b).] Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be independent $\text{EXP}(\lambda)$ variables,
each with mean $\lambda$. Use moment generating functions to find the distribution
of $\sum_{i=1}^nX_i$, and write down its mean and variance.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a).} The map is increasing with $x=\frac{\beta y}{2}$ and
$\frac{dx}{dy}=\frac{\beta}{2}$, so
\[f_Y(y)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha)\beta^\alpha}
\left(\frac{\beta y}{2}\right)^{\alpha-1}e^{-\frac{y}{2}}\cdot\frac{\beta}{2}
=\frac{1}{2^{\alpha}\Gamma(\alpha)}y^{\alpha-1}e^{-\frac{y}{2}},\qquad y>0.\]
This is the $\chi^2$ density with $2\alpha$ degrees of freedom, since
$\frac{2\alpha}{2}=\alpha$. $\blacksquare$

\textbf{(b).} Each $X_i$ has $M(t)=(1-\lambda t)^{-1}$ for $t<\frac1\lambda$, so
by independence
\[M_{\sum X_i}(t)=\prod_{i=1}^n(1-\lambda t)^{-1}=(1-\lambda t)^{-n},\]
which is the moment generating function of $\text{GAM}(n,\lambda)$. Hence
\[\sum_{i=1}^nX_i\sim\text{GAM}(n,\lambda),\qquad
E\left(\sum X_i\right)=n\lambda,\qquad
\text{Var}\left(\sum X_i\right)=n\lambda^2.\]

\begin{note}
Part (a) is used constantly in chapter 3. Every exponential or gamma sum there is
converted to a chi-square so that a critical value can be read from a table ---
$\frac{2}{\theta_0}\sum X_i\sim\chi^2(2n)$ is exactly (a) applied to the
$\text{GAM}(n,\theta_0)$ of (b).
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $Y_1$ and $Y_2$ be independent $N\left(\mu,\sigma^2\right)$ variables. Find
the distribution of
\[U=\frac{Y_1-3Y_2}{2}.\]
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
A linear combination of independent normal variables is normal, so only the mean
and variance are needed:
\[E(U)=\frac{\mu-3\mu}{2}=-\mu,\]
\[\text{Var}(U)=\frac{1}{4}\left[\text{Var}(Y_1)+9\,\text{Var}(Y_2)\right]
=\frac{1}{4}\left(\sigma^2+9\sigma^2\right)=\frac{5\sigma^2}{2}.\]
\[\therefore\quad U\sim N\!\left(-\mu,\ \frac{5\sigma^2}{2}\right).\]
Formally, $M_U(t)=M_{Y_1}\!\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)
M_{Y_2}\!\left(-\frac{3t}{2}\right)$, and multiplying the two normal moment
generating functions gives
$\exp\left(-\mu t+\frac{5\sigma^2t^2}{4}\right)$, as stated.

\begin{note}
The coefficients are squared in the variance but not in the mean, and the minus
sign disappears there. Subtracting increases the spread just as adding does ---
variances of independent variables always accumulate.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent, each $\text{EXP}(\theta)$ with density
$\frac{1}{\theta}e^{-\frac{x}{\theta}}$ on $x>0$. Find the p.d.f. of $U=X-Y$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Use the convolution formula, taking care with the support. For $u>0$ the
constraint $y>0$ and $x=u+y>0$ reduces to $y>0$:
\[f_U(u)=\int_0^\infty f_X(u+y)f_Y(y)\,dy
=\frac{1}{\theta^2}\int_0^\infty e^{-\frac{u+y}{\theta}}e^{-\frac{y}{\theta}}dy
=\frac{e^{-\frac{u}{\theta}}}{\theta^2}\int_0^\infty e^{-\frac{2y}{\theta}}dy
=\frac{e^{-\frac{u}{\theta}}}{2\theta}.\]
For $u<0$ the roles of $X$ and $Y$ swap, and since they are identically
distributed the answer is the mirror image. Combining,
\[f_U(u)=\frac{1}{2\theta}e^{-\frac{|u|}{\theta}},\qquad-\infty<u<\infty,\]
the \textbf{Laplace} (double exponential) distribution. It integrates to
$2\cdot\frac{1}{2\theta}\cdot\theta=1$.

\begin{note}
Do the $u>0$ case and then invoke symmetry --- do not attempt both integrals.
The symmetry is exact because $X-Y$ and $Y-X$ have the same distribution when $X$
and $Y$ are independent and identically distributed, and that single observation
halves the work.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent $N\left(0,\sigma^2\right)$ variables. Find the
distribution of $W=X^2+Y^2$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Standardise first: $\frac{X}{\sigma}$ and $\frac{Y}{\sigma}$ are independent
$N(0,1)$, so
\[\frac{W}{\sigma^2}=\left(\frac{X}{\sigma}\right)^2
+\left(\frac{Y}{\sigma}\right)^2\sim\chi^2(2),\]
being a sum of two independent squared standard normals. Since
$\chi^2(2)=\text{GAM}(1,2)$ is the exponential distribution with mean $2$,
\[W\sim\text{EXP}\left(\text{mean }2\sigma^2\right),\qquad
f_W(w)=\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}e^{-\frac{w}{2\sigma^2}},\quad w>0.\]

\begin{note}
$W$ is the squared distance from the origin of a point scattered symmetrically in
the plane, and the result says that squared distance is exponential. It is the
reason a two-dimensional Gaussian target produces exponentially distributed miss
distances squared --- and, read backwards, it is how normal random numbers are
generated from uniform ones.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from $N\left(\mu,\sigma^2\right)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Use moment generating functions to find the distribution of $\bar X$,
and state its mean and variance.
\item[(b).] Show that
$\frac{\bar X-\mu}{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n}}\sim N(0,1)$, that
$\left(\frac{\bar X-\mu}{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n}}\right)^2\sim\chi^2(1)$, and that
$\sum_{i=1}^n\left(\frac{X_i-\mu}{\sigma}\right)^2\sim\chi^2(n)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a).} Each $X_i$ has $M(t)=\exp\left(\mu t+\frac{\sigma^2t^2}{2}\right)$.
By independence,
\[M_{\bar X}(t)=E\left[e^{\frac{t}{n}\sum X_i}\right]
=\left[M\!\left(\frac{t}{n}\right)\right]^n
=\left[\exp\left(\frac{\mu t}{n}+\frac{\sigma^2t^2}{2n^2}\right)\right]^n
=\exp\left(\mu t+\frac{\left(\frac{\sigma^2}{n}\right)t^2}{2}\right),\]
which is the moment generating function of a normal distribution with mean $\mu$
and variance $\frac{\sigma^2}{n}$:
\[\bar X\sim N\!\left(\mu,\ \frac{\sigma^2}{n}\right),\qquad
E\left(\bar X\right)=\mu,\qquad
\text{Var}\left(\bar X\right)=\frac{\sigma^2}{n}.\]

\textbf{(b).} Standardising a normal variable gives a standard normal, and by (a)
the standard deviation of $\bar X$ is $\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n}$, so the first
statement is immediate. The square of a standard normal is $\chi^2(1)$ by
definition, giving the second. For the third, each
$\frac{X_i-\mu}{\sigma}\sim N(0,1)$ and they are independent, so the sum of their
squares is a sum of $n$ independent $\chi^2(1)$ variables, which is $\chi^2(n)$
because degrees of freedom add.

\begin{note}
Only $\mu$ is used here, not $\bar X$, in the third part. Replacing $\mu$ by
$\bar X$ costs one degree of freedom and gives
$\frac{(n-1)S^2}{\sigma^2}\sim\chi^2(n-1)$ --- the extra estimated quantity is
paid for exactly once.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{GAM}(\alpha,\beta)$. Find the
distribution of $\bar X$, together with its mean, variance and moment generating
function.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Each $X_i$ has $M(t)=(1-\beta t)^{-\alpha}$, so
\[M_{\bar X}(t)=\left[M\!\left(\frac{t}{n}\right)\right]^n
=\left(1-\frac{\beta t}{n}\right)^{-n\alpha},\]
which is the moment generating function of a gamma distribution with shape
$n\alpha$ and scale $\frac{\beta}{n}$:
\[\bar X\sim\text{GAM}\!\left(n\alpha,\ \frac{\beta}{n}\right),\qquad
f_{\bar X}(w)=\frac{w^{n\alpha-1}e^{-\frac{nw}{\beta}}}
{\Gamma(n\alpha)\left(\frac{\beta}{n}\right)^{n\alpha}},\quad w>0.\]
Its moments follow from the gamma formulas $E=\text{shape}\times\text{scale}$ and
$\text{Var}=\text{shape}\times\text{scale}^2$:
\[E\left(\bar X\right)=n\alpha\cdot\frac{\beta}{n}=\alpha\beta,\qquad
\text{Var}\left(\bar X\right)=n\alpha\left(\frac{\beta}{n}\right)^2
=\frac{\alpha\beta^2}{n}.\]

\begin{note}
The mean is unchanged and the variance is divided by $n$, exactly as for the
normal case --- but here the \emph{shape} of $\bar X$ changes with $n$ as well.
As $n$ grows the shape parameter $n\alpha$ grows, and a gamma distribution with
large shape looks increasingly normal. That is the central limit theorem visible
in a single formula.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_6$ be a random sample from $\text{UNIF}(0,1)$, with order
statistics $Y_1<Y_2<\cdots<Y_6$. Find the joint p.d.f. of $Y_2$ and $Y_5$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
For $i<j$ the joint density of $Y_i$ and $Y_j$ is
\begin{align*}
f(y_i,y_j)={}&\frac{n!}{(i-1)!\,(j-i-1)!\,(n-j)!}\\
&\times\left[F(y_i)\right]^{i-1}
\left[F(y_j)-F(y_i)\right]^{j-i-1}
\left[1-F(y_j)\right]^{n-j}f(y_i)f(y_j),
\end{align*}
the multinomial count of the ways to place $i-1$ observations below $y_i$, one
at $y_i$, $j-i-1$ between, one at $y_j$ and $n-j$ above.

Here $n=6$, $i=2$, $j=5$, and for the uniform distribution $F(y)=y$ and
$f(y)=1$ on $(0,1)$. The constant is
\[\frac{6!}{1!\,2!\,1!}=\frac{720}{2}=360,\]
\[\therefore\quad f(y_2,y_5)=360\,y_2\left(y_5-y_2\right)^2\left(1-y_5\right),
\qquad 0<y_2<y_5<1.\]
Integrating over the triangle confirms a total mass of $1$.

\begin{note}
Each factor is doing visible work: $y_2$ is the chance one observation falls
below $Y_2$, $(y_5-y_2)^2$ that two fall in the middle, $(1-y_5)$ that one falls
above. Write the picture down first and the formula assembles itself.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from the exponential distribution with
mean $\theta$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Show that $Y_1=\min\left(X_1,\ldots,X_n\right)$ is exponential with
mean $\frac{\theta}{n}$.
\item[(b).] Find the p.d.f. of $Y_n=\max\left(X_1,\ldots,X_n\right)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Here $F(x)=1-e^{-\frac{x}{\theta}}$ for $x>0$.

\textbf{(a).} The minimum exceeds $y$ exactly when all $n$ observations do:
\[P\left(Y_1>y\right)=\left[1-F(y)\right]^n
=\left[e^{-\frac{y}{\theta}}\right]^n=e^{-\frac{ny}{\theta}},\]
which is the survival function of an exponential distribution with mean
$\frac{\theta}{n}$. Differentiating $F_{Y_1}(y)=1-e^{-\frac{ny}{\theta}}$,
\[f_{Y_1}(y)=\frac{n}{\theta}e^{-\frac{ny}{\theta}},\qquad y>0.\]

\textbf{(b).} The maximum is at most $y$ exactly when all $n$ are:
\[F_{Y_n}(y)=\left[1-e^{-\frac{y}{\theta}}\right]^n,\qquad
f_{Y_n}(y)=\frac{n}{\theta}\left(1-e^{-\frac{y}{\theta}}\right)^{n-1}
e^{-\frac{y}{\theta}},\quad y>0.\]

\begin{note}
The minimum stays exponential and simply speeds up by a factor of $n$ --- a
consequence of the memoryless property, and the reason the first failure in a
system of $n$ components arrives $n$ times sooner than a single one. The maximum
is not exponential at all, and has no such tidy description.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{UNIF}(0,a)$. Find the p.d.f.
of the range $R=X_{(n)}-X_{(1)}$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
On $(0,a)$ we have $f(x)=\frac1a$ and $F(x)=\frac{x}{a}$. The joint density of the
minimum and maximum is
\begin{align*}
f\left(y_1,y_n\right)&=n(n-1)\left[F(y_n)-F(y_1)\right]^{n-2}f(y_1)f(y_n)\\
&=\frac{n(n-1)\left(y_n-y_1\right)^{n-2}}{a^n},\qquad 0<y_1<y_n<a.
\end{align*}
Put $R=Y_n-Y_1$ and $S=Y_1$; then $y_1=s$, $y_n=s+r$, the Jacobian is $1$, and
the region becomes $0<r<a$, $0<s<a-r$. So
\[f_R(r)=\int_0^{a-r}\frac{n(n-1)r^{n-2}}{a^n}\,ds
=\frac{n(n-1)r^{n-2}(a-r)}{a^n},\qquad 0<r<a.\]
It integrates to $1$, since
\[\frac{n(n-1)}{a^n}\left[\frac{a\,r^{n-1}}{n-1}-\frac{r^n}{n}\right]_0^a
=n(n-1)\left(\frac{1}{n-1}-\frac{1}{n}\right)=1.\]

\begin{note}
Writing $R=\frac{r}{a}$ shows the range is $a$ times a $\text{BETA}(n-1,2)$
variable, whose mean is $\frac{n-1}{n+1}$. So the sample range covers a fraction
$\frac{n-1}{n+1}$ of the interval on average --- $\frac13$ of it for $n=2$, but
over $90\%$ once $n=20$. It is a usable estimator of $a$, and the factor
$\frac{n+1}{n-1}$ is what corrects its bias.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from the $\text{BETA}(2,3)$ distribution,
with density $f(x)=12x(1-x)^2$ on $0<x<1$. Find the joint p.d.f. of $Y_1$ and
$Y_n$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
First the distribution function:
\[F(x)=\int_0^x12w(1-w)^2dw
=12\int_0^x\left(w-2w^2+w^3\right)dw
=12\left[\frac{x^2}{2}-\frac{2x^3}{3}+\frac{x^4}{4}\right],\]
\[F(x)=6x^2-8x^3+3x^4=x^2\left(3x^2-8x+6\right),\qquad 0<x<1,\]
and $F(1)=6-8+3=1$, as it must be.

The joint density of the extremes is the general formula with $i=1$, $j=n$:
\[f\left(y_1,y_n\right)=n(n-1)\left[F(y_n)-F(y_1)\right]^{n-2}f(y_1)f(y_n),\]
\begin{align*}
f\left(y_1,y_n\right)={}&
144\,n(n-1)\,y_1\left(1-y_1\right)^2y_n\left(1-y_n\right)^2\\
&\times\left[y_n^2\left(3y_n^2-8y_n+6\right)-y_1^2\left(3y_1^2-8y_1+6\right)\right]^{n-2}
\end{align*}
for $0<y_1<y_n<1$, the $144$ being $12\times12$ from the two densities.

\begin{note}
Nothing here depends on the distribution being beta. The formula for the extremes
is the same for every continuous population; only $F$ and $f$ change. Getting $F$
right is the whole job, which is why it is worth the check $F(1)=1$.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,X_2$ be a random sample of size $2$ from the distribution with density
$f(x)=2x$, $0<x<1$, and let $Y_1<Y_2$ be the order statistics. Show that
\[U=\frac{Y_1}{Y_2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad V=Y_2\]
are independent, and identify their distributions.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The joint density of the order statistics from a sample of size $2$ is
$2!\,f(y_1)f(y_2)$ on $y_1<y_2$:
\[f\left(y_1,y_2\right)=2\left(2y_1\right)\left(2y_2\right)=8y_1y_2,
\qquad 0<y_1<y_2<1.\]
With $u=\frac{y_1}{y_2}$ and $v=y_2$ the inverse is $y_1=uv$, $y_2=v$, so
\[|J|=\left|\begin{matrix}v & u\\0 & 1\end{matrix}\right|=v,\]
and the region $0<y_1<y_2<1$ becomes the rectangle $0<u<1$, $0<v<1$. Hence
\[f_{U,V}(u,v)=8(uv)(v)\cdot v=8uv^3
=\underbrace{\left(2u\right)}_{0<u<1}\cdot
\underbrace{\left(4v^3\right)}_{0<v<1}.\]
The joint density factorises over a rectangle, so $U$ and $V$ are independent,
with
\[f_U(u)=2u\quad(0<u<1),\qquad f_V(v)=4v^3\quad(0<v<1),\]
each integrating to $1$.

\begin{note}
The rectangle matters as much as the factorisation. Had the region stayed
triangular, no amount of algebra would give independence, because the range of one
variable would still depend on the other. Choosing $U$ as a \emph{ratio} is what
straightens the region out.
\end{note}
\end{solution}


\section{Practice problems: limit theorems}

Problems on Chebyshev's inequality, limiting distributions and the central limit
theorem, written for these notes. Normal probabilities are quoted to four decimal
places from the standard table.

\begin{problem}
The number of litres of fuel sold at a filling station in a day has mean $4200$
and standard deviation $300$. Nothing else is known about the distribution.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Give a lower bound for the probability that a day's sales fall between
$3600$ and $4800$ litres.
\item[(b).] Sales are now known to be approximately normal. Compare.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a).} The interval is $4200\pm600$, and
$600=k\sigma$ with $k=\frac{600}{300}=2$. Chebyshev's inequality gives
\[P\left(\left|X-4200\right|\geq600\right)\leq\frac{1}{2^2}=0.25,\]
\[\therefore\quad P\left(3600<X<4800\right)\geq1-0.25=0.75.\]

\textbf{(b).} If $X\sim N\left(4200,300^2\right)$ then
\[P\left(3600<X<4800\right)=P(-2<Z<2)=0.9772-0.0228=0.9544.\]

\begin{note}
$0.75$ against $0.9544$: the bound is correct but weak, and deliberately so. It
holds for \emph{every} distribution with that mean and variance, including some
very lopsided ones, so it cannot be tight for the normal in particular. Use
Chebyshev when the shape is unknown, and a table when it is not.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $Z_n\sim BIN(n,p)$ with $0<p<1$ fixed. Use moment generating functions to
find the limiting distribution of
\[Y_n=\frac{Z_n-np}{\sqrt{np(1-p)}}.\]
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Write $q=1-p$ and $\sigma_n=\sqrt{npq}$. Since
$M_{Z_n}(t)=\left(q+pe^t\right)^n$,
\[M_{Y_n}(t)=e^{-\frac{npt}{\sigma_n}}
\left(q+pe^{\frac{t}{\sigma_n}}\right)^n,\qquad\text{so}\qquad
\ln M_{Y_n}(t)=-\frac{npt}{\sigma_n}
+n\ln\left(q+pe^{\frac{t}{\sigma_n}}\right).\]
Expand the exponential, writing $s=\frac{t}{\sigma_n}\to0$:
\[q+pe^{s}=q+p\left(1+s+\frac{s^2}{2}+\cdots\right)
=1+ps+\frac{ps^2}{2}+\cdots,\]
since $q+p=1$. Now use $\ln(1+w)=w-\frac{w^2}{2}+\cdots$ with
$w=ps+\frac{ps^2}{2}+\cdots$:
\[\ln\left(q+pe^{s}\right)=ps+\frac{ps^2}{2}-\frac{p^2s^2}{2}+O\left(s^3\right)
=ps+\frac{pq\,s^2}{2}+O\left(s^3\right),\]
because $\frac{p}{2}-\frac{p^2}{2}=\frac{p(1-p)}{2}=\frac{pq}{2}$. Therefore
\[\ln M_{Y_n}(t)=-\frac{npt}{\sigma_n}+n\left[\frac{pt}{\sigma_n}
+\frac{pq\,t^2}{2\sigma_n^2}+O\!\left(\frac{1}{n^{\frac32}}\right)\right]
=\frac{npq\,t^2}{2npq}+O\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt n}\right)
\ \longrightarrow\ \frac{t^2}{2}.\]
\[\therefore\quad M_{Y_n}(t)\longrightarrow e^{\frac{t^2}{2}},
\qquad\text{so}\qquad
\frac{Z_n-np}{\sqrt{np(1-p)}}\ \overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\ N(0,1).\]

\begin{note}
This is the central limit theorem in the one case where it can be checked by hand
from the moment generating function alone: $Z_n$ is a sum of $n$ independent
Bernoulli variables, so the general theorem covers it. Doing it directly shows
where the $\frac{t^2}{2}$ comes from --- the linear term always cancels against
the centring, and the quadratic term always survives at exactly order one.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from $\text{POI}(1)$, and let
\[Y_n=\frac{\sqrt n\left(\bar X-\mu\right)}{\sigma}.\]
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Show that $Y_n=\sqrt n\left(\bar X-1\right)$ and that its moment
generating function is
\[\exp\left[-t\sqrt n+n\left(e^{t/\sqrt n}-1\right)\right].\]
\item[(b).] Deduce the limiting distribution of $Y_n$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a).} For $\text{POI}(1)$ both the mean and the variance are $1$, so
$\mu=1$, $\sigma=1$, and $Y_n=\sqrt n\left(\bar X-1\right)$ immediately.

A sum of independent Poisson variables is Poisson with the means added, so
$S=\sum_{i=1}^nX_i\sim\text{POI}(n)$ and $M_S(t)=e^{n\left(e^t-1\right)}$. Now
\[Y_n=\sqrt n\left(\frac{S}{n}-1\right)=\frac{S-n}{\sqrt n},\]
so
\[M_{Y_n}(t)=E\left[e^{\frac{t(S-n)}{\sqrt n}}\right]
=e^{-t\sqrt n}\,M_S\!\left(\frac{t}{\sqrt n}\right)
=\exp\left[-t\sqrt n+n\left(e^{\frac{t}{\sqrt n}}-1\right)\right].
\qquad\blacksquare\]

\textbf{(b).} This is exactly the moment generating function computed in the
worked example of section 1.4, where $Z_n\sim\text{POI}(n)$ and
$Y_n=\frac{Z_n-n}{\sqrt n}$ --- and indeed $S$ here \emph{is} such a $Z_n$.
Expanding as there,
\[\ln M_{Y_n}(t)=\frac{t^2}{2}+O\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt n}\right)
\ \longrightarrow\ \frac{t^2}{2},\]
\[\therefore\quad Y_n\ \overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\ N(0,1).\]

\begin{note}
Part (a) is the whole point of the question. Recognising that a sample mean from
$\text{POI}(1)$ is a rescaled $\text{POI}(n)$ turns a question about $n$ variables
into a question about one, and the limit is then something already done. Look for
this reduction before starting any expansion.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
A random sample of size $20$ is taken from the distribution with density
$f(x)=2x$, $0<x<1$. Use the central limit theorem to approximate
$P\left(0.60<\bar X<0.75\right)$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
First the population mean and variance:
\[\mu=\int_0^12x^2dx=\frac23,\qquad
E\left(X^2\right)=\int_0^12x^3dx=\frac12,\]
\[\sigma^2=\frac12-\left(\frac23\right)^2=\frac12-\frac49=\frac{1}{18}.\]
So $\bar X$ has mean $\frac23=0.6667$ and variance
$\frac{1}{18\times20}=\frac{1}{360}$, giving
\[\sigma_{\bar X}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{360}}=0.0527.\]
By the central limit theorem $\bar X$ is approximately normal, so
\[P\left(0.60<\bar X<0.75\right)
\approx P\!\left(\frac{0.60-0.6667}{0.0527}<Z<\frac{0.75-0.6667}{0.0527}\right)
=P(-1.26<Z<1.58),\]
\[P\left(0.60<\bar X<0.75\right)\approx0.9429-0.1038=0.8391.\]

\begin{note}
Simulation puts the true value at about $0.842$, so the approximation is good to
about three parts in a thousand at $n=20$. The population here is only mildly
skewed, which is why so small a sample suffices; the same $n$ against a heavily
skewed population would do far worse.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $Y\sim BIN(150,0.3)$. Use a normal approximation, with a continuity
correction, to find (a) $P(Y\geq50)$ and (b) $P(Y=45)$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Here $\mu=np=45$ and $\sigma^2=np(1-p)=150(0.3)(0.7)=31.5$, so
$\sigma=5.6125$.

\textbf{(a).} The event $Y\geq50$ becomes $Y>49.5$:
\[P(Y\geq50)\approx P\!\left(Z>\frac{49.5-45}{5.6125}\right)=P(Z>0.80)
=1-0.7881=0.2119.\]
The exact binomial value is $0.2101$.

\textbf{(b).}
\[P(Y=45)\approx P\left(44.5<Y<45.5\right)
=P\!\left(\frac{-0.5}{5.6125}<Z<\frac{0.5}{5.6125}\right)=P(-0.089<Z<0.089),\]
\[P(Y=45)\approx0.5355-0.4645=0.0710,\]
against an exact value of $0.0709$.

\begin{note}
$np=45$ and $n(1-p)=105$ are both comfortably above $5$, which is the usual
condition for this approximation to be trustworthy. It is a statement about the
smaller of the two expected counts, not about $n$: a sample of $10\,000$ with
$p=0.0002$ fails it, and no amount of extra data repairs that.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $\bar X$ be the mean of a random sample of size $50$ from a $\chi^2(20)$
distribution. Find an approximate value for $P\left(19<\bar X<21\right)$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
A $\chi^2(k)$ distribution has mean $k$ and variance $2k$, so here
$\mu=20$ and $\sigma^2=40$. Then
\[\text{Var}\left(\bar X\right)=\frac{40}{50}=0.8,\qquad
\sigma_{\bar X}=\sqrt{0.8}=0.8944.\]
By the central limit theorem,
\[P\left(19<\bar X<21\right)
\approx P\!\left(\frac{19-20}{0.8944}<Z<\frac{21-20}{0.8944}\right)
=P(-1.12<Z<1.12),\]
\[P\left(19<\bar X<21\right)\approx0.8686-0.1314=0.7372.\]

\begin{note}
No approximation was really needed: a sum of independent chi-squares is chi-square,
so $50\bar X\sim\chi^2(1000)$ exactly, and the exact answer is $0.7367$. The normal
approximation is doing well here precisely because $\chi^2(1000)$ is itself nearly
normal --- which is the central limit theorem again, since a $\chi^2(1000)$ variable
\emph{is} a sum of a thousand independent pieces.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $\bar X$ be the mean of a random sample of size $64$ from
$\text{GAM}(2,3)$. Find an approximate value for $P\left(5<\bar X<7\right)$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
For $\text{GAM}(\alpha,\beta)$ with $\alpha=2$ and $\beta=3$,
\[\mu=\alpha\beta=6,\qquad\sigma^2=\alpha\beta^2=18.\]
So $\text{Var}\left(\bar X\right)=\frac{18}{64}=0.2813$ and
$\sigma_{\bar X}=0.5303$. Then
\[P\left(5<\bar X<7\right)
\approx P\!\left(\frac{5-6}{0.5303}<Z<\frac{7-6}{0.5303}\right)
=P(-1.89<Z<1.89),\]
\[P\left(5<\bar X<7\right)\approx0.9706-0.0294=0.9412.\]

\begin{note}
Exactly, $\bar X\sim\text{GAM}\!\left(128,\frac{3}{64}\right)$ by the earlier
exercise on the sampling distribution of a gamma mean. That gives $0.94125$,
against $0.94124$ from the normal approximation --- agreement to five decimal
places. A gamma distribution with shape $128$ is for practical purposes normal
already, so there was nothing left for the approximation to get wrong.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Independent random samples of size $36$ are taken from $N(10,25)$ and $N(8,16)$,
with means $\bar X$ and $\bar Y$ respectively. Find $P\left(\bar X>\bar Y\right)$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
The two sample means are normal and independent:
\[\bar X\sim N\!\left(10,\frac{25}{36}\right),\qquad
\bar Y\sim N\!\left(8,\frac{16}{36}\right).\]
A difference of independent normal variables is normal, with the means subtracted
and the variances \emph{added}:
\[\bar X-\bar Y\sim N\!\left(2,\ \frac{25}{36}+\frac{16}{36}\right)
=N\!\left(2,\ \frac{41}{36}\right),\qquad
\text{sd}=\sqrt{\frac{41}{36}}=1.0672.\]
Therefore
\[P\left(\bar X>\bar Y\right)=P\left(\bar X-\bar Y>0\right)
=P\!\left(Z>\frac{0-2}{1.0672}\right)=P(Z>-1.87)=0.9693.\]

\begin{note}
No approximation is involved --- both populations are normal, so the difference of
the means is exactly normal at any sample size. The central limit theorem would
give the same answer for non-normal populations at this $n$, but here it is not
needed, and saying so is part of the answer.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\chapter{Estimation}

\section{Introduction}

In statistical inference the data $X_1, X_2, \dots, X_n$ are assumed to come
from a distribution $f(x; \theta)$ whose form is known but whose parameter
$\theta$ is not. The parameter lies in a \emph{parameter space} $\Omega$, and the
task of this chapter is to use the sample to make a best guess at $\theta$, or at
a function $\tau(\theta)$ of it, and to say how good the guess is.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:statistic}
A \emph{statistic} is a function $T = T(X_1, X_2, \dots , X_n)$ of the sample
that does not depend on any unknown parameter.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:statistic}
The sample mean $\bar{X} = \frac{1}{n}\sum X_i$ is a statistic. The quantity
$\frac{\bar{X} - \mu}{\sigma / \sqrt{n}}$ is not a statistic if $\mu$ or $\sigma$
is unknown, since it cannot be computed from the data alone.
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:estimator}
A statistic $T = T(X_1,\dots,X_n)$ used to estimate $\theta$ or $\tau(\theta)$ is
an \emph{estimator}; it is a random variable, often written $\hat\theta$. Its
value $t = T(x_1, \dots, x_n)$ for an observed sample is an \emph{estimate}.
\end{definition}

For example, if $X_1,\dots,X_n$ are independent with $E(X_i) = \mu$, then
$\bar{X}$ is an estimator of $\mu$ and the number $\bar{x}$ computed from a
particular sample is an estimate of $\mu$.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:samplemoments}
Let $X_1, X_2, \dots , X_n$ be a random sample. The \emph{$k^{\text{th}}$ sample
moment about $0$} is $M_k' = \frac{1}{n}\sum^n_{i =1}X^k_i$, and the
\emph{$k^{\text{th}}$ sample moment about the mean} is
$M_k = \frac{1}{n}\sum^n_{i = 1}(X_i - \bar{X})^k$.
\end{definition}

The corresponding population moments are $\mu'_k = E(X^k)$ and
$\mu_k = E\left[(X - \mu)^k\right]$. Note that $M'_1 = \bar{X}$ and $M_1 = 0$.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:samplemoments}
For a random sample, $E(M'_k) = \mu'_k$ if $\mu'_k$ exists, and
\[\text{Var}(M_k') = \frac{1}{n}\left(\mu'_{2k} - (\mu'_k)^2\right)\]
if $\mu'_{2k}$ exists.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
$M'_k$ is the mean of the $n$ independent variables $X_i^k$, each with mean
$\mu'_k$ and variance $E(X^{2k}) - [E(X^k)]^2 = \mu'_{2k} - (\mu'_k)^2$. The mean
of $n$ independent identically distributed variables has the same mean and
$\frac1n$ times the variance.
\end{proof}

With $k = 1$ this gives $E(\bar X) = \mu$ and
$\text{Var}(\bar X) = \frac1n\left[E(X^2) - (E(X))^2\right] = \frac{\sigma^2}{n}$.

Several methods produce estimators; the main ones are the method of moments, the
method of maximum likelihood, and, mainly in regression, the method of least
squares. The rest of the chapter develops these and the criteria for judging the
results.

\section{Method of Moments}

Let the population p.f. or p.d.f. $f(x;\theta_1, \dots , \theta_k)$ depend on $k$
parameters. The \emph{method of moments} equates the first $k$ sample moments to
the corresponding population moments, which are functions of the parameters,
\[M'_j = \mu'_j(\theta_1,\dots,\theta_k),\qquad j = 1,2,\dots,k ,\]
and solves for the parameters. The solutions are the \emph{method of moments
estimators} (MME). The method rests on the law of large numbers: each $M_j'$ is
close to $\mu_j'$ in large samples.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mmenormal}
Find the method of moments estimators of $\mu$ and $\sigma^2$ for a random
sample from $N(\mu,\sigma^2)$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
With $k = 2$, the equations are $M'_1 = \mu'_1$ and $M'_2 = \mu'_2$, that is
\[\bar{X} = \mu ,\qquad \frac{1}{n}\sum^n_{i = 1} X^2_i = \sigma^2 + \mu^2 .\]
So $\hat{\mu} = \bar{X}$ and
\[\hat\sigma^2 = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n X_i^2 - \bar{X}^2 = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n
(X_i - \bar{X})^2 .\]
Note the divisor $n$, not $n - 1$: the method of moments estimator of the
variance is slightly biased, as shown later in this chapter.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mmeexp}
Let $X_1, \dots , X_n$ be a random sample from the exponential distribution with
p.d.f. $f(x;\lambda) = \lambda e^{-\lambda x}$, $x>0$, $\lambda>0$. Find the
method of moments estimator of $\lambda$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
With one parameter, equate $M'_1 = \bar X$ to $E(X) = \frac{1}{\lambda}$:
\[\bar X = \frac{1}{\lambda}\qquad\text{so}\qquad \hat{\lambda}_{\text{MME}} =
\frac{1}{\bar X} = \frac{n}{\sum_{i=1}^n X_i} .\]
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mmebeta}
Find the method of moments estimators of $\alpha$ and $\beta$ for a random sample
from the beta distribution with p.d.f.
$f(x;\alpha,\beta) = \frac{x^{\alpha-1}(1-x)^{\beta-1}}{B(\alpha,\beta)}$,
$0<x<1$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The first two moments of the beta distribution are
\[\mu'_1 = \frac{\alpha}{\alpha + \beta},\qquad \mu'_2 = \frac{\alpha(\alpha +
1)}{(\alpha + \beta)(\alpha + \beta + 1)} ,\]
since $E(X^k) = \frac{B(\alpha + k,\beta)}{B(\alpha,\beta)}$. Write
$\bar X = M_1'$ and $M_2' = \frac1n\sum X_i^2$. The equations are
\begin{equation}
\bar X = \frac{\alpha}{\alpha + \beta} ,
\label{eq:mmebeta1}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
M_2' = \frac{\alpha(\alpha + 1)}{(\alpha + \beta)(\alpha + \beta + 1)} .
\label{eq:mmebeta2}
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:mmebeta1}, $\alpha + \beta = \frac{\alpha}{\bar X}$, and then
\eqref{eq:mmebeta2} becomes
\[M_2' = \bar X\,\frac{\alpha + 1}{\frac{\alpha}{\bar X} + 1} = \frac{\bar
X^2(\alpha + 1)}{\alpha + \bar X} .\]
Solving for $\alpha$, $M_2'(\alpha + \bar X) = \bar X^2(\alpha + 1)$, so
\[\hat\alpha = \frac{\bar X\left(\bar X - M_2'\right)}{M_2' - \bar X^2},\qquad
\hat\beta = \hat\alpha\,\frac{1 - \bar X}{\bar X} = \frac{(1 - \bar X)\left(\bar
X - M_2'\right)}{M_2' - \bar X^2} .\]
The denominator $M_2' - \bar X^2$ is the sample variance with divisor $n$.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mmeuniform}
Find the method of moments estimator of $\theta$ for a random sample from the
uniform distribution on $(0,\theta)$, and show that it can be impossible.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Equating $\bar X$ to $E(X) = \frac{\theta}{2}$ gives $\hat\theta = 2\bar X$. For
the sample $0.1, 0.2, 0.9$, $\hat\theta = 0.8$, which is smaller than an observed
value: a uniform distribution on $(0,0.8)$ could not have produced $0.9$. The
method of moments uses only the moments and ignores what the data say about the
support; the maximum likelihood estimator of Example~\ref{ex:mleuniformzero}
does not have this defect.
\end{solution}

\section{Method of Least Squares}

In the simple linear regression model
\[Y_i = \alpha + \beta x_i + \epsilon_i ,\qquad i = 1,2,\dots,n,\]
the \emph{least squares estimators} of $\alpha$ and $\beta$ minimise the sum of
squared deviations
\[L(\alpha,\beta) = \sum^n_{i=1}\left(Y_i - \alpha - \beta x_i\right)^2 .\]

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:lse}
The least squares estimators in simple linear regression are
\[\hat{\beta} = \frac{\sum^n_{i = 1}(x_i - \bar{x})(Y_i - \bar{Y})}{\sum^n_{i =
1}(x_i - \bar{x})^2} = \frac{\sum^n_{i = 1} x_iY_i - n\bar{x}\bar{Y}}{\sum^n_{i
= 1} x^2_i - n\bar{x}^2},\qquad \hat\alpha = \bar Y - \hat\beta\bar x .\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Setting the partial derivatives to zero,
\[\frac{\partial L}{\partial \alpha} = -2\sum^n_{i=1}(Y_i - \alpha - \beta x_i) =
0,\qquad \frac{\partial L}{\partial \beta} = -2\sum^n_{i=1}x_i(Y_i - \alpha -
\beta x_i) = 0 .\]
The first gives $n\bar{Y} - n\alpha - n\beta \bar{x} = 0$, so
$\alpha = \bar{Y} - \beta\bar{x}$. Substituting into the second,
\[\sum^n_{i=1}x_iY_i - \left(\bar{Y} - \beta \bar{x}\right)n\bar{x} - \beta
\sum^n_{i = 1} x^2_i = 0,\qquad\text{so}\qquad \beta\left(\sum x_i^2 - n\bar
x^2\right) = \sum x_iY_i - n\bar x\bar Y ,\]
which gives $\hat\beta$; the two forms agree because $\sum(x_i - \bar x)(Y_i -
\bar Y) = \sum x_iY_i - n\bar x\bar Y$ and $\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2 = \sum x_i^2 -
n\bar x^2$. Since $L$ is a convex quadratic, the stationary point is the
minimum.
\end{proof}

The same principle estimates other linear models.
\begin{enumerate}
\item In the \emph{cell means model}, each of $k$ treatment groups has its own
mean:
\[Y_{ij} = \mu_{j} + \varepsilon_{ij},\qquad i = 1,\dots ,n_j,\quad j =
1,\dots,k .\]
Minimising $L = \sum_{j=1}^k\sum_{i=1}^{n_j}(Y_{ij} - \mu_{j})^2$, the equation
$\frac{\partial L}{\partial \mu_j} = -2\sum_{i=1}^{n_j}(Y_{ij} - \mu_j) = 0$
gives $\hat{\mu}_j = \bar{Y}_{\cdot j} = \frac{1}{n_j}\sum_{i=1}^{n_j}Y_{ij}$, the
group mean.

\item In the \emph{one-way analysis of variance} model, the response is an
overall mean plus a treatment effect,
\[Y_{ij} = \mu + \tau_j + \varepsilon_{ij},\qquad \sum^k_{j=1}n_j\tau_j = 0 .\]
The fitted values $\hat\mu + \hat\tau_j$ must equal the group means
$\bar Y_{\cdot j}$, as in the cell means model, and the constraint then gives
$\hat\mu = \frac{1}{N}\sum_j n_j\bar Y_{\cdot j} = \bar Y_{\cdot\cdot}$, the
grand mean of all $N = \sum n_j$ observations, and
$\hat\tau_j = \bar{Y}_{\cdot j} - \bar{Y}_{\cdot\cdot}$.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:lsenumeric}
Fit the least squares line to the points $(1,2)$, $(2,3)$, $(3,5)$, $(4,4)$,
$(5,6)$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Here $\bar x = 3$, $\bar y = 4$, $\sum(x_i - \bar x)(y_i - \bar y) = (-2)(-2) +
(-1)(-1) + 0 + (1)(0) + (2)(2) = 9$ and $\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2 = 10$. So
$\hat\beta = 0.9$ and $\hat\alpha = 4 - 0.9\cdot 3 = 1.3$: the fitted line is
$\hat y = 1.3 + 0.9x$.
\end{solution}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\section{Method of Maximum Likelihood}

The idea of maximum likelihood is to estimate $\theta$ by the value in the
parameter space that makes the observed data most probable.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:likelihood}
Let $X_1, \dots , X_n$ be a random sample from $f(x;\theta)$, $\theta \in \Omega$,
with observed values $\mathbf x = (x_1, \dots, x_n)$. The \emph{likelihood
function} of $\theta$ is
\[L(\theta) = L(\theta;\mathbf x) = \prod^n_{i = 1}f(x_i;\theta) ,\qquad \theta
\in \Omega ,\]
and the \emph{log likelihood} is $l(\theta) = \ln L(\theta)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:mle}
The value $\hat\theta$ of $\theta$ that maximises $L(\theta)$, equivalently
$l(\theta)$, is the \emph{maximum likelihood (M.L.) estimate}. As a function of the
data, $\hat{\theta} = \hat{\theta}(x_1, \dots , x_n)$; the corresponding random
variable $\hat{\theta}(X_1, \dots, X_n)$ is the \emph{M.L. estimator}.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:score}
For a scalar $\theta$, the \emph{score function} and the \emph{information
function} are
\[S(\theta) = \frac{\partial l(\theta)}{\partial \theta} ,\qquad I(\theta) =
-\frac{\partial^2 l(\theta)}{\partial \theta^2} .\]
For a vector $\theta = (\theta_1, \dots , \theta_k)^{\mathsf T}$, the score
vector has components $\frac{\partial l}{\partial \theta_j}$ and the
\emph{information matrix} is the $k\times k$ symmetric matrix with entries
\[I(\theta)_{ij} = -\frac{\partial^2 l(\theta)}{\partial \theta_i\partial
\theta_j} .\]
\end{definition}

These functions depend on the data as well as on $\theta$; we write
$L(\theta;\mathbf x)$, $S(\theta;\mathbf x)$ and $I(\theta;\mathbf x)$ when the
dependence matters, and $L(\theta;\mathbf X)$ and so on when studying their
sampling properties as functions of the random sample.

\subsubsection{Finding M.L. Estimates}

If $l(\theta)$ is twice differentiable on an open interval, $\hat\theta$ is usually
found by solving the \emph{likelihood equation} $S(\theta) = 0$, and for a scalar
$\theta$ the solution is confirmed to be a maximum by checking
$I(\hat{\theta}) > 0$. Two warnings: if the support of the distribution depends on
$\theta$, as for the uniform distribution on $(0,\theta)$, the maximum is usually
at an end point and is not found from $S(\theta) = 0$; and often $S(\theta) = 0$
has no explicit solution and must be solved numerically.

\subsubsection{Newton's Method}
\label{sec:newton}

Expanding the score about a current estimate $\theta^{(i)}$,
$S(\theta)\approx S\left(\theta^{(i)}\right) - I\left(\theta^{(i)}\right)\left(\theta -
\theta^{(i)}\right)$, and setting this to zero gives the \emph{Newton--Raphson}
iteration
\[\theta^{(i + 1)} = \theta^{(i)} + \left[I\left(\theta^{(i)}\right)\right]^{-1}
S\left(\theta^{(i)}\right) ,\qquad i = 0, 1, 2, \dots ,\]
started from an initial estimate $\theta^{(0)}$, for instance the method of
moments estimate, and continued until successive values agree to the required
accuracy.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mlepower}
Let $X_1, \dots , X_n$ be a random sample from
$f(x;\theta) = \theta x^{\theta - 1}$, $0< x < 1$, $\theta > 0$. Find the score
function, the information function and the M.L. estimator of $\theta$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The likelihood is
$L(\theta) = \prod \theta x_i^{\theta - 1} = \theta^n\left(\prod x_i\right)^{\theta -
1}$, so
\[l(\theta) = n\ln \theta + (\theta - 1) \sum^n_{i = 1} \ln x_i ,\qquad S(\theta)
= \frac{n}{\theta} + \sum^n_{i = 1} \ln x_i ,\qquad I(\theta) =
\frac{n}{\theta^2} .\]
Setting $S(\theta) = 0$ gives
\[\hat{\theta} = -\frac{n}{\sum^n_{i = 1}\ln X_i} ,\]
which is positive because each $\ln X_i<0$, and $I(\hat\theta)>0$, so it is the
maximum.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mleuniformzero}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from the uniform distribution on
$(0,\theta)$. Find the M.L. estimator of $\theta$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Each factor of the likelihood is $\frac1\theta$ if $0<x_i<\theta$ and $0$
otherwise, so $L(\theta) = \theta^{-n}$ when $\theta>\max x_i = y_n$, and
$L(\theta) = 0$ when $\theta<y_n$. On $\theta>y_n$ the likelihood decreases, so it
is largest at the smallest admissible value: $\hat\theta = Y_n$, the largest
observation. The likelihood equation $S(\theta) = -\frac{n}{\theta} = 0$ has no
solution; the maximum is at the edge of the region where $L>0$.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mleuniformshift}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from the uniform distribution on
$\left(\theta - \frac{1}{2},\theta + \frac{1}{2}\right)$. Find an M.L. estimator
of $\theta$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
With $I_A$ the indicator of the set $A$, the likelihood is
\[L(\theta) = \prod^n_{i=1}I_{\left(\theta - \frac12,\theta +
\frac12\right)}(x_i) = I_{\left(\theta-\frac{1}{2},\infty\right)}(y_1)\cdot
I_{\left(-\infty,\theta + \frac{1}{2}\right)}(y_n),\]
where $y_1$ and $y_n$ are the smallest and largest observations. So $L(\theta) =
1$ exactly when $y_1>\theta - \frac12$ and $y_n<\theta + \frac12$, that is
\[y_n - \frac{1}{2} < \theta < y_1 + \frac{1}{2},\]
and $L(\theta) = 0$ otherwise. Every $\theta$ in this interval maximises the
likelihood: the M.L. estimator is not unique. The usual choice is the midpoint,
$\hat{\theta} = \frac{Y_1 + Y_n}{2}$, the \emph{midrange}.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mleweibull}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from the Weibull p.d.f.
$f(x;\beta) = \beta x^{\beta - 1} e^{-x^{\beta}}$, $x > 0$, $\beta > 0$. Set up the
Newton--Raphson iteration for the M.L. estimator of $\beta$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The log likelihood is
\[l(\beta) = n\ln \beta + (\beta - 1)\sum^n_{i = 1}\ln x_i - \sum^n_{i =
1}x_i^{\beta} ,\]
so
\[S(\beta) = \frac{n}{\beta}+ \sum^n_{i = 1}\ln x_i - \sum^n_{i = 1} x_i^{\beta}
\ln x_i ,\qquad I(\beta) = \frac{n}{\beta^2} + \sum^n_{i = 1} x_i^{\beta}(\ln
x_i)^2 .\]
The equation $S(\beta) = 0$ has no explicit solution, but $I(\beta)>0$ for every
$\beta$, so $l$ is concave and has a single maximum, which the iteration
$\beta^{(i + 1)} = \beta^{(i)} + S\left(\beta^{(i)}\right)/I\left(\beta^{(i)}\right)$
finds from any reasonable starting value.
\end{solution}

\begin{thm}[Invariance property]
\label{thm:invariance}
If $\hat{\theta}$ is the M.L. estimator of $\theta$ and $\tau = h(\theta)$ is a
one-to-one function, then $\hat{\tau} = h(\hat{\theta})$ is the M.L. estimator of
$\tau$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
In terms of $\tau$ the likelihood is $L^*(\tau) = L\left(h^{-1}(\tau)\right)$, which
is maximised when $h^{-1}(\tau) = \hat\theta$, that is $\tau = h(\hat\theta)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mlepoissoninvariance}
For a random sample from the Poisson distribution with mean $\theta$, find the
M.L. estimator of $e^{-1/\theta}$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The log likelihood is
\[l(\theta) = \left(\sum^n_{i = 1} x_i\right)\ln \theta - n\theta - \sum^n_{i =
1}\ln(x_i!) ,\qquad S(\theta) = \frac{1}{\theta}\sum^n_{i = 1} x_i - n ,\]
so $\hat{\theta} = \bar{X}$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:invariance}, the M.L. estimator
of $e^{-1/\theta}$ is $e^{-1/\bar X}$. Similarly, the M.L. estimator of
$P(X = 0) = e^{-\theta}$ is $e^{-\bar X}$.
\end{solution}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:fisher}
For a scalar $\theta$, the \emph{Fisher information} is the expected information,
\[J(\theta) = E\left[I(\theta;\mathbf X)\right] = E\left[-\frac{\partial^2
l(\theta;\mathbf X)}{\partial \theta^2}\right].\]
For a vector $\theta$, the \emph{Fisher information matrix} $J(\theta)$ has
entries $E\left[-\frac{\partial^2 l(\theta;\mathbf X)}{\partial \theta_i\partial
\theta_j}\right]$.
\end{definition}

For a random sample, the log likelihood is a sum of $n$ independent terms
$\ln f(X_i;\theta)$, so
\[J(\theta) = n\,E\left[-\frac{\partial^2 \ln f(X;\theta)}{\partial
\theta^2}\right],\]
$n$ times the information in a single observation.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mleexpscale}
Let $X_1, \dots , X_n$ be a random sample from
$f(x;\theta) = \frac{1}{\theta}e^{-x/\theta}$, $x > 0$. Find the score function,
the information function, the Fisher information and the M.L. estimator of
$\theta$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The likelihood is $L(\theta) = \theta^{-n}e^{-\sum x_i/\theta}$, so
\[l(\theta) = -n\ln \theta - \frac{1}{\theta}\sum^n_{i = 1} x_i ,\qquad
S(\theta) = -\frac{n}{\theta} + \frac{1}{\theta^2}\sum^n_{i = 1} x_i ,\qquad
I(\theta) = -\frac{n}{\theta^2} + \frac{2}{\theta^3}\sum^n_{i = 1} x_i .\]
Since $E(X_i) = \theta$,
\[J(\theta) = -\frac{n}{\theta^2} + \frac{2}{\theta^3}\cdot n\theta =
\frac{n}{\theta^2} .\]
Setting $S(\theta) = 0$ gives $\hat\theta = \frac1n\sum X_i = \bar X$, and
$I(\hat\theta) = \frac{n}{\bar x^2}>0$.
\end{solution}

\section{Properties of Estimators}

Several properties of estimators are desirable: unbiasedness, small variance or
mean squared error, sufficiency, efficiency and consistency.

\subsubsection{Sufficiency}

Does a statistic contain all the information in the sample about the parameter?
The concept of sufficiency makes the question precise.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:sufficient}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $f(x;\theta)$. A statistic $T$ is
\emph{sufficient} for $\theta$ if the conditional distribution of
$X_1, \dots , X_n$ given $T = t$ does not depend on $\theta$.
\end{definition}

Once $T$ is known, the remaining detail of the sample has a distribution that is
the same whatever $\theta$ is, and so carries no further information about it.
For discrete variables, when $T(\mathbf x) = t$ the conditional p.f. is
\[P(\mathbf X = \mathbf x\mid T = t) = \frac{\prod_{i=1}^nf(x_i;\theta)}{f_T(t;
\theta)} ,\]
and sufficiency means this ratio is free of $\theta$.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:sufficientbernoulli}
Let $X_1, \dots , X_n$ be a random sample from the Bernoulli distribution with
$P(X = 1) = \theta$. Verify that $T = \sum^n_{i = 1}X_i$ is sufficient for
$\theta$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The joint p.f. is $\prod \theta^{x_i}(1 - \theta)^{1 - x_i} = \theta^t(1 -
\theta)^{n - t}$, where $t = \sum x_i$, and $T\sim\text{BIN}(n,\theta)$. So, for
any $\mathbf x$ with $\sum x_i = t$,
\[P(\mathbf X = \mathbf x\mid T = t) = \frac{\theta^t\, (1 -
\theta)^{n - t}}{\binom{n}{t}\theta^t\, (1 - \theta)^{n - t}} =
\frac{1}{\binom{n}{t}} ,\]
which does not depend on $\theta$. Given the number of successes, every
arrangement of them is equally likely, whatever the success probability.
\end{solution}

Checking the definition directly requires the distribution of $T$. The following
theorem avoids that.

\begin{thm}[Factorisation theorem]
\label{thm:factorisation}
Let $X_1, \dots , X_n$ be a random sample from $f(x;\theta)$. Statistics
$T_1,\dots,T_k$ are \emph{jointly sufficient} for $\theta$ if and only if the
likelihood factors as
\[L(\theta) = \prod_{i=1}^nf(x_i;\theta) = g(t_1, \dots , t_k;\theta)\,h(x_1, \dots
, x_n)\]
for all $\mathbf x$ and $\theta$, where $g$ depends on the data only through
$t_j = T_j(\mathbf x)$, and $h\geq 0$ does not involve $\theta$. With $k = 1$ this
is the condition for a single sufficient statistic.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}[Proof in the discrete case, $k = 1$]
If $T$ is sufficient, then $P(\mathbf X = \mathbf x) = P(T = t)\,P(\mathbf X =
\mathbf x\mid T = t)$, the first factor depending on $\theta$ and $t$ only and the
second on $\mathbf x$ only; this is the factorisation. Conversely, if the
factorisation holds, then
$P(T = t) = \sum_{\mathbf y : T(\mathbf y) = t}g(t;\theta)h(\mathbf y) =
g(t;\theta)\sum_{\mathbf y : T(\mathbf y) = t}h(\mathbf y)$, so
\[P(\mathbf X = \mathbf x\mid T = t) = \frac{g(t;\theta)h(\mathbf
x)}{g(t;\theta)\sum_{T(\mathbf y) = t}h(\mathbf y)} = \frac{h(\mathbf
x)}{\sum_{T(\mathbf y) = t}h(\mathbf y)} ,\]
which is free of $\theta$.
\end{proof}

\begin{note}
\begin{enumerate}
\item The M.L. estimator is a function of any sufficient statistic, since the
likelihood depends on $\theta$ only through $g(t;\theta)$.
\item Sufficient statistics are not unique: any one-to-one function of a
sufficient statistic is sufficient.
\item The best unbiased estimators, found later in this chapter, are functions
of sufficient statistics.
\end{enumerate}
\end{note}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:sufficientpower}
Let $X_1, \dots , X_n$ be a random sample from $f(x;\theta) = \theta\,
x^{\theta - 1}$, $0 < x < 1$, $\theta > 0$. Find a sufficient statistic.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The likelihood is
\[L(\theta) = \underbrace{\theta^n \left(\prod^n_{i = 1}x_i\right)^{\theta -
1}}_{g(t;\theta)}\cdot\underbrace{1}_{h(\mathbf x)} ,\]
so $T = \prod^n_{i = 1} X_i$ is sufficient, and so is the one-to-one function
$\sum_{i=1}^n\ln X_i$, of which the M.L. estimator of Example~\ref{ex:mlepower}
is a function.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:sufficientuniformshift}
For a random sample from the uniform distribution on
$\left(\theta - \frac{1}{2}, \theta + \frac{1}{2}\right)$, find a sufficient
statistic for $\theta$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
As in Example~\ref{ex:mleuniformshift}, the likelihood is
\[L(\theta) = I_{\left(\theta - \frac12, \infty\right)}(y_1) \cdot I_{\left(-\infty,
\theta + \frac12\right)}(y_n),\]
a function of $\theta$ and the pair $(y_1,y_n)$ only, with $h = 1$. So
$(Y_1, Y_n)$ is jointly sufficient for $\theta$: a single parameter can need two
statistics.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:normalfull}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\mu, \sigma^2)$, with
$\theta = (\mu, \sigma^2)^{\mathsf T}$. Show that
$T = \left(\sum X_i, \sum X_i^2\right)$ is sufficient for $\theta$, and find the
score vector, the information matrix, the Fisher information matrix and the M.L.
estimator of $\theta$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The likelihood is
\begin{align*}
L(\mu, \sigma^2) &= \left(2\pi \sigma^2\right)^{-n/2} \exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}
\sum^n_{i = 1} (x_i - \mu)^2\right\}\\
&= \left(2\pi\sigma^2\right)^{-n/2}
\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\left(\sum x^2_i - 2\mu\sum x_i +
n\mu^2\right)\right\},
\end{align*}
which depends on the data only through $\sum x_i$ and $\sum x_i^2$; with $h = 1$,
Theorem~\ref{thm:factorisation} shows $T$ is sufficient. The log likelihood is
\[l(\mu, \sigma^2) = -\frac{n}{2}\ln (2\pi) - \frac{n}{2}\ln \sigma^2 -
\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum^n_{i = 1}(x_i - \mu)^2 ,\]
so the score vector has components
\[\frac{\partial l}{\partial \mu} = \frac{1}{\sigma^2}\sum^n_{i = 1}(x_i - \mu)
,\qquad \frac{\partial l}{\partial \sigma^2} = -\frac{n}{2\sigma^2} +
\frac{1}{2\sigma^4}\sum^n_{i = 1}(x_i - \mu)^2 .\]
Differentiating again, the information matrix is
\[I(\mu,\sigma^2) = \begin{pmatrix}
\frac{n}{\sigma^2} & \frac{1}{\sigma^4}\sum(x_i - \mu)\\[1ex]
\frac{1}{\sigma^4}\sum(x_i - \mu) & -\frac{n}{2\sigma^4} +
\frac{1}{\sigma^6}\sum(x_i - \mu)^2
\end{pmatrix}.\]
Taking expectations, with $E(X_i - \mu) = 0$ and $E(X_i - \mu)^2 = \sigma^2$,
\[J(\mu,\sigma^2) = \begin{pmatrix}
\frac{n}{\sigma^2} & 0\\[1ex]
0 & -\frac{n}{2\sigma^4} + \frac{n\sigma^2}{\sigma^6}
\end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}
\frac{n}{\sigma^2} & 0\\[1ex]
0 & \frac{n}{2\sigma^4}
\end{pmatrix}.\]
Setting the score to zero, the first equation gives $\hat{\mu} = \bar{x}$, and
then the second gives
$\hat{\sigma}^2 = \frac{1}{n}\sum (x_i - \bar{x})^2$. So the M.L. estimator is
$\hat{\theta} = \left(\bar{X},\ \frac{1}{n}\sum(X_i - \bar{X})^2\right)^{\mathsf T}$,
the same as the method of moments estimator here.
\end{solution}

\subsubsection{Unbiasedness}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:unbiased}
An estimator $T$ is an \emph{unbiased} estimator of $\tau(\theta)$ if
$E(T) = \tau(\theta)$ for all $\theta\in \Omega$.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:unbiasednormal}
For a random sample from a distribution with mean $\mu$ and variance $\sigma^2$
(for instance $N(\mu, \sigma^2)$), show that $\bar{X}$ is unbiased for $\mu$ and
$S^2 = \frac{1}{n - 1}\sum(X_i - \bar X)^2$ is unbiased for $\sigma^2$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
First, $E(\bar{X}) = \frac{1}{n}\sum E(X_i) = \mu$. For $S^2$, write
$X_i - \bar X = (X_i - \mu) - (\bar X - \mu)$; since
$\sum(X_i - \mu) = n(\bar X - \mu)$,
\[\sum^n_{i = 1}(X_i - \bar{X})^2 = \sum^n_{i = 1}(X_i - \mu)^2 - n(\bar{X} -
\mu)^2 .\]
Taking expectations,
\[E\left[\sum^n_{i = 1}(X_i - \bar{X})^2\right] = n\sigma^2 - n\cdot
\frac{\sigma^2}{n} = (n - 1)\sigma^2 ,\]
so $E(S^2) = \sigma^2$. The divisor $n - 1$ is exactly what removes the bias.
\end{solution}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:mse}
If $T$ is an estimator of $\tau(\theta)$, its \emph{bias} is
$\text{bias}(T) = E(T) - \tau(\theta)$, and its \emph{mean squared error} is
$\text{MSE}(T) = E\left[(T - \tau(\theta))^2\right]$.
\end{definition}

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:mse}
If $T$ is an estimator of $\tau(\theta)$, then
\[\text{MSE}(T) = \text{Var}(T) + \left[\text{bias}(T)\right]^2 .\]
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Write $T - \tau(\theta) = (T - E(T)) + (E(T) - \tau(\theta))$ and
square. The cross term has expectation
$2(E(T) - \tau(\theta))E(T - E(T)) = 0$, leaving
$E(T - E(T))^2 + (E(T) - \tau(\theta))^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mseuniform}
Let $X_1, \dots , X_n$ be a random sample from the uniform distribution on
$(0,\theta)$, and let $T_1 = 2\bar{X}$ and $T_2 = X_{(n)}$ be estimators of
$\theta$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Is $T_1$ unbiased for $\theta$?
\item[(b)] Is $T_2$ unbiased for $\theta$?
\item[(c)] Find the mean squared errors of $T_1$ and $T_2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Since $E(X) = \int_0^\theta\frac{x}{\theta}\,dx = \frac{\theta}{2}$,
$E(T_1) = 2E(\bar X) = \theta$: unbiased.

\item[(b)] By Example~\ref{ex:uniformmax}, $T_2$ has p.d.f.
$\frac{ny^{n-1}}{\theta^n}$ on $(0,\theta)$ and $E(T_2) = \frac{n\theta}{n + 1}$:
biased, with $\text{bias}(T_2) = -\frac{\theta}{n + 1}$.

\item[(c)] Since $E(X^2) = \frac{\theta^2}{3}$, $\text{Var}(X) =
\frac{\theta^2}{12}$, and
\[\text{MSE}(T_1) = \text{Var}(2\bar X) = \frac{4}{n}\cdot\frac{\theta^2}{12} =
\frac{\theta^2}{3n} .\]
For $T_2$, $E(T_2^2) = \int_0^\theta y^2\frac{ny^{n-1}}{\theta^n}\,dy =
\frac{n\theta^2}{n + 2}$, so
\[\text{Var}(T_2) = \frac{n\theta^2}{n + 2} - \frac{n^2\theta^2}{(n+1)^2} =
\frac{n\theta^2}{(n + 1)^2(n + 2)} ,\]
and by Theorem~\ref{thm:mse}
\[\text{MSE}(T_2) = \frac{n\theta^2}{(n + 1)^2(n + 2)} + \frac{\theta^2}{(n +
1)^2} = \frac{\theta^2}{(n + 1)^2}\cdot\frac{2n + 2}{n + 2} = \frac{2\theta^2}{(n +
1)(n + 2)} .\]
\end{enumerate}
The two are equal for $n = 1$ and $n = 2$, and for $n\geq 3$ the biased estimator
$T_2$ has the smaller mean squared error; its MSE falls like $\frac{1}{n^2}$
against $\frac1n$ for $T_1$.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9]
\begin{axis}[
    axis lines = left,
    xlabel = {$n$ (Sample Size)},
    ylabel = {MSE},
    domain = 1:10,
    samples = 100,
    ymin = 0, ymax = 0.4,
    legend pos = north east,
    grid = major,
    grid style = {dashed, gray!30},
    title = {MSE Comparison: $T_1$ vs $T_2$ ($\theta=1$)}
]
    % MSE(T1) = 1/(3n)
    \addplot [
        thick,
        color=red,
    ] {1/(3*x)};
    \addlegendentry{$MSE(T_1) = \frac{\theta^2}{3n}$}

    % MSE(T2) = 2/((n+1)(n+2))
    \addplot [
        thick,
        color=blue,
    ] {2/((x+1)*(x+2))};
    \addlegendentry{$MSE(T_2) = \frac{2\theta^2}{(n+1)(n+2)}$}
    
    % Highlight the crossover at n=2
    \draw [dashed, black] (axis cs:2,0) -- (axis cs:2,0.166);
    \node [anchor=south] at (axis cs:2,0.2) {\small $n=2$ (Crossover)};

\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The $MSE(T_2)$ decays at a rate of $1/n^2$, making it more efficient than $T_1$ for all $n > 2$.}
\end{figure}
\end{solution}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:efficiency}
The \emph{relative efficiency} of an unbiased estimator $T$ of $\tau(\theta)$ to
another unbiased estimator $T^*$ is
\[\text{re}(T,T^{*}) = \frac{\text{Var}(T^*)}{\text{Var}(T)} .\]
An unbiased estimator $T^*$ is \emph{efficient} if $\text{re}(T,T^*) \leq 1$ for
all unbiased estimators $T$ of $\tau(\theta)$ and all $\theta \in \Omega$, and then
the \emph{efficiency} of any unbiased $T$ is $e(T) = \text{re}(T,T^*)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:umvue}
An estimator $T^*$ of $\tau(\theta)$ is a \emph{uniformly minimum variance
unbiased estimator} (UMVUE) if it is unbiased for $\tau(\theta)$ and
$\text{Var}(T^*) \leq \text{Var}(T)$ for every other unbiased estimator $T$ of
$\tau(\theta)$, for all $\theta \in \Omega$.
\end{definition}

\begin{thm}[Cram\'er--Rao inequality]
\label{thm:cramerrao}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $f(x;\theta)$, $\theta \in
\Omega$, and let $T$ be an unbiased estimator of $\tau(\theta)$. Under regularity
conditions (the support does not depend on $\theta$, and differentiation with
respect to $\theta$ may be taken inside the integrals),
\[\text{Var}(T)\geq \frac{\left[\tau'(\theta)\right]^2}{J(\theta)} ,\]
where $J(\theta)$ is the Fisher information.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Let $S = S(\theta;\mathbf X) = \frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\ln L(\theta;\mathbf
X)$. Differentiating $\int L\,d\mathbf x = 1$ gives $E(S) = \int\frac{\partial
L}{\partial\theta}\,d\mathbf x = 0$, and differentiating once more gives
$\text{Var}(S) = E(S^2) = J(\theta)$. Differentiating $\int T L\,d\mathbf x =
\tau(\theta)$ gives $E(TS) = \tau'(\theta)$, so
$\text{Cov}(T,S) = \tau'(\theta)$. By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality,
$\left[\text{Cov}(T,S)\right]^2\leq\text{Var}(T)\text{Var}(S)$, that is
$[\tau'(\theta)]^2\leq\text{Var}(T)J(\theta)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{note}
\begin{enumerate}
\item The number $\frac{[\tau'(\theta)]^2}{J(\theta)}$ is the \emph{Cram\'er--Rao
lower bound} (CRLB).
\item An unbiased estimator whose variance equals the CRLB is a UMVUE, and is
called efficient.
\item The \emph{efficiency} of an unbiased estimator is
$e(T) = \frac{\text{CRLB}}{\text{Var}(T)}$.
\item The regularity conditions matter: for the uniform distribution on
$(0,\theta)$ the support depends on $\theta$, and the unbiased estimator
$\frac{n + 1}{n}Y_n$ has variance $\frac{\theta^2}{n(n + 2)}$, below what the
formula would give.
\end{enumerate}
\end{note}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:crlbexp}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from
$f(x;\theta) = \frac{1}{\theta}e^{-x/\theta}$, $x > 0$, and let $T_1 = \bar{X}$
and $T_2 = nX_{(1)}$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Show that $T_1$ and $T_2$ are unbiased estimators of $\theta$.
\item[(b)] Find the CRLB for unbiased estimators of $\theta$.
\item[(c)] Is either estimator a UMVUE of $\theta$?
\item[(d)] Find the efficiencies of $T_1$ and $T_2$.
\item[(e)] Find the CRLB for unbiased estimators of $\frac{1}{\theta}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $E(T_1) = E(X) = \theta$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:extremes}, with
$F(y) = 1 - e^{-y/\theta}$, the minimum has p.d.f.
$n\left(e^{-y/\theta}\right)^{n-1}\frac1\theta e^{-y/\theta} =
\frac{n}{\theta}e^{-ny/\theta}$: it is exponential with mean $\frac{\theta}{n}$.
So $E(T_2) = n\cdot\frac{\theta}{n} = \theta$.

\item[(b)] With $\ln f(x;\theta) = -\ln\theta - \frac{x}{\theta}$,
$\frac{\partial^2}{\partial\theta^2}\ln f = \frac{1}{\theta^2} -
\frac{2x}{\theta^3}$, so
\[J(\theta) = n\,E\left[\frac{2X}{\theta^3} - \frac{1}{\theta^2}\right] =
\frac{n}{\theta^2},\qquad \text{CRLB} = \frac{1}{J(\theta)} = \frac{\theta^2}{n},\]
in agreement with Example~\ref{ex:mleexpscale}.

\item[(c)] $\text{Var}(T_1) = \frac{\text{Var}(X)}{n} = \frac{\theta^2}{n}$,
using $E(X^2) = 2\theta^2$: $T_1$ attains the bound and is the UMVUE. Since
$X_{(1)}$ is exponential with mean $\frac\theta n$, its variance is
$\frac{\theta^2}{n^2}$, and $\text{Var}(T_2) = n^2\cdot\frac{\theta^2}{n^2} =
\theta^2$: $T_2$ does not attain the bound.

\item[(d)] $e(T_1) = \frac{\theta^2/n}{\theta^2/n} = 1$ and
$e(T_2) = \frac{\theta^2/n}{\theta^2} = \frac{1}{n}$. The estimator based on the
smallest observation alone wastes almost all the information.

\item[(e)] With $\tau(\theta) = \frac1\theta$, $\tau'(\theta) = -\frac{1}{\theta^2}$
and
\[\text{CRLB} = \frac{\theta^{-4}}{n/\theta^2} = \frac{1}{n\theta^2} .\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\subsubsection{Consistency}
\label{sec:consistency}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:asymptunbiased}
A sequence $\{T_n\}$ of estimators of $\tau(\theta)$ is \emph{asymptotically
unbiased} if $\lim_{n\to \infty} E(T_n) = \tau(\theta)$ for all
$\theta \in \Omega$, that is, if $\text{bias}(T_n)\to 0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:consistent}
A sequence $\{T_n\}$ of estimators of $\tau(\theta)$ is \emph{consistent} if
$T_n\overset{P}{\longrightarrow}\tau(\theta)$ for every $\theta \in \Omega$, that is,
if for every $\varepsilon > 0$,
\[\lim_{n\to \infty}P\left(|T_n - \tau(\theta)| < \varepsilon\right) = 1 .\]
\end{definition}

Convergence in probability and the weak law of large numbers are those of the
section on convergence and limit theorems. Two further facts are used: if
$X_n\overset{P}{\longrightarrow} a$ and $g$ is continuous at $a$, then
$g(X_n)\overset{P}{\longrightarrow} g(a)$; and if also
$Y_n\overset{P}{\longrightarrow} b$ and $g(x,y)$ is continuous at $(a,b)$, then
$g(X_n,Y_n)\overset{P}{\longrightarrow} g(a,b)$.

\begin{thm}
\label{thm:mseconsistency}
If $\{T_n\}$ is asymptotically unbiased for $\tau(\theta)$ and
$\lim_{n\to\infty}\text{Var}(T_n) = 0$, then $\{T_n\}$ is consistent.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:mse}, $\text{MSE}(T_n) = \text{Var}(T_n) +
[\text{bias}(T_n)]^2\to 0$, and by Chebyshev's inequality in the form
$P(|T_n - \tau|\geq\varepsilon)\leq\frac{E(T_n - \tau)^2}{\varepsilon^2}$, the
probability tends to $0$.
\end{proof}

The condition is sufficient but not necessary: a consistent estimator can have
infinite variance for every $n$.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:consistentvariance}
Let $X_1, \dots, X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\mu,\sigma^2)$, and let
$T = \frac{1}{n}\sum(X_i - \bar{X})^2$. Show that $T$ is asymptotically unbiased
and consistent for $\sigma^2$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Since $T = \frac{n-1}{n}S^2$ and $E(S^2) = \sigma^2$,
\[E(T) = \frac{n-1}{n}\sigma^2,\qquad \text{bias}(T) = -\frac{\sigma^2}{n}\to
0 .\]
For the variance, $\frac{(n-1)S^2}{\sigma^2}\sim\chi^2(n - 1)$, which has variance
$2(n - 1)$, so $\text{Var}(S^2) = \frac{2\sigma^4}{n - 1}$ and
\[\text{Var}(T) = \left(\frac{n - 1}{n}\right)^2\frac{2\sigma^4}{n - 1} =
\frac{2(n-1)\sigma^4}{n^2}\to 0 .\]
By Theorem~\ref{thm:mseconsistency}, $T$ is consistent. So is $S^2$, for the same
reason.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:consistentmax}
Show that $Y_n = X_{(n)}$ is consistent for $\theta$ for a random sample from the
uniform distribution on $(0,\theta)$, directly from the definition.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
For $0<\varepsilon<\theta$, since $Y_n<\theta$,
\[P(|Y_n - \theta|\geq\varepsilon) = P(Y_n\leq\theta - \varepsilon) = \left(\frac{\theta
- \varepsilon}{\theta}\right)^n\to 0 .\]
\end{solution}


\section{Practice problems}

Problems on estimation. Work each one before opening the solution.

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,X_2,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from
\[f(x;\theta)=\theta^2xe^{-\theta x},\qquad x>0,\ \theta>0.\]
Find (a) the method of moments estimator for $\theta$, (b) a sufficient statistic
for $\theta$, (c) the maximum likelihood estimator for $\theta$, (d) the information
function for $\theta$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Recognise the density first: it is $\text{GAM}(\alpha=2,\ \text{rate }\theta)$, which
saves deriving moments from scratch.

\textbf{(a) Method of moments.} The first population moment is
\[E(X)=\int_0^\infty x\cdot\theta^2xe^{-\theta x}\,dx
=\theta^2\int_0^\infty x^2e^{-\theta x}\,dx=\theta^2\cdot\frac{2}{\theta^3}
=\frac{2}{\theta}.\]
Equating it to the first sample moment $\bar X$,
\[\frac{2}{\theta}=\bar X\quad\implies\quad \tilde\theta=\frac{2}{\bar X}
=\frac{2n}{\sum_{i=1}^n X_i}.\]

\textbf{(b) Sufficient statistic.} The likelihood is
\[L(\theta)=\prod_{i=1}^n\theta^2x_ie^{-\theta x_i}
=\theta^{2n}e^{-\theta\sum x_i}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^n x_i.\]
This is already in the factorisation form $g\!\left(\sum x_i;\theta\right)h(\mathbf{x})$,
with
\[g\!\left(\textstyle\sum x_i;\theta\right)=\theta^{2n}e^{-\theta\sum x_i},
\qquad h(\mathbf{x})=\prod x_i,\]
and $h$ free of $\theta$. By the factorisation criterion
\[T=\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\ \text{ is sufficient for }\theta.\]

\textbf{(c) Maximum likelihood.} Take logarithms:
\[\ell(\theta)=2n\ln\theta+\sum\ln x_i-\theta\sum x_i.\]
\[\frac{d\ell}{d\theta}=\frac{2n}{\theta}-\sum x_i=0
\quad\implies\quad \hat\theta=\frac{2n}{\sum x_i}=\frac{2}{\bar X}.\]
It is a maximum because
\[\frac{d^2\ell}{d\theta^2}=-\frac{2n}{\theta^2}<0\quad\text{for every }\theta>0.\]

\textbf{(d) Information function.} For a single observation,
\[\ln f=2\ln\theta+\ln x-\theta x,\qquad
\frac{\partial\ln f}{\partial\theta}=\frac{2}{\theta}-x,\qquad
\frac{\partial^2\ln f}{\partial\theta^2}=-\frac{2}{\theta^2}.\]
The second derivative is already free of $x$, so taking the expectation changes
nothing:
\[I_1(\theta)=-E\!\left[\frac{\partial^2\ln f}{\partial\theta^2}\right]
=\frac{2}{\theta^2},\qquad\text{and for the sample}\qquad
I_n(\theta)=\frac{2n}{\theta^2}.\]

\begin{note}
The moment estimator and the maximum likelihood estimator coincide here. That is
not a general rule --- it happens because $E(X)$ is a one-to-one function of
$\theta$ and the likelihood equation reduces to the same relation. When they
disagree, it is the maximum likelihood estimator that inherits the good
large-sample properties.
\end{note}

\begin{note}
Part (d) is unusually quick because $\frac{\partial^2\ln f}{\partial\theta^2}$
contains no $x$. In general it does, and the expectation has to be evaluated ---
as it does in the next question.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,X_2,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from
\[f(x;\theta)=\binom{x-1}{2}\theta^3(1-\theta)^{x-3},\qquad x=3,4,5,\ldots\]
Find (a) the MME for $\theta$, (b) a sufficient statistic for $\theta$, (c) the MLE
for $\theta$, (d) the Fisher information function.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
This is the negative binomial in its ``trials until the third success'' form: $X$
counts the trials needed to obtain $3$ successes, each of probability $\theta$. Its
mean is therefore
\[E(X)=\frac{3}{\theta}.\]

\textbf{(a) Method of moments.}
\[\frac{3}{\theta}=\bar X\quad\implies\quad \tilde\theta=\frac{3}{\bar X}
=\frac{3n}{\sum X_i}.\]

\textbf{(b) Sufficient statistic.} The likelihood is
\[L(\theta)=\prod_{i=1}^n\binom{x_i-1}{2}\theta^3(1-\theta)^{x_i-3}
=\underbrace{\theta^{3n}(1-\theta)^{\sum x_i-3n}}_{g\left(\sum x_i;\theta\right)}
\cdot\underbrace{\prod_{i=1}^n\binom{x_i-1}{2}}_{h(\mathbf{x})}.\]
By the factorisation criterion,
\[T=\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\ \text{ is sufficient for }\theta.\]

\textbf{(c) Maximum likelihood.}
\[\ell(\theta)=\sum\ln\binom{x_i-1}{2}+3n\ln\theta+\left(\sum x_i-3n\right)\ln(1-\theta),\]
\[\frac{d\ell}{d\theta}=\frac{3n}{\theta}-\frac{\sum x_i-3n}{1-\theta}=0.\]
Multiplying through by $\theta(1-\theta)$,
\[3n(1-\theta)=\theta\left(\sum x_i-3n\right)
\ \implies\ 3n-3n\theta=\theta\sum x_i-3n\theta
\ \implies\ 3n=\theta\sum x_i,\]
the $3n\theta$ terms cancelling on both sides, so
\[\hat\theta=\frac{3n}{\sum x_i}=\frac{3}{\bar X}.\]

\textbf{(d) Fisher information.} For one observation the terms in $\theta$ are
\[\ln f=\text{constant}+3\ln\theta+(x-3)\ln(1-\theta),\]
\[\frac{\partial\ln f}{\partial\theta}=\frac{3}{\theta}-\frac{x-3}{1-\theta},
\qquad
\frac{\partial^2\ln f}{\partial\theta^2}=-\frac{3}{\theta^2}-\frac{x-3}{(1-\theta)^2}.\]
Now the second derivative \emph{does} involve $x$, so the expectation must be taken.
Since $E(X)=\frac{3}{\theta}$,
\[E(X-3)=\frac{3}{\theta}-3=\frac{3(1-\theta)}{\theta},\]
and therefore
\[I_1(\theta)=-E\!\left[\frac{\partial^2\ln f}{\partial\theta^2}\right]
=\frac{3}{\theta^2}+\frac{1}{(1-\theta)^2}\cdot\frac{3(1-\theta)}{\theta}
=\frac{3}{\theta^2}+\frac{3}{\theta(1-\theta)}.\]
Over the common denominator $\theta^2(1-\theta)$,
\[I_1(\theta)=\frac{3(1-\theta)+3\theta}{\theta^2(1-\theta)}
=\frac{3}{\theta^2(1-\theta)},\qquad
I_n(\theta)=\frac{3n}{\theta^2(1-\theta)}.\]

\begin{note}
The $3n\theta$ terms cancelling in (c) is what makes the likelihood equation
solvable in closed form. Notice also that the answer is $\frac{3}{\bar X}$, again
matching the moment estimator, and that both are just ``three successes divided by
the average number of trials'' --- which is what one would have guessed.
\end{note}
\end{solution}


\begin{problem}
Let $Y_1,\ldots,Y_n$ be a random sample from
\[f(y;\mu,\sigma^2)=\frac{1}{y\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^2}}
e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}(\log y-\mu)^2},\qquad y>0.\]
With $\theta=(\mu,\sigma^2)$, find (a) the sufficient statistic for $\theta$, (b) the
MLE for $\theta$, (c) the information matrix for $\theta$, (d) the MLE for $E(Y)$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
This is the lognormal density. The one observation worth making before any algebra
is that $W=\log Y\sim N(\mu,\sigma^2)$: everything then follows from the normal case.

\textbf{(a) Sufficient statistic.} Writing $w_i=\log y_i$,
\[L(\theta)=\left(2\pi\sigma^2\right)^{-n/2}\left(\prod y_i\right)^{-1}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum(w_i-\mu)^2\right).\]
Expanding the square, $\sum(w_i-\mu)^2=\sum w_i^2-2\mu\sum w_i+n\mu^2$, so
\[L(\theta)=\underbrace{\left(2\pi\sigma^2\right)^{-n/2}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\sum w_i^2-2\mu\sum w_i+n\mu^2}{2\sigma^2}\right)}
_{g\left(\sum w_i,\ \sum w_i^2;\ \theta\right)}
\cdot\underbrace{\left(\prod y_i\right)^{-1}}_{h(\mathbf{y})}.\]
\[\therefore\quad T=\left(\sum_{i=1}^n\log Y_i,\ \sum_{i=1}^n(\log Y_i)^2\right)
\text{ is jointly sufficient for }\theta.\]

\textbf{(b) Maximum likelihood.} These are the normal estimators applied to the
$w_i$:
\[\hat\mu=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n\log Y_i,\qquad
