\hat\sigma^2=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n\left(\log Y_i-\hat\mu\right)^2.\]

\textbf{(c) Information matrix.} Again as for the normal in $w$,
\[I(\theta)=n\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sigma^2} & 0\\[3pt]
0 & \frac{1}{2\sigma^4}\end{pmatrix}.\]
The off-diagonal entries are zero, so $\hat\mu$ and $\hat\sigma^2$ are
asymptotically independent.

\textbf{(d) MLE of $E(Y)$.} For the lognormal,
\[E(Y)=e^{\mu+\frac{\sigma^2}{2}}.\]
By the invariance property of maximum likelihood --- the MLE of $\tau(\theta)$ is
$\tau$ evaluated at the MLE ---
\[\widehat{E(Y)}=e^{\hat\mu+\frac{\hat\sigma^2}{2}}.\]

\begin{note}
Note what $E(Y)$ is \emph{not}: it is not $e^{\hat\mu}$. The $\frac{\sigma^2}{2}$
term is the whole difference between the median of a lognormal, $e^{\mu}$, and its
mean. Reporting $e^{\hat\mu}$ as the estimated mean understates it, and by more the
larger the variance.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from $X\sim\text{UNIF}(0,\theta)$. Let
$T_1=X_{(n)}$ and let $T_2$ be the MME for $\theta$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Find $T_2$. Is it an unbiased estimator of $\theta$?
\item[(b).] Is $T_1$ asymptotically unbiased for $\theta$?
\item[(c).] Show that $T_1$ is a consistent estimator of $\theta$.
\item[(d).] Find the MSE of $T_1$. \quad (e) Find the MSE of $T_2$.
\quad (f) Find $e(T_1,T_2)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a).} $E(X)=\frac{\theta}{2}$, so equating to $\bar X$ gives
\[T_2=2\bar X,\qquad E(T_2)=2\cdot\frac{\theta}{2}=\theta.\]
So $T_2$ is \textbf{unbiased}.

\textbf{(b).} The largest order statistic has density
\[g(x)=\frac{nx^{n-1}}{\theta^n},\qquad 0<x<\theta,\]
so
\[E(T_1)=\int_0^\theta x\cdot\frac{nx^{n-1}}{\theta^n}\,dx=\frac{n\theta}{n+1}
\ \longrightarrow\ \theta\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.\]
It is biased for every finite $n$ --- always \emph{under}-estimating, as it must,
since the largest observation cannot exceed $\theta$ --- but
\textbf{asymptotically unbiased}.

\textbf{(c).} Also
\[E\left(T_1^2\right)=\int_0^\theta x^2\cdot\frac{nx^{n-1}}{\theta^n}\,dx
=\frac{n\theta^2}{n+2},\]
so, using the result of (d) below,
\[\text{MSE}(T_1)=\frac{2\theta^2}{(n+1)(n+2)}\ \longrightarrow\ 0
\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.\]
An estimator whose mean squared error tends to zero converges in probability to
$\theta$, so $T_1$ is \textbf{consistent}.

\textbf{(d).} Since $T_1$ is biased, use $\text{MSE}=E\left(T_1^2\right)-2\theta
E(T_1)+\theta^2$:
\[\text{MSE}(T_1)=\frac{n\theta^2}{n+2}-\frac{2n\theta^2}{n+1}+\theta^2
=\theta^2\cdot\frac{n(n+1)-2n(n+2)+(n+1)(n+2)}{(n+1)(n+2)}.\]
The numerator is
\[\left(n^2+n\right)-\left(2n^2+4n\right)+\left(n^2+3n+2\right)=2,\]
the $n^2$ and $n$ terms cancelling completely, so
\[\text{MSE}(T_1)=\frac{2\theta^2}{(n+1)(n+2)}.\]

\textbf{(e).} $T_2$ is unbiased, so its MSE is its variance. With
$\text{Var}(X)=\frac{\theta^2}{12}$,
\[\text{MSE}(T_2)=\text{Var}\left(2\bar X\right)=4\cdot\frac{\theta^2}{12n}
=\frac{\theta^2}{3n}.\]

\textbf{(f).}
\[e(T_1,T_2)=\frac{\text{MSE}(T_2)}{\text{MSE}(T_1)}
=\frac{\frac{\theta^2}{3n}}{\frac{2\theta^2}{(n+1)(n+2)}}
=\frac{(n+1)(n+2)}{6n}.\]

\begin{note}
This is the standard cautionary example. $T_2$ is unbiased and $T_1$ is not, yet
$T_1$ is enormously better: its MSE falls like $\frac{1}{n^2}$ while $T_2$'s falls
only like $\frac{1}{n}$, and the efficiency ratio grows without bound. Unbiasedness
on its own is not a reason to prefer an estimator.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from $f(x;\theta)=\frac{2\theta^2}{x^3}$,
$x>\theta$. Let $T_1$ be the MME and $T_2$ the MLE for $\theta$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Find $T_1$. Is it unbiased for $\theta$? \quad (b) Find $T_2$.
\item[(c).] Show that $T_2$ is asymptotically unbiased.
\item[(d).] Show that $T_2$ is a consistent estimator.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a).}
\[E(X)=\int_\theta^\infty x\cdot\frac{2\theta^2}{x^3}\,dx
=2\theta^2\int_\theta^\infty x^{-2}dx=2\theta^2\cdot\frac{1}{\theta}=2\theta.\]
Setting $2\theta=\bar X$,
\[T_1=\frac{\bar X}{2},\qquad E(T_1)=\frac{2\theta}{2}=\theta,\]
so $T_1$ is \textbf{unbiased}.

\textbf{(b).} The likelihood is
\[L(\theta)=\frac{2^n\theta^{2n}}{\prod x_i^3},\qquad\text{valid only while }
\theta<x_i\text{ for every }i.\]
Differentiating is useless here: $L$ is \emph{increasing} in $\theta$, so it is made
as large as the constraint permits. The binding constraint is
$\theta\leq\min_i x_i$, hence
\[T_2=X_{(1)}=\min_i X_i.\]

\textbf{(c).} The smallest order statistic has density
\[g(x)=n\left[1-F(x)\right]^{n-1}f(x)
=n\left(\frac{\theta^2}{x^2}\right)^{n-1}\cdot\frac{2\theta^2}{x^3}
=\frac{2n\theta^{2n}}{x^{2n+1}},\qquad x>\theta,\]
using $F(x)=1-\frac{\theta^2}{x^2}$. Then
\[E(T_2)=\int_\theta^\infty x\cdot\frac{2n\theta^{2n}}{x^{2n+1}}\,dx
=2n\theta^{2n}\int_\theta^\infty x^{-2n}dx
=2n\theta^{2n}\cdot\frac{\theta^{1-2n}}{2n-1}=\frac{2n\theta}{2n-1}.\]
As $n\to\infty$ this tends to $\theta$, so $T_2$ is \textbf{asymptotically
unbiased}. Note it over-estimates, the mirror image of the uniform case, because
the smallest observation must exceed $\theta$.

\textbf{(d).} By the same integral with $x^2$,
\[E\left(T_2^2\right)=2n\theta^{2n}\int_\theta^\infty x^{1-2n}dx
=\frac{2n\theta^2}{2n-2}=\frac{n\theta^2}{n-1}.\]
Hence
\[\text{MSE}(T_2)=\frac{n\theta^2}{n-1}-\frac{4n\theta^2}{2n-1}+\theta^2
=\frac{\theta^2}{(n-1)(2n-1)}\ \longrightarrow\ 0,\]
so $T_2$ is \textbf{consistent}.

\begin{note}
Part (b) is the one to be careful with. Setting $\frac{d\ell}{d\theta}=0$ finds
nothing, because the likelihood has no interior stationary point --- the maximum is
at the boundary of the parameter space. Whenever the range of $x$ depends on
$\theta$, look at the constraint before differentiating.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from $f(x;\theta)=\theta e^{-\theta x}$,
$x>0$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Find the MLE of $\theta$.
\item[(b).] Find the MLE of $\frac{1}{\theta}$. Is it unbiased?
\item[(c).] Is the MLE of $\frac{1}{\theta}$ a U.M.V.U.E.?
\item[(d).] Is the MLE of $\theta$ consistent? \quad (e) Is the MLE of
$\frac{1}{\theta}$ consistent?
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a).} $\ell(\theta)=n\ln\theta-\theta\sum x_i$, so
\[\frac{d\ell}{d\theta}=\frac{n}{\theta}-\sum x_i=0\quad\implies\quad
\hat\theta=\frac{n}{\sum X_i}=\frac{1}{\bar X},\]
a maximum since $\frac{d^2\ell}{d\theta^2}=-\frac{n}{\theta^2}<0$.

\textbf{(b).} By invariance, the MLE of $\frac{1}{\theta}$ is
$\frac{1}{\hat\theta}=\bar X$. Since $E(X)=\frac{1}{\theta}$,
\[E\left(\bar X\right)=\frac{1}{\theta},\]
so it is \textbf{unbiased}.

\textbf{(c).} The exponential family here has $T=\sum X_i$ as a complete sufficient
statistic for $\theta$. Now $\bar X=\frac{T}{n}$ is a function of $T$ alone and is
unbiased for $\frac{1}{\theta}$. By the Lehmann--Scheff\'e theorem an unbiased
function of a complete sufficient statistic is the unique UMVUE, so
\[\bar X\ \text{ is the U.M.V.U.E. of }\ \frac{1}{\theta}.\]

\textbf{(d) and (e).} By the weak law of large numbers $\bar X\xrightarrow{P}
E(X)=\frac{1}{\theta}$, so the estimator in (b) is \textbf{consistent}. Since
$g(u)=\frac{1}{u}$ is continuous at $u=\frac{1}{\theta}\neq 0$, the continuous
mapping theorem gives
\[\hat\theta=\frac{1}{\bar X}\xrightarrow{P}\theta,\]
so the estimator in (a) is \textbf{consistent} as well.

\begin{note}
Invariance transfers straight through maximum likelihood but \emph{not} through
unbiasedness. Here $\bar X$ is unbiased for $\frac{1}{\theta}$ while
$\frac{1}{\bar X}$ is \emph{not} unbiased for $\theta$ --- in fact
$E\left(\frac{1}{\bar X}\right)=\frac{n\theta}{n-1}$. Consistency, by contrast,
does transfer, which is what (d) uses.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from
$f(x;\theta)=\frac{e^{-\theta}\theta^x}{x!}$, $x=0,1,2,\ldots$
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Find the MLE of $\theta$.
\item[(b).] Find the CRLB for the variances of unbiased estimators of $\theta$.
\item[(c).] Is the MLE of $\theta$ a U.M.V.U.E.?
\item[(d).] Find the CRLB for variances of unbiased estimators of $e^{-\theta}$.
\item[(e).] Show that $T=\left(\frac{n-1}{n}\right)^{\sum_{i=1}^nX_i}$ is unbiased
for $e^{-\theta}$. \quad (f) Find the efficiency of $T$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a).} $\ell(\theta)=-n\theta+\ln\theta\sum x_i-\sum\ln x_i!$, so
\[\frac{d\ell}{d\theta}=-n+\frac{\sum x_i}{\theta}=0\quad\implies\quad
\hat\theta=\bar X.\]

\textbf{(b).} From $\ln f=-\theta+x\ln\theta-\ln x!$,
\[\frac{\partial^2\ln f}{\partial\theta^2}=-\frac{x}{\theta^2},\qquad
I_1(\theta)=-E\!\left[-\frac{X}{\theta^2}\right]=\frac{\theta}{\theta^2}
=\frac{1}{\theta}.\]
\[\therefore\quad \text{CRLB}=\frac{1}{nI_1(\theta)}=\frac{\theta}{n}.\]

\textbf{(c).} $\bar X$ is unbiased for $\theta$ and
$\text{Var}\left(\bar X\right)=\frac{\theta}{n}$, which \emph{equals} the bound. An
unbiased estimator attaining the CRLB has the smallest possible variance, so
$\bar X$ is the \textbf{U.M.V.U.E.} of $\theta$.

\textbf{(d).} For $\tau(\theta)=e^{-\theta}$ we have
$\tau'(\theta)=-e^{-\theta}$, and the bound for a function of $\theta$ is
\[\text{CRLB}=\frac{\left[\tau'(\theta)\right]^2}{nI_1(\theta)}
=\frac{e^{-2\theta}}{\frac{n}{\theta}}=\frac{\theta e^{-2\theta}}{n}.\]

\textbf{(e).} Since $\sum X_i\sim\text{POI}(n\theta)$, its probability generating
function is $E\left(s^{\sum X_i}\right)=e^{n\theta(s-1)}$. Putting
$s=\frac{n-1}{n}$,
\[E(T)=e^{n\theta\left(\frac{n-1}{n}-1\right)}
=e^{n\theta\left(-\frac{1}{n}\right)}=e^{-\theta},\]
so $T$ is \textbf{unbiased} for $e^{-\theta}$.

\textbf{(f).} Using the same generating function with $s^2$,
\[E\left(T^2\right)=e^{n\theta\left(\left(\frac{n-1}{n}\right)^2-1\right)}
=e^{n\theta\cdot\frac{1-2n}{n^2}}=e^{-2\theta+\frac{\theta}{n}},\]
\[\text{Var}(T)=e^{-2\theta+\frac{\theta}{n}}-e^{-2\theta}
=e^{-2\theta}\left(e^{\theta/n}-1\right).\]
Therefore
\[\text{eff}(T)=\frac{\text{CRLB}}{\text{Var}(T)}
=\frac{\frac{\theta e^{-2\theta}}{n}}{e^{-2\theta}\left(e^{\theta/n}-1\right)}
=\frac{\theta}{n\left(e^{\theta/n}-1\right)}.\]

\begin{note}
$T$ does not attain the bound, so it is not efficient for any finite $n$. But for
large $n$ the exponent $\frac{\theta}{n}$ is small and
$e^{\theta/n}-1\approx\frac{\theta}{n}$, which makes the efficiency tend to $1$:
$T$ is \emph{asymptotically} efficient.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from
\[f(x;\theta)=\frac{1}{2^\theta\Gamma(\theta)}x^{\theta-1}e^{-\frac{x}{2}},
\qquad x>0.\]
Let $T_1$ be the MME and $T_2$ the MLE for $\theta$. Find (a) $T_1$, (b) $T_2$,
(c) the MSE of $T_1$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
This is $\text{GAM}(\alpha=\theta,\ \beta=2)$: shape unknown, scale known and equal
to $2$. So $E(X)=2\theta$ and $\text{Var}(X)=4\theta$.

\textbf{(a).} Equating $E(X)=2\theta$ to $\bar X$,
\[T_1=\frac{\bar X}{2}.\]

\textbf{(b).} The log-likelihood is
\[\ell(\theta)=-n\theta\ln 2-n\ln\Gamma(\theta)+(\theta-1)\sum\ln x_i
-\frac{1}{2}\sum x_i,\]
\[\frac{d\ell}{d\theta}=-n\ln 2-n\psi(\theta)+\sum\ln x_i=0,\]
where $\psi=\frac{\Gamma'}{\Gamma}$ is the digamma function. So $T_2$ solves
\[\psi(\theta)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n\ln x_i-\ln 2.\]
There is no closed form: $\psi$ cannot be inverted in elementary functions, so
$T_2$ must be obtained numerically --- by Newton's method, as in section~\ref{sec:newton}.

\textbf{(c).} $T_1$ is unbiased, since
$E\left(\frac{\bar X}{2}\right)=\frac{2\theta}{2}=\theta$, so its MSE is its
variance:
\[\text{MSE}(T_1)=\text{Var}\!\left(\frac{\bar X}{2}\right)
=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{\text{Var}(X)}{n}=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{4\theta}{n}
=\frac{\theta}{n}.\]

\begin{note}
This is the standard case where the two methods genuinely part company. The moment
estimator is immediate and has a clean MSE; the likelihood estimator has no closed
form at all. That is the usual trade --- moments are easy to write down, maximum
likelihood is usually better behaved, and when the likelihood equation will not
solve, the moment estimator makes a good starting value for the iteration.
\end{note}
\end{solution}


\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from
\[f_X(x;\alpha,\lambda)=\lambda\frac{(\lambda x)^{\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda x}}
{\Gamma(\alpha)},\qquad \alpha>0,\ \lambda>0,\ x>0.\]
Find (i) joint sufficient statistics for $\theta=(\alpha,\lambda)$, (ii) the MLE
for $\lambda$ if $\alpha=4$, (iii) the Cram\'er--Rao lower bound for $\lambda$ if
$\alpha=4$, (iv) the UMVUE for $\lambda$ if it exists when $\alpha=4$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(i).} The likelihood is
\[L=\frac{\lambda^{n\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)^n}
\left(\prod x_i\right)^{\alpha-1}e^{-\lambda\sum x_i}.\]
Both $\prod x_i$ and $\sum x_i$ appear, and neither can be dispensed with while
$\alpha$ is unknown, so
\[T=\left(\sum_{i=1}^nX_i,\ \prod_{i=1}^nX_i\right)
\quad\text{--- equivalently }\left(\sum X_i,\ \sum\ln X_i\right)\text{ ---}\]
is jointly sufficient for $(\alpha,\lambda)$.

\textbf{(ii).} With $\alpha=4$ the density is
$f(x)=\frac{\lambda^4x^3e^{-\lambda x}}{6}$, so
\[\ell(\lambda)=4n\ln\lambda+3\sum\ln x_i-\lambda\sum x_i-n\ln 6,\]
\[\frac{d\ell}{d\lambda}=\frac{4n}{\lambda}-\sum x_i=0
\quad\implies\quad \hat\lambda=\frac{4n}{\sum X_i}=\frac{4}{\bar X}.\]

\textbf{(iii).} From $\ln f=4\ln\lambda+3\ln x-\lambda x-\ln 6$,
\[\frac{\partial^2\ln f}{\partial\lambda^2}=-\frac{4}{\lambda^2},\qquad
I_1(\lambda)=\frac{4}{\lambda^2},\qquad
\text{CRLB}=\frac{1}{nI_1(\lambda)}=\frac{\lambda^2}{4n}.\]

\textbf{(iv).} With $\alpha$ known, this is a one-parameter exponential family and
$T=\sum X_i$ is a \emph{complete} sufficient statistic. Since each
$X_i\sim\text{GAM}(4,\lambda)$, we have $T\sim\text{GAM}(4n,\lambda)$, and
\[E\!\left(\frac{1}{T}\right)
=\int_0^\infty\frac{1}{t}\cdot\frac{\lambda^{4n}t^{4n-1}e^{-\lambda t}}
{\Gamma(4n)}\,dt=\frac{\lambda\,\Gamma(4n-1)}{\Gamma(4n)}=\frac{\lambda}{4n-1}.\]
So the MLE $\frac{4n}{T}$ is \emph{biased}, with
$E\!\left(\frac{4n}{T}\right)=\frac{4n\lambda}{4n-1}$. Correcting the constant,
\[E\!\left(\frac{4n-1}{T}\right)=\lambda,\]
and $\frac{4n-1}{T}$ is a function of the complete sufficient statistic alone. By
the Lehmann--Scheff\'e theorem,
\[\text{UMVUE of }\lambda\ =\ \frac{4n-1}{\sum_{i=1}^nX_i}.\]

\begin{note}
The UMVUE does \emph{not} attain the Cram\'er--Rao bound here. Using
$E\!\left(\frac{1}{T^2}\right)=\frac{\lambda^2}{(4n-1)(4n-2)}$,
\[\text{Var}\!\left(\frac{4n-1}{T}\right)=\frac{\lambda^2}{4n-2}
\quad>\quad \frac{\lambda^2}{4n}=\text{CRLB},\]
the gap being $\frac{\lambda^2}{4n(2n-1)}$. So ``minimum variance among unbiased
estimators'' and ``attains the CRLB'' are not the same statement --- the bound is
simply not always achievable, and an estimator can be the best there is without
reaching it.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from
$f_X(x;\lambda)=\frac{\lambda^xe^{-\lambda}}{x!}$, $x=0,1,2,\ldots$
Find (i) a sufficient statistic for $\lambda$, (ii) the efficiency of the MLE for
$\lambda$, (iii) the relative efficiency of the MLE to $W=X_1$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(i).} The likelihood is
\[L=\frac{\lambda^{\sum x_i}e^{-n\lambda}}{\prod x_i!}
=\underbrace{\lambda^{\sum x_i}e^{-n\lambda}}_{g\left(\sum x_i;\lambda\right)}
\cdot\underbrace{\frac{1}{\prod x_i!}}_{h(\mathbf{x})},\]
so $T=\sum_{i=1}^nX_i$ is sufficient for $\lambda$.

\textbf{(ii).} The MLE is $\hat\lambda=\bar X$, with
$\text{Var}\left(\bar X\right)=\frac{\lambda}{n}$. From
$\frac{\partial^2\ln f}{\partial\lambda^2}=-\frac{x}{\lambda^2}$ we get
$I_1(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\lambda}$ and $\text{CRLB}=\frac{\lambda}{n}$. Hence
\[\text{eff}\left(\hat\lambda\right)
=\frac{\text{CRLB}}{\text{Var}\left(\bar X\right)}
=\frac{\lambda/n}{\lambda/n}=1.\]
The MLE is \textbf{efficient} --- it attains the bound exactly.

\textbf{(iii).} $W=X_1$ is also unbiased for $\lambda$, since $E(X_1)=\lambda$, but
it throws away $n-1$ of the observations. With $\text{Var}(W)=\lambda$,
\[\text{RE}\left(\hat\lambda,W\right)
=\frac{\text{Var}(W)}{\text{Var}\left(\hat\lambda\right)}
=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda/n}=n.\]
The sample mean is $n$ times as efficient as a single observation.

\begin{note}
Contrast this with the previous question. There the UMVUE fell short of the bound;
here the MLE meets it exactly. The Poisson mean is one of the cases where the
score function factors as $I(\lambda)\left(\bar x-\lambda\right)$, and that
proportionality is precisely the condition for equality in the Cram\'er--Rao
inequality.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from
\[f_X(x;\theta)=\frac{\theta}{(1+x)^{\theta+1}},\qquad x>0,\ \theta>0.\]
Find (i) a sufficient statistic for $\theta$, (ii) the Cram\'er--Rao lower bound
for $\theta$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(i).} Write the likelihood with the product in the exponent:
\[L=\frac{\theta^n}{\prod(1+x_i)^{\theta+1}}
=\theta^n\exp\!\left(-(\theta+1)\sum\ln(1+x_i)\right).\]
This depends on the sample only through $\sum\ln(1+x_i)$, so
\[T=\sum_{i=1}^n\ln\left(1+X_i\right)\ \text{ is sufficient for }\theta.\]

\textbf{(ii).} From $\ln f=\ln\theta-(\theta+1)\ln(1+x)$,
\[\frac{\partial\ln f}{\partial\theta}=\frac{1}{\theta}-\ln(1+x),\qquad
\frac{\partial^2\ln f}{\partial\theta^2}=-\frac{1}{\theta^2}.\]
The second derivative is free of $x$, so
\[I_1(\theta)=\frac{1}{\theta^2},\qquad
\text{CRLB}=\frac{1}{nI_1(\theta)}=\frac{\theta^2}{n}.\]

\begin{note}
The substitution $Y=\ln(1+X)$ makes this transparent. Since
$P(X>x)=(1+x)^{-\theta}$,
\[P(Y>y)=P\!\left(X>e^y-1\right)=e^{-\theta y},\]
so $Y\sim\text{EXP}(\theta)$ with $E(Y)=\frac{1}{\theta}$. That is why the
sufficient statistic is a sum of logarithms, and it confirms the score has mean
zero: $E\!\left[\frac{1}{\theta}-\ln(1+X)\right]=\frac{1}{\theta}-\frac{1}{\theta}=0$,
as it must.
\end{note}
\end{solution}


\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,X_2,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from
\[f(x,\theta)=\theta^x(1-\theta)^{1-x},\qquad x=0,1.\]
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Find a sufficient statistic for $\theta$.
\item[(b).] Find the maximum likelihood estimator of $\theta$.
\item[(c).] Determine whether the estimator $T=\bar X$ is a U.M.V.U.E. for
$\theta$.
\item[(d).] Determine whether $T_1=\bar X^2$ is a consistent estimator for
$\tau(\theta)=\theta^2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
This is the Bernoulli distribution, $E(X)=\theta$ and
$\text{Var}(X)=\theta(1-\theta)$.

\textbf{(a).} The likelihood factorises as
\[L(\theta)=\prod_{i=1}^n\theta^{x_i}(1-\theta)^{1-x_i}
=\theta^{\sum x_i}(1-\theta)^{n-\sum x_i}
=\underbrace{\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\sum x_i}
(1-\theta)^n}_{g\left(\sum x_i,\ \theta\right)}\cdot\underbrace{1}_{h(\underline{x})},\]
so by the factorisation criterion
\[S=\sum_{i=1}^nX_i\]
is sufficient for $\theta$.

\textbf{(b).} Taking logarithms,
\[\ell(\theta)=\left(\sum x_i\right)\ln\theta
+\left(n-\sum x_i\right)\ln(1-\theta),\]
\[\ell'(\theta)=\frac{\sum x_i}{\theta}-\frac{n-\sum x_i}{1-\theta}=0
\quad\implies\quad(1-\theta)\sum x_i=\theta\left(n-\sum x_i\right),\]
which gives $\sum x_i=n\theta$ and hence
\[\hat\theta=\frac{\sum x_i}{n}=\bar X,\]
with $\ell''(\theta)<0$ throughout, confirming a maximum.

\textbf{(c).} Written in exponential family form,
\[f(x,\theta)=(1-\theta)\exp\left[x\ln\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right],\]
so $S=\sum X_i$ is a \emph{complete} sufficient statistic. Now $\bar X=S/n$ is a
function of $S$ alone and is unbiased,
$E\left(\bar X\right)=\frac{1}{n}\cdot n\theta=\theta$. By the
Lehmann--Scheff\'e theorem,
\[\therefore\quad T=\bar X\ \textbf{is the UMVUE of }\theta.\]

\textbf{(d).} $\bar X\stackrel{p}{\longrightarrow}\theta$ by the weak law of
large numbers, and squaring is a continuous function, so
$\bar X^2\stackrel{p}{\longrightarrow}\theta^2$. Directly:
\[E\left(\bar X^2\right)=\text{Var}\left(\bar X\right)+\left[E\bar X\right]^2
=\frac{\theta(1-\theta)}{n}+\theta^2\ \longrightarrow\ \theta^2,\]
and the variance of $\bar X^2$ tends to $0$ as well, so the mean square error
vanishes.
\[\therefore\quad T_1=\bar X^2\ \textbf{is consistent for }\theta^2.\]

\begin{note}
$T_1$ is consistent but \emph{biased} at every finite $n$, by exactly
$\frac{\theta(1-\theta)}{n}$. Squaring an unbiased estimator does not give an
unbiased estimator of the square --- the gap is the variance, and it is only
consistency that survives.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,X_2,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from
\[f(x;\theta)=e^{-(x-\theta)},\qquad x>\theta.\]
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Find the method of moments estimator of $\theta$.
\item[(b).] Find the maximum likelihood estimator of $\theta$.
\item[(c).] Determine whether $T=\frac{nX_{(1)}-2}{n}$ is an asymptotically
unbiased estimator of $\theta$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
This is the exponential distribution shifted to start at $\theta$: if
$Z\sim\text{EXP}(1)$ then $X=\theta+Z$.

\textbf{(a).} $E(X)=\theta+E(Z)=\theta+1$. Equating to the first sample moment,
\[\bar X=\theta+1\qquad\implies\qquad\tilde\theta=\bar X-1.\]

\textbf{(b).} The likelihood needs care, because $\theta$ appears in the
\emph{support}:
\[L(\theta)=\prod_{i=1}^ne^{-(x_i-\theta)}=e^{-\sum x_i}e^{n\theta}
\qquad\text{provided }x_i>\theta\ \text{for every }i.\]
As a function of $\theta$ this is strictly increasing, so it is made as large as
possible by taking $\theta$ as large as the constraint allows. The constraint is
$\theta<\min_ix_i$, hence
\[\hat\theta=X_{(1)}=\min\left(X_1,\ldots,X_n\right).\]
Differentiating would have given $\ell'(\theta)=n\neq0$ and no solution at all ---
the maximum is at the boundary, not at a turning point.

\textbf{(c).} First find $E\left(X_{(1)}\right)$. The minimum exceeds $m$ only if
every observation does, so for $m>\theta$
\[P\left(X_{(1)}>m\right)=\left[e^{-(m-\theta)}\right]^n=e^{-n(m-\theta)},
\qquad f_{X_{(1)}}(m)=ne^{-n(m-\theta)}.\]
So $X_{(1)}$ is itself shifted exponential, with mean $\frac1n$ above $\theta$:
\[E\left(X_{(1)}\right)=\int_\theta^\infty m\,ne^{-n(m-\theta)}dm
=\theta+\frac{1}{n}.\]
Therefore
\[E(T)=E\left(X_{(1)}-\frac{2}{n}\right)=\theta+\frac{1}{n}-\frac{2}{n}
=\theta-\frac{1}{n},\]
\[\lim_{n\to\infty}E(T)=\theta,\]
\[\therefore\quad T\ \textbf{is asymptotically unbiased for }\theta,\]
though it is biased by $-\frac1n$ at every finite $n$.

\begin{note}
The maximum likelihood estimator $X_{(1)}$ overshoots by $\frac1n$, so
$X_{(1)}-\frac1n$ is \emph{exactly} unbiased. The estimator in the question
subtracts $\frac2n$ — twice as much — which overcorrects and leaves a bias of
$-\frac1n$. Both are asymptotically unbiased, so the property is a weak one: it
says nothing about how good the estimator is at the sample size you actually
have.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\chapter{Hypothesis Testing}

\section{Introduction}

In scientific work we often have to decide whether a claim about a population is
supported by data. Because measurements are subject to random error, any decision
we make from a sample can be wrong. The theory of hypothesis testing does not
remove that risk; it arranges for the errors to occur at a rate fixed in advance,
and among the rules that do so it looks for the one most likely to detect a false
claim.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:hypothesis}
A \emph{statistical hypothesis} is a statement about the distribution of a random
variable $X$. When the distribution is $f(x;\theta)$ with $\theta$ in a parameter
space $\Omega$, a hypothesis has the form $H\colon \theta\in\Omega_0$ for a subset
$\Omega_0\subset\Omega$.
\begin{itemize}
\item A hypothesis is \emph{simple} if it specifies the distribution completely,
that is, $\Omega_0$ contains a single point.
\item Otherwise it is \emph{composite}.
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:simplecomposite}
Let $X\sim N(\mu,\sigma^2)$.
\begin{itemize}
\item $H\colon \mu = 4,\ \sigma^2 = 16$ is simple.
\item $H\colon \mu = 4,\ \sigma^2>0$ is composite, since $\sigma^2$ is not fixed.
\item $H\colon \mu\le 4$ with $\sigma^2 = 16$ known is composite.
\end{itemize}
\end{example}

We test a \emph{null hypothesis} $H_0\colon\theta\in\Omega_0$ against an
\emph{alternative} $H_1\colon\theta\in\Omega_1$, where $\Omega_0$ and $\Omega_1$
are disjoint subsets of $\Omega$, usually with $\Omega_1 = \Omega - \Omega_0$.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:test}
A \emph{test} is a rule that decides, for each possible sample
$x = (x_1,\dots,x_n)$, whether to reject $H_0$. The set of samples for which $H_0$
is rejected is the \emph{critical region} (or rejection region) $R$. Usually $R$ is
described through a \emph{test statistic} $T = t(X_1,\dots,X_n)$, for example
\[R = \{x : t(x)\ge c\} .\]
\end{definition}

A test is carried out in four steps:
\begin{enumerate}
\item state the hypotheses $H_0\colon\theta\in\Omega_0$ and
$H_1\colon\theta\in\Omega_1$;
\item choose the critical region $R$, before seeing the data;
\item collect the sample $x_1,\dots,x_n$;
\item reject $H_0$ if $x\in R$, and otherwise do not reject it.
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Errors and the power function}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:power}
The \emph{power function} of a test with critical region $R$ is
\[\pi(\theta) = P_\theta(X\in R),\qquad \theta\in\Omega ,\]
the probability of rejecting $H_0$ when the true parameter value is $\theta$.
\end{definition}

Two kinds of error are possible, as table~\ref{tab:errors} shows.
\begin{itemize}
\item A \emph{Type I error} is rejecting $H_0$ when it is true. Its probability
is $\alpha(\theta) = \pi(\theta)$ for $\theta\in\Omega_0$.
\item A \emph{Type II error} is failing to reject $H_0$ when it is false. Its
probability is $\beta(\theta) = P_\theta(X\notin R) = 1 - \pi(\theta)$ for
$\theta\in\Omega_1$.
\end{itemize}
An ideal test has $\pi(\theta)=0$ on $\Omega_0$ and $\pi(\theta)=1$ on
$\Omega_1$. With a finite sample this is impossible: shrinking $R$ lowers the
Type I error but raises the Type II error, as figure~\ref{fig:errors} shows.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\caption{The four outcomes of a test.}
\label{tab:errors}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.6}
\begin{tabular}{|l|c|c|}
\hline
 & $H_0$ true & $H_0$ false \\ \hline
Do not reject $H_0$ & correct decision & Type II error ($\beta$) \\ \hline
Reject $H_0$ & Type I error ($\alpha$) & correct decision (power $1-\beta$) \\ \hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    % Customizing the look for elegance
    declare function={norm(\x,\m,\s)=exp(-(\x-\m)^2/(2*\s^2));},
    >=stealth
]
\begin{axis}[
    width=12cm, height=7cm,
    axis lines=middle,
    inner axis line style={->, color=gray!60},
    xlabel={$T$}, ylabel={},
    domain=-3:9, samples=150,
    ytick=\empty,
    xtick={0, 3, 5.5},
    xticklabels={$\mu_0$, $c$, $\mu_1$},
    enlargelimits=upper,
    clip=false,
    axis on top % Ensures lines sit nicely on top of shading
]

    % --- 1. Shading Areas (The "Elegance" factor: Opacity and Pastel colors) ---
    
    % Alpha: Area under H0 to the right of c
    \addplot [fill=cyan!20, draw=none, domain=3:8] {norm(x,0,1)} \closedcycle;
    
    % Beta: Area under H1 to the left of c
    \addplot [fill=orange!20, draw=none, domain=-1:3] {norm(x,5.5,1)} \closedcycle;

    % --- 2. Plotting the Curves ---
    
    % H0 Distribution
    \addplot [very thick, cyan!80!black] {norm(x,0,1)};
    \node[cyan!80!black] at (axis cs: 0, 1.1) {\footnotesize Null $H_0$};

    % H1 Distribution
    \addplot [very thick, orange!80!black] {norm(x,5.5,1)};
    \node[orange!80!black] at (axis cs: 5.5, 1.1) {\footnotesize Alt $H_1$};

    % --- 3. Decision Boundary ---
    \draw [thick, gray!80, dashed] (axis cs:3,0) -- (axis cs:3,1.1) 
        node[pos=1.05, black] {\footnotesize Critical Value};

    % --- 4. Refined Labeling with Pointers ---
    
    % Alpha Label
    \draw[<-] (axis cs:3.4, 0.05) .. controls (axis cs:4.5, 0.2) .. (axis cs:5, 0.5) 
        node[right, font=\small] {$\alpha$ \scriptsize (Type I Error)};
        
    % Beta Label
    \draw[<-] (axis cs:2.6, 0.05) .. controls (axis cs:1.5, 0.2) .. (axis cs:1, 0.5) 
        node[left, font=\small] {$\beta$ \scriptsize (Type II Error)};
        
    % Power Label
    \node[orange!80!black] at (axis cs: 7.5, 0.2) {\small Power $(1-\beta)$};

    % Rejection Region Indicator
    \draw[thick, <->, gray] (axis cs:3, -0.15) -- (axis cs:8.5, -0.15) 
        node[midway, below, black] {\scriptsize Rejection Region};

\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Distribution overlap illustrating Type I and Type II errors.}
\label{fig:errors}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{The Neyman--Pearson approach}

Neyman and Pearson proposed treating the two errors asymmetrically. The Type I
error is regarded as the more serious, so its probability is held below a fixed
small number $\alpha$; among the tests that do this we then look for one with the
greatest power on $\Omega_1$.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:size}
A test has \emph{level of significance} $\alpha$ if $\pi(\theta)\le\alpha$ for
every $\theta\in\Omega_0$. Its \emph{size} is
\[\sup_{\theta\in\Omega_0}\pi(\theta) ,\]
the largest probability of a Type I error. A test of size $\alpha$ has level
$\alpha$; a test of level $\alpha$ has size at most $\alpha$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:pvalue}
For a test that rejects $H_0$ when $t(x)\ge c$, the \emph{$p$-value} of an
observed sample $x$ is
\[p(x) = \sup_{\theta\in\Omega_0} P_\theta(T\ge t(x)) ,\]
the smallest size at which the observed data would lead to rejection. The test of
size $\alpha$ rejects $H_0$ exactly when $p(x)\le\alpha$.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:binomialsize}
A coin is tossed $10$ times and $X$, the number of heads, is recorded. To test
$H_0\colon p = 0.5$ against $H_1\colon p = 0.7$ we reject $H_0$ if $X\ge 8$. Find
the size of the test and the probability of a Type II error.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Under $H_0$, $X\sim\text{BIN}(10,0.5)$, so the size is
\[\pi(0.5) = P(X\ge 8) = \frac{\binom{10}{8} + \binom{10}{9} + \binom{10}{10}}{2^{10}}
= \frac{45 + 10 + 1}{1024} = 0.0547 .\]
Under $H_1$, $X\sim\text{BIN}(10,0.7)$ and the power is
\[\pi(0.7) = 45(0.7)^8(0.3)^2 + 10(0.7)^9(0.3) + (0.7)^{10}
= 0.2335 + 0.1211 + 0.0282 = 0.3828 .\]
The Type II error probability is $\beta = 1 - 0.3828 = 0.6172$. With only ten
tosses, a coin with $p = 0.7$ would go undetected more often than not.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:normalpower}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_{16}$ be a random sample from $N(\mu,16)$. To test
$H_0\colon\mu = 10$ against $H_1\colon\mu>10$ we reject $H_0$ if
$\bar x>11.645$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Find the power function and the size.
\item[(b)] Find the power at $\mu = 12$ and $\mu = 13$.
\item[(c)] Find the $p$-value if $\bar x = 12.1$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Here $\bar X\sim N(\mu,1)$ because $\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n} =
\frac{4}{4} = 1$. So
\[\pi(\mu) = P_\mu(\bar X>11.645) = P(Z>11.645 - \mu) = 1 - \Phi(11.645 - \mu).\]
The null space is the single point $\mu = 10$, so the size is
$\pi(10) = 1 - \Phi(1.645) = 0.05$.
\item[(b)] $\pi(12) = 1 - \Phi(-0.355) = 0.639$ and
$\pi(13) = 1 - \Phi(-1.355) = 0.912$.
\item[(c)] The observed statistic is $\bar x = 12.1$, so
$p = P_{10}(\bar X\ge 12.1) = P(Z\ge 2.1) = 0.0179$. This is below $0.05$, and
indeed $12.1>11.645$; $H_0$ is rejected at the $5\%$ level but not at the $1\%$
level.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\section{Most Powerful Tests}

When both hypotheses are simple, $H_0\colon\theta=\theta_0$ against
$H_1\colon\theta=\theta_1$, we are choosing between two completely specified
distributions, and the power function has just two values: the size
$\pi(\theta_0)$ and the power $\pi(\theta_1)$.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:mp}
A test with critical region $R^*$ is a \emph{most powerful} (MP) test of size
$\alpha$ of $H_0\colon\theta=\theta_0$ against $H_1\colon\theta=\theta_1$ if
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $P_{\theta_0}(X\in R^*) = \alpha$, and
\item[(ii)] $P_{\theta_1}(X\in R^*)\ge P_{\theta_1}(X\in R)$ for every other
critical region $R$ with $P_{\theta_0}(X\in R)\le\alpha$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

Write $L(\theta;x) = \prod_{i=1}^n f(x_i;\theta)$ for the likelihood. If $H_1$ is
true we expect $L(\theta_1;x)$ to be large compared with $L(\theta_0;x)$, so a
natural rule rejects $H_0$ when the likelihood ratio
$\frac{L(\theta_1;x)}{L(\theta_0;x)}$ is large. The Neyman--Pearson lemma says this
rule cannot be beaten.

\begin{lem}[Neyman--Pearson lemma]
\label{lem:np}
Let $X$ have joint density (or probability function) $f(x;\theta)$. For a
constant $k>0$ let
\[R = \left\{x : \frac{f(x;\theta_1)}{f(x;\theta_0)}>k\right\} ,\]
and let $\alpha = P_{\theta_0}(X\in R)$. Then $R$ is a most powerful critical
region of size $\alpha$ for testing $H_0\colon\theta=\theta_0$ against
$H_1\colon\theta=\theta_1$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Let $R_1$ be any critical region with $P_{\theta_0}(X\in R_1)\le\alpha$. We write
$f_0$ and $f_1$ for $f(x;\theta_0)$ and $f(x;\theta_1)$, and use integrals; for a
discrete distribution replace them by sums. Split each region into its part shared
with the other and its own part:
\[R = (R\cap R_1)\cup(R\cap R_1^c),\qquad R_1 = (R\cap R_1)\cup(R_1\cap R^c).\]
The shared part cancels in differences, so
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:npdiff1}
P_{\theta_1}(X\in R) - P_{\theta_1}(X\in R_1)
= \int_{R\cap R_1^c} f_1\,dx - \int_{R_1\cap R^c} f_1\,dx ,
\end{equation}
and in the same way
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:npdiff0}
\int_{R\cap R_1^c} f_0\,dx - \int_{R_1\cap R^c} f_0\,dx
= P_{\theta_0}(X\in R) - P_{\theta_0}(X\in R_1) \ge \alpha - \alpha = 0 .
\end{equation}
On $R\cap R_1^c$ we are inside $R$, so $f_1>k f_0$; on $R_1\cap R^c$ we are
outside $R$, so $f_1\le k f_0$. Hence
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:npbound}
\int_{R\cap R_1^c} f_1\,dx \ge k\int_{R\cap R_1^c} f_0\,dx,\qquad
\int_{R_1\cap R^c} f_1\,dx \le k\int_{R_1\cap R^c} f_0\,dx .
\end{equation}
Putting \eqref{eq:npbound} into \eqref{eq:npdiff1} and then using
\eqref{eq:npdiff0},
\[P_{\theta_1}(X\in R) - P_{\theta_1}(X\in R_1)
\ge k\left[\int_{R\cap R_1^c} f_0\,dx - \int_{R_1\cap R^c} f_0\,dx\right]\ge 0 .\]
So no region of size at most $\alpha$ has more power than $R$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\label{rem:npuse}
In practice the inequality $\frac{L(\theta_1;x)}{L(\theta_0;x)}>k$ is simplified,
by taking logarithms and collecting constants, until it reads $t(x)>c$ or
$t(x)<c$ for some statistic $T$. Each simplification replaces $k$ by a new
constant, and we only need to keep track of which direction the inequality points.
The constant $c$ is then found from the size condition
$P_{\theta_0}(X\in R) = \alpha$, using the distribution of $T$ under $H_0$.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mpnormal16}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\mu,16)$. Find the most powerful
test of size $\alpha = 0.05$ of $H_0\colon\mu = 10$ against $H_1\colon\mu = 12$.
How large must $n$ be for the power to reach $0.90$?
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The likelihood ratio is
\[\frac{L(12;x)}{L(10;x)}
= \frac{(32\pi)^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{32}\sum(x_i - 12)^2\right)}
{(32\pi)^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{32}\sum(x_i - 10)^2\right)}
= \exp\left(\frac{1}{32}\left[\sum(x_i - 10)^2 - \sum(x_i - 12)^2\right]\right).\]
Expanding the squares, $(x_i - 10)^2 - (x_i - 12)^2 = 4x_i - 44$, so the ratio is
\[\exp\left(\frac{4\sum x_i - 44n}{32}\right),\]
an increasing function of $\sum x_i$, and hence of $\bar x$. The ratio exceeds
$k$ exactly when $\bar x>c$ for some $c$, so $R = \{x : \bar x>c\}$. Under $H_0$,
$\bar X\sim N\left(10,\frac{16}{n}\right)$ and
\[0.05 = P_{10}(\bar X>c) = P\left(Z>\frac{c - 10}{4/\sqrt n}\right)
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad c = 10 + 1.645\,\frac{4}{\sqrt n} = 10 + \frac{6.58}{\sqrt n} .\]
The MP test rejects $H_0$ if $\bar x>10 + \frac{6.58}{\sqrt n}$.

The power is
\[\pi(12) = P_{12}(\bar X>c) = P\left(Z>\frac{c - 12}{4/\sqrt n}\right)
= P\left(Z>1.645 - \frac{\sqrt n}{2}\right).\]
For power $0.90$ we need $1.645 - \frac{\sqrt n}{2} = -1.2816$, so
$\sqrt n = 2(2.9266) = 5.853$ and $n = 34.3$. A sample of $n = 35$ is needed. For
$n = 16$, $c = 11.645$ and the power is $0.639$, as in
example~\ref{ex:normalpower}; figure~\ref{fig:powercurve} shows the whole power
function for $n = 16$.
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[declare function={Phi(\x)=1/(1+exp(-0.07056*\x*\x*\x-1.5976*\x));}]
\begin{axis}[
    width=10cm, height=6cm,
    xlabel={true mean $\mu$}, ylabel={power $\pi(\mu)$},
    ymin=0, ymax=1.05, xmin=8, xmax=16,
    axis lines=left, grid=major, grid style={draw=gray!25},
    xtick={8,10,12,14,16}, ytick={0.05,0.5,1},
    yticklabels={$0.05$,$0.5$,$1$}]
\addplot[very thick, blue, smooth, samples=120, domain=8:16] {1-Phi(11.645-x)};
\addplot[only marks, mark=*, red] coordinates {(10,0.05) (12,0.639)};
\node[anchor=west, font=\footnotesize] at (axis cs:10.1,0.13) {size $\alpha = 0.05$};
\node[anchor=west, font=\footnotesize] at (axis cs:12.1,0.58) {$\pi(12) = 0.639$};
\draw[dashed, gray] (axis cs:11.645,0) -- (axis cs:11.645,0.5) -- (axis cs:8,0.5);
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Power function of the test that rejects $H_0\colon\mu = 10$ when
$\bar x>11.645$, for samples of $16$ from $N(\mu,16)$. The power is $\alpha$ at
the null value and equals $\frac12$ at $\mu = c$.}
\label{fig:powercurve}
\end{figure}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mpnormalgeneral}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\mu,\sigma^2)$ with $\sigma^2$
known. Find the most powerful test of size $\alpha$ of $H_0\colon\mu = \mu_0$
against $H_1\colon\mu = \mu_1$, where $\mu_1>\mu_0$, and its power.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
As in example~\ref{ex:mpnormal16},
\[\frac{L(\mu_1;x)}{L(\mu_0;x)}
= \exp\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum\left[(x_i - \mu_0)^2 - (x_i - \mu_1)^2\right]\right)
= \exp\left(\frac{(\mu_1 - \mu_0)}{\sigma^2}\sum x_i - \frac{n(\mu_1^2 - \mu_0^2)}{2\sigma^2}\right).\]
Since $\mu_1 - \mu_0>0$ this increases with $\bar x$, so $R = \{x : \bar x>c\}$.
Writing $z_\alpha$ for the point with $P(Z>z_\alpha) = \alpha$, the size condition
gives
\[c = \mu_0 + z_\alpha\,\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n} ,\]
and the power is
\[\pi(\mu_1) = P\left(Z>\frac{c - \mu_1}{\sigma/\sqrt n}\right)
= 1 - \Phi\left(z_\alpha - \frac{(\mu_1 - \mu_0)\sqrt n}{\sigma}\right).\]
The power grows with the sample size $n$ and with the distance
$\frac{\mu_1 - \mu_0}{\sigma}$ measured in standard deviations.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mpvariance}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\mu,\sigma^2)$ with $\mu$ known.
Find the most powerful test of size $\alpha$ of $H_0\colon\sigma = \sigma_0$
against $H_1\colon\sigma = \sigma_1$, where $\sigma_1<\sigma_0$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Write $q = \sum_{i=1}^n (x_i - \mu)^2$. The likelihood ratio is
\[\frac{L(\sigma_1;x)}{L(\sigma_0;x)}
= \frac{(2\pi\sigma_1^2)^{-n/2} e^{-q/(2\sigma_1^2)}}{(2\pi\sigma_0^2)^{-n/2} e^{-q/(2\sigma_0^2)}}
= \left(\frac{\sigma_0}{\sigma_1}\right)^n
\exp\left(-\frac{q}{2}\left[\frac{1}{\sigma_1^2} - \frac{1}{\sigma_0^2}\right]\right).\]
Because $\sigma_1<\sigma_0$, the bracket is positive and the ratio decreases in
$q$. So the ratio exceeds $k$ exactly when $q<c$, and $R = \{x : q<c\}$: small
spread around $\mu$ is evidence for the smaller variance.

Under $H_0$, $\frac{Q}{\sigma_0^2} = \sum\left(\frac{X_i - \mu}{\sigma_0}\right)^2
\sim\chi^2(n)$ by corollary~\ref{cor:chisquaresample}. Writing $\chi^2_{n,\gamma}$
for the point with upper-tail probability $\gamma$,
\[\alpha = P_{\sigma_0}(Q<c) = P\left(\chi^2(n)<\frac{c}{\sigma_0^2}\right)
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad c = \sigma_0^2\,\chi^2_{n,1-\alpha} .\]
The MP test rejects $H_0$ if $\sum (x_i - \mu)^2<\sigma_0^2\,\chi^2_{n,1-\alpha}$.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:mpexponential}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from the exponential distribution
\[f(x;\theta) = \frac{1}{\theta}e^{-x/\theta},\qquad x>0,\ \theta>0 .\]
Find the most powerful test of size $\alpha$ of $H_0\colon\theta = \theta_0$
against $H_1\colon\theta = \theta_1$, where $\theta_1<\theta_0$. Give the test
and its power when $n = 10$, $\theta_0 = 2$, $\theta_1 = 1$ and $\alpha = 0.05$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Write $y = \sum x_i$. The likelihood ratio is
\[\frac{L(\theta_1;x)}{L(\theta_0;x)} = \frac{\theta_1^{-n}e^{-y/\theta_1}}{\theta_0^{-n}e^{-y/\theta_0}}
= \left(\frac{\theta_0}{\theta_1}\right)^n
\exp\left(-\left[\frac{1}{\theta_1} - \frac{1}{\theta_0}\right] y\right).\]
Since $\theta_1<\theta_0$ the bracket is positive and the ratio decreases in $y$,
so $R = \{x : \sum x_i<c\}$: short lifetimes point to the smaller mean.

To find $c$ we need the distribution of $Y = \sum X_i$. The sum of $n$ independent
$\text{EXP}(\theta)$ variables is $\text{GAM}(n,\theta)$, and then
$\frac{2Y}{\theta}\sim\text{GAM}(n,2) = \chi^2(2n)$. Hence
\[\alpha = P_{\theta_0}(Y<c) = P\left(\chi^2(2n)<\frac{2c}{\theta_0}\right)
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad c = \frac{\theta_0}{2}\,\chi^2_{2n,1-\alpha} .\]
The MP test rejects $H_0$ if $\sum x_i<\frac{\theta_0}{2}\chi^2_{2n,1-\alpha}$.

For $n = 10$, $\theta_0 = 2$ and $\alpha = 0.05$, $\chi^2_{20,0.95} = 10.851$, so
$c = 10.851$: reject $H_0$ if the ten lifetimes total less than $10.85$. Under
$\theta_1 = 1$, $2Y\sim\chi^2(20)$, so the power is
\[P_1(Y<10.851) = P\left(\chi^2(20)<21.70\right) = 0.643 .\]
\end{solution}

\subsubsection{Discrete distributions and randomised tests}

For a discrete statistic the size $P_{\theta_0}(T\ge c)$ jumps as $c$ moves, and it
may skip over the value $\alpha$ we want. The remedy is to allow the test to
reject with some probability $\gamma$ when $T$ falls exactly on the boundary. Such
a \emph{randomised test} is described by a function $\phi(x)\in[0,1]$, the
probability of rejecting $H_0$ when $x$ is observed; its power function is
$\pi(\theta) = E_\theta\left[\phi(X)\right]$. The Neyman--Pearson lemma holds for
randomised tests as well, with the boundary $\frac{f_1}{f_0} = k$ handled by
randomisation.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:randomised}
Let $X_1,X_2,X_3$ be a random sample from the Bernoulli distribution
$f(x;\theta) = \theta^x(1 - \theta)^{1 - x}$, $x = 0,1$. Find the most powerful
test of size $\alpha = 0.05$ of $H_0\colon\theta = \frac34$ against
$H_1\colon\theta = \frac14$, and its power.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
With $y = \sum x_i$,
\[\frac{L(\frac14;x)}{L(\frac34;x)}
= \frac{(\frac14)^y(\frac34)^{3 - y}}{(\frac34)^y(\frac14)^{3 - y}} = 3^{3 - 2y},\]
which decreases in $y$. So the MP test rejects for small $Y = \sum X_i$, and
$Y\sim\text{BIN}(3,\theta)$. Under $H_0$ the probabilities are
\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{c|cccc}
$y$ & 0 & 1 & 2 & 3\\
\hline
$P_{3/4}(Y = y)$ & $\frac{1}{64} = 0.0156$ & $\frac{9}{64} = 0.1406$ & $\frac{27}{64} = 0.4219$ & $\frac{27}{64} = 0.4219$
\end{tabular}
\end{table}
Rejecting only when $y = 0$ gives size $0.0156$; rejecting when $y\le 1$ gives
size $0.1562$. Neither is $0.05$. So reject when $y = 0$, and when $y = 1$ reject
with probability $\gamma$, chosen so that
\[\frac{1}{64} + \gamma\,\frac{9}{64} = 0.05
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad \gamma = \frac{0.05 - 0.015625}{0.140625} = 0.2444 .\]
The test is $\phi(x) = 1$ if $y = 0$, $\phi(x) = 0.2444$ if $y = 1$, and
$\phi(x) = 0$ otherwise. Under $\theta = \frac14$, $P(Y = 0) = P(Y = 1) =
\frac{27}{64}$, so the power is
\[\pi\left(\tfrac14\right) = \frac{27}{64} + 0.2444\cdot\frac{27}{64} = 0.525 .\]
In practice one usually prefers a non-randomised test of level $\alpha$, here the
test that rejects only when $y = 0$, and reports its actual size $0.0156$.
\end{solution}

\section{Uniformly Most Powerful Tests}

Most alternatives of interest are composite, for example $H_1\colon\theta>\theta_0$.
A test is then judged by its whole power function on $\Omega_1$.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:ump}
A test with critical region $R^*$ is a \emph{uniformly most powerful} (UMP) test
of size $\alpha$ of $H_0\colon\theta\in\Omega_0$ against
$H_1\colon\theta\in\Omega_1$ if
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $\sup_{\theta\in\Omega_0} P_\theta(X\in R^*) = \alpha$, and
\item[(ii)] $P_\theta(X\in R^*)\ge P_\theta(X\in R)$ for every
$\theta\in\Omega_1$ and every critical region $R$ of level $\alpha$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

So a UMP test is most powerful against every single alternative at once. The way
to find one is to fix an arbitrary $\theta_1\in\Omega_1$, construct the MP test of
$\theta_0$ against $\theta_1$, and see whether the critical region depends on
$\theta_1$. If it does not, the region is MP for every $\theta_1$, hence UMP.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:umpnormal}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\theta,1)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Find the MP test of size $0.05$ of $H_0\colon\theta = 0$ against
$H_1\colon\theta = \theta_1$, where $\theta_1>0$.
\item[(b)] Find the UMP test of size $0.05$ of $H_0\colon\theta = 0$ against
$H_1\colon\theta>0$.
\item[(c)] Find and sketch its power function.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] The likelihood ratio is
\[\frac{L(\theta_1;x)}{L(0;x)}
= \exp\left(\frac12\sum x_i^2 - \frac12\sum(x_i - \theta_1)^2\right)
= \exp\left(\theta_1\sum x_i - \frac{n\theta_1^2}{2}\right).\]
Since $\theta_1>0$ this increases in $\bar x$, so $R = \{x : \bar x>c\}$. Under
$H_0$, $\bar X\sim N(0,\frac1n)$, and $P_0(\bar X>c) = P(Z>c\sqrt n) = 0.05$
gives $c = \frac{1.645}{\sqrt n}$. The MP test rejects $H_0$ if
$\bar x>\frac{1.645}{\sqrt n}$.
\item[(b)] The region found in (a) used only the sign of $\theta_1$, not its
value. The same region is therefore MP against every $\theta_1>0$, and the test
that rejects when $\bar x>\frac{1.645}{\sqrt n}$ is UMP of size $0.05$ for
$H_1\colon\theta>0$.
\item[(c)] Since $\bar X\sim N(\theta,\frac1n)$,
\[\pi(\theta) = P_\theta\left(\bar X>\frac{1.645}{\sqrt n}\right)
= P\left(Z>1.645 - \theta\sqrt n\right) = 1 - \Phi(1.645 - \theta\sqrt n).\]
It equals $0.05$ at $\theta = 0$, increases with $\theta$, tends to $1$ as
$\theta\to\infty$ and to $0$ as $\theta\to-\infty$. For $n = 4$:
$\pi(0.25) = 0.126$, $\pi(0.5) = 0.260$, $\pi(1) = 0.639$. The curve is the solid
one in figure~\ref{fig:twosided}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:umpvariance}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_{20}$ be a random sample from $N(0,\sigma^2)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Derive the UMP test of size $0.05$ of $H_0\colon\sigma = \sigma_0$
against $H_1\colon\sigma<\sigma_0$.
\item[(b)] Find its power function and describe its shape.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Example~\ref{ex:mpvariance} with $\mu = 0$ and $n = 20$ shows that for
any $\sigma_1<\sigma_0$ the MP test rejects when $\sum x_i^2<c$ with
$c = \sigma_0^2\chi^2_{20,0.95} = 10.851\,\sigma_0^2$. The region depends only on
$\sigma_0$, not on $\sigma_1$, so it is UMP against $H_1\colon\sigma<\sigma_0$:
reject $H_0$ if $\sum_{i=1}^{20} x_i^2<10.851\,\sigma_0^2$.
\item[(b)] For any $\sigma$, $\sum\left(\frac{X_i}{\sigma}\right)^2\sim\chi^2(20)$,
so
\[\pi(\sigma) = P_\sigma\left(\sum X_i^2<10.851\,\sigma_0^2\right)
= P\left(\chi^2(20)<10.851\,\frac{\sigma_0^2}{\sigma^2}\right).\]
At $\sigma = \sigma_0$ this is $0.05$. As $\sigma$ decreases the ratio
$\frac{\sigma_0^2}{\sigma^2}$ grows and so does the power; for example
$\pi(0.8\sigma_0) = P(\chi^2(20)<16.95) = 0.344$ and
$\pi(0.5\sigma_0) = P(\chi^2(20)<43.40) = 0.998$. For $\sigma>\sigma_0$ the power
falls below $0.05$, for instance $\pi(1.2\sigma_0) = 0.005$. The power function
decreases in $\sigma$ from $1$ near $\sigma = 0$ towards $0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

A UMP test need not exist, and for two-sided alternatives it usually does not.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:noump}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\theta,1)$. To test
$H_0\colon\theta = 0$ against $H_1\colon\theta\ne 0$ consider the critical region
$R = \left\{x : |\bar x|>\frac{1.96}{\sqrt n}\right\}$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Find its power function.
\item[(b)] Is the test uniformly most powerful?
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Since $\sqrt n(\bar X - \theta)\sim N(0,1)$,
\[\pi(\theta) = P_\theta\left(\bar X>\frac{1.96}{\sqrt n}\right)
+ P_\theta\left(\bar X<-\frac{1.96}{\sqrt n}\right)
= 1 - \Phi(1.96 - \theta\sqrt n) + \Phi(-1.96 - \theta\sqrt n).\]
It is symmetric in $\theta$, equals $0.05$ at $\theta = 0$, and increases with
$|\theta|$. For $n = 1$, $\pi(\pm1) = 0.1685 + 0.0015 = 0.170$.
\item[(b)] By example~\ref{ex:umpnormal}, the MP test of size $0.05$ against any
$\theta_1>0$ rejects when $\bar x>\frac{1.645}{\sqrt n}$, and by the same
argument with the inequality reversed the MP test against any $\theta_1<0$
rejects when $\bar x<-\frac{1.645}{\sqrt n}$. A UMP test would have to be most
powerful against both kinds of alternative, so it would have to coincide with
both one-sided tests, which is impossible. Concretely, at $\theta = 1$ with
$n = 1$ the one-sided test has power $1 - \Phi(0.645) = 0.259$, more than the
$0.170$ of the two-sided test; at $\theta = -1$ the one-sided test has power
$0.004$. No test of size $0.05$ is UMP for $H_1\colon\theta\ne 0$; the two-sided
test is a sensible compromise, and it is UMP among \emph{unbiased} tests, those
with $\pi(\theta)\ge\alpha$ for all $\theta\in\Omega_1$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[declare function={Phi(\x)=1/(1+exp(-0.07056*\x*\x*\x-1.5976*\x));}]
\begin{axis}[
    width=10cm, height=6cm,
    xlabel={$\theta$}, ylabel={power},
    ymin=0, ymax=1.05, xmin=-2, xmax=2,
    axis lines=left, grid=major, grid style={draw=gray!25},
    ytick={0.05,0.5,1}, yticklabels={$0.05$,$0.5$,$1$},
    legend pos=north west, legend style={font=\footnotesize, draw=none, fill=none}]
\addplot[very thick, blue, smooth, samples=120, domain=-2:2] {1-Phi(1.645-2*x)};
\addplot[very thick, orange!80!black, dashed, smooth, samples=120, domain=-2:2] {1-Phi(1.96-2*x)+Phi(-1.96-2*x)};
\legend{one-sided test,two-sided test}
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Power functions for samples of $n = 4$ from $N(\theta,1)$. The one-sided
test is more powerful for $\theta>0$ but nearly useless for $\theta<0$; the
two-sided test gives up some power on the right to protect both sides.}
\label{fig:twosided}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Monotone likelihood ratio}

The examples above share a structure that makes a UMP test exist for one-sided
hypotheses.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:mlr}
A family of joint densities $f(x;\theta)$, $\theta\in\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}$, has
a \emph{monotone likelihood ratio} (MLR) in the statistic $T = t(X)$ if for every
$\theta_1<\theta_2$ the ratio
\[\frac{f(x;\theta_2)}{f(x;\theta_1)}\]
depends on $x$ only through $t(x)$ and is a nondecreasing function of $t(x)$.
\end{definition}

If the ratio is nonincreasing in $t(x)$ instead, the family has MLR in $-T$, so the
definition covers both directions.

\begin{thm}[Karlin--Rubin]
\label{thm:karlinrubin}
Suppose the family $f(x;\theta)$ has MLR in $T = t(X)$, and let $c$ satisfy
$P_{\theta_0}(T\ge c) = \alpha$. Then the test that rejects $H_0$ when $t(x)\ge c$
is a UMP test of size $\alpha$ of $H_0\colon\theta\le\theta_0$ against
$H_1\colon\theta>\theta_0$ (and also of $H_0\colon\theta = \theta_0$ against
$H_1\colon\theta>\theta_0$).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\theta_1>\theta_0$. By MLR,
$\frac{f(x;\theta_1)}{f(x;\theta_0)} = g(t(x))$ with $g$ nondecreasing. Let
$k = g(c)$. If $g(t(x))>k$ then $t(x)>c$; conversely a sample with $t(x)\ge c$ has
$g(t(x))\ge k$. So the region $\{t(x)\ge c\}$ agrees with the Neyman--Pearson region
of lemma~\ref{lem:np} except possibly on the boundary $\{g = k\}$, which does not
affect the argument of its proof (there $f_1 = k f_0$ and the bound
\eqref{eq:npbound} holds with equality). Hence $\{t(x)\ge c\}$ is MP of size
$\alpha$ for $\theta_0$ against $\theta_1$. It does not depend on $\theta_1$, so it
is UMP against $H_1\colon\theta>\theta_0$.

For the composite null $\theta\le\theta_0$ it remains to show that the size is
still $\alpha$, that is, $\pi(\theta)\le\alpha$ for $\theta<\theta_0$. Apply the
first part with $\theta$ in place of $\theta_0$ and $\theta_0$ in place of
$\theta_1$: the region $\{t\ge c\}$ is MP of its own size $\pi(\theta)$ for $\theta$
against $\theta_0$. The trivial test that rejects with probability $\pi(\theta)$
regardless of the data has the same size and power $\pi(\theta)$ at $\theta_0$, so
the MP test has $\pi(\theta_0)\ge\pi(\theta)$, that is, $\alpha\ge\pi(\theta)$. The
same argument shows that $\pi$ is nondecreasing.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\label{rem:mlrdirections}
The other one-sided cases follow by symmetry.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If the MLR is nondecreasing in $t(x)$, the UMP test of
$H_0\colon\theta\ge\theta_0$ against $H_1\colon\theta<\theta_0$ rejects when
$t(x)\le c$.
\item If the MLR is nonincreasing in $t(x)$, the UMP test of
$H_0\colon\theta\le\theta_0$ against $H_1\colon\theta>\theta_0$ rejects when
$t(x)\le c$, and the test against $\theta<\theta_0$ rejects when $t(x)\ge c$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{coro}
\label{cor:expfamilymlr}
Suppose $f(x;\theta) = c(\theta)h(x)\exp(w(\theta)u(x))$ is a
one-parameter exponential family with $w$ increasing. Then the joint density of a
random sample has MLR in $T = \sum_{i=1}^n u(X_i)$.
\end{coro}

\begin{proof}
For $\theta_1<\theta_2$,
\[\frac{\prod f(x_i;\theta_2)}{\prod f(x_i;\theta_1)}
= \left(\frac{c(\theta_2)}{c(\theta_1)}\right)^n
\exp\left([w(\theta_2) - w(\theta_1)]\sum u(x_i)\right),\]
which depends on $x$ through $\sum u(x_i)$ and is increasing in it because
$w(\theta_2)>w(\theta_1)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:umpbernoulli}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from $f(x;\theta) = \theta^x(1-\theta)^{1-x}$,
$x = 0,1$, $0<\theta<1$. Find a UMP test of size $\alpha$ of
$H_0\colon\theta\le\theta_0$ against $H_1\colon\theta>\theta_0$. For $n = 20$ and
$\theta_0 = 0.2$, find a test of level $0.05$, its actual size, and its power at
$\theta = 0.4$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
With $y = \sum x_i$, the joint probability function is
$\theta^y(1-\theta)^{n-y}$, and for $\theta_1<\theta_2$
\[\frac{\theta_2^y(1-\theta_2)^{n-y}}{\theta_1^y(1-\theta_1)^{n-y}}
= \left(\frac{\theta_2(1-\theta_1)}{\theta_1(1-\theta_2)}\right)^y
\left(\frac{1-\theta_2}{1-\theta_1}\right)^n .\]
Since $\theta_2>\theta_1$ and $1-\theta_1>1-\theta_2$, the base
$\frac{\theta_2(1-\theta_1)}{\theta_1(1-\theta_2)}$ exceeds $1$, so the ratio is
increasing in $y$: the family has MLR in $Y = \sum X_i$. (Equivalently, the Bernoulli
is an exponential family with $w(\theta) = \ln\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}$, which is
increasing.) By theorem~\ref{thm:karlinrubin} the UMP test rejects $H_0$ when
$y\ge k$, where $Y\sim\text{BIN}(n,\theta_0)$ and
\[P_{\theta_0}(Y\ge k) = \sum_{y=k}^n\binom ny\theta_0^y(1-\theta_0)^{n-y} = \alpha ,\]
with randomisation at $y = k-1$ if no integer $k$ gives exactly $\alpha$.

For $n = 20$ and $\theta_0 = 0.2$, the upper tail probabilities are
$P(Y\ge 7) = 0.0867$ and $P(Y\ge 8) = 0.0321$. The non-randomised test of level
$0.05$ rejects when $y\ge 8$; its actual size is $0.032$. Its power function is
$\pi(\theta) = P_\theta(Y\ge 8)$, which increases from $\pi(0.2) = 0.032$ to
$\pi(0.4) = 0.584$ and on to $1$ as $\theta\to1$.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:umpexponential}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from $f(x;\theta) = \frac1\theta e^{-x/\theta}$,
$x>0$. Find a UMP test of size $\alpha$ of $H_0\colon\theta\ge\theta_0$ against
$H_1\colon\theta<\theta_0$, and its power function.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
For $\theta_1<\theta_2$,
\[\frac{f(x;\theta_2)}{f(x;\theta_1)}
= \frac{\theta_2^{-n}e^{-\sum x_i/\theta_2}}{\theta_1^{-n}e^{-\sum x_i/\theta_1}}
= \left(\frac{\theta_1}{\theta_2}\right)^n
\exp\left(\left[\frac{1}{\theta_1} - \frac{1}{\theta_2}\right]\sum x_i\right),\]
and the bracket is positive, so the family has a nondecreasing MLR in
$T = \sum X_i$. By remark~\ref{rem:mlrdirections}(1) the UMP test of
$H_0\colon\theta\ge\theta_0$ against $H_1\colon\theta<\theta_0$ rejects when
$\sum x_i\le c$. As in example~\ref{ex:mpexponential}, $\frac{2T}{\theta}\sim\chi^2(2n)$,
so
\[c = \frac{\theta_0}{2}\,\chi^2_{2n,1-\alpha},\qquad
\pi(\theta) = P_\theta(T\le c) = P\left(\chi^2(2n)\le\frac{\theta_0}{\theta}\,\chi^2_{2n,1-\alpha}\right).\]
The power is $\alpha$ at $\theta = \theta_0$, below $\alpha$ for
$\theta>\theta_0$, and rises to $1$ as $\theta\to0$. For $n = 10$, $\theta_0 = 2$
and $\alpha = 0.05$ the test rejects when $\sum x_i\le 10.85$ and, as computed in
example~\ref{ex:mpexponential}, $\pi(1) = 0.643$.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:umppoisson}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_{10}$ be a random sample from the Poisson distribution with mean
$\lambda$. Find a UMP test of level $0.05$ of $H_0\colon\lambda\le 1$ against
$H_1\colon\lambda>1$, its size, and its power at $\lambda = 1.5$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The Poisson probability function is
$e^{-\lambda}\frac{1}{x!}\exp(x\ln\lambda)$, an exponential family with
$w(\lambda) = \ln\lambda$ increasing. By corollary~\ref{cor:expfamilymlr} and
theorem~\ref{thm:karlinrubin} the UMP test rejects when $Y = \sum X_i\ge k$. Under
$\lambda = 1$, $Y\sim$ Poisson$(10)$ (example~\ref{ex:poissonsum}), with
$P(Y\ge 15) = 0.0835$ and $P(Y\ge 16) = 0.0487$. So the test rejects $H_0$ when
$\sum x_i\ge 16$; its size is $0.049$. At $\lambda = 1.5$, $Y\sim$ Poisson$(15)$ and
the power is $P(Y\ge 16) = 0.432$.
\end{solution}

\section{Generalised Likelihood Ratio Tests}

When $H_0$ is composite, or the alternative is two-sided, or there are nuisance
parameters, a UMP test usually does not exist. A general method that always gives
a sensible test replaces each likelihood in the Neyman--Pearson ratio by its
largest value under the hypothesis concerned.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:glrt}
The \emph{generalised likelihood ratio} for testing $H_0\colon\theta\in\Omega_0$
against $H_1\colon\theta\in\Omega - \Omega_0$ is
\[\Lambda(x) = \frac{\sup_{\theta\in\Omega} L(\theta;x)}{\sup_{\theta\in\Omega_0} L(\theta;x)}
= \frac{L(\hat\theta;x)}{L(\hat\theta_0;x)} ,\]
where $\hat\theta$ is the maximum likelihood estimate over the whole parameter
space and $\hat\theta_0$ is the maximum likelihood estimate under the restriction
$\theta\in\Omega_0$. The \emph{generalised likelihood ratio test} (GLRT) rejects
$H_0$ when $\Lambda(x)>k$, with $k$ chosen so that
$\sup_{\theta\in\Omega_0} P_\theta(\Lambda(X)>k) = \alpha$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
\label{rem:glrt}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Since $\Omega_0\subset\Omega$, the numerator is at least the denominator,
so $\Lambda(x)\ge 1$, and large values are evidence against $H_0$.
\item As with the Neyman--Pearson ratio, the inequality $\Lambda(x)>k$ is
simplified to a statement about a familiar statistic. When the exact distribution
of $\Lambda(X)$ is out of reach, theorem~\ref{thm:wilks} gives an approximation.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:glrtztest}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\mu,\sigma^2)$ with $\sigma^2$
known. Find the GLRT of size $\alpha$ of $H_0\colon\mu = \mu_0$ against
$H_1\colon\mu\ne\mu_0$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Here $\Omega = \mathbb{R}$ and $\Omega_0 = \{\mu_0\}$. The unrestricted MLE is
$\hat\mu = \bar x$ and the restricted one is $\mu_0$, so
\[\Lambda(x) = \frac{(2\pi\sigma^2)^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2\right)}
{(2\pi\sigma^2)^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum(x_i - \mu_0)^2\right)}
= \exp\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\left[\sum(x_i - \mu_0)^2 - \sum(x_i - \bar x)^2\right]\right).\]
Writing $x_i - \mu_0 = (x_i - \bar x) + (\bar x - \mu_0)$ and using
$\sum(x_i - \bar x) = 0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:sumsquaressplit}
\sum(x_i - \mu_0)^2 = \sum(x_i - \bar x)^2 + n(\bar x - \mu_0)^2 .
\end{equation}
So
\[\Lambda(x) = \exp\left(\frac{n(\bar x - \mu_0)^2}{2\sigma^2}\right)
= \exp\left(\frac{z^2}{2}\right),\qquad z = \frac{\bar x - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt n} .\]
$\Lambda>k$ is equivalent to $|z|>c$. Under $H_0$, $Z\sim N(0,1)$, so
$c = z_{\alpha/2}$ and the GLRT is the familiar two-sided $z$ test: reject $H_0$ if
\[\left|\frac{\bar x - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt n}\right|>z_{\alpha/2} .\]
Note that $2\ln\Lambda = z^2\sim\chi^2(1)$ exactly under $H_0$.
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:glrtttest}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ be a random sample from $N(\mu,\sigma^2)$ with both parameters
unknown, and consider $H_0\colon\mu = 0$ against $H_1\colon\mu\ne 0$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Show that the GLRT rejects $H_0$ when $\frac{n\bar x^2}{s^2}>c$, where
$s^2 = \frac{1}{n-1}\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2$.
\item[(b)] Show that $T = \frac{n\bar X^2}{S^2}\sim F(1,n-1)$ under $H_0$, and
find the test of size $0.05$ for $n = 20$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Here $\theta = (\mu,\sigma^2)$, $\Omega = \{(\mu,\sigma^2) : \mu\in\mathbb{R},
\sigma^2>0\}$ and $\Omega_0 = \{(0,\sigma^2) : \sigma^2>0\}$. The log likelihood is
\[l(\mu,\sigma^2) = -\frac n2\ln(2\pi\sigma^2) - \frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum(x_i - \mu)^2 .\]
Over $\Omega$ the MLEs are $\hat\mu = \bar x$ and
$\hat\sigma^2 = \frac1n\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2$ (example~\ref{ex:normalfull}).
Substituting, the exponent becomes $-\frac{n\hat\sigma^2}{2\hat\sigma^2} = -\frac n2$,
so
\[L(\hat\theta;x) = (2\pi\hat\sigma^2)^{-n/2}e^{-n/2} .\]
Under $H_0$, $\mu = 0$ and the score equation
$\frac{\partial l}{\partial\sigma^2} = -\frac{n}{2\sigma^2} + \frac{1}{2\sigma^4}\sum x_i^2 = 0$
gives $\hat\sigma_0^2 = \frac1n\sum x_i^2$, and in the same way
\[L(\hat\theta_0;x) = (2\pi\hat\sigma_0^2)^{-n/2}e^{-n/2} .\]
Hence, using \eqref{eq:sumsquaressplit} with $\mu_0 = 0$,
\begin{align*}
\Lambda(x) &= \left(\frac{\hat\sigma_0^2}{\hat\sigma^2}\right)^{n/2}
= \left(\frac{\sum x_i^2}{\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2}\right)^{n/2}\\
&= \left(1 + \frac{n\bar x^2}{\sum(x_i - \bar x)^2}\right)^{n/2}
= \left(1 + \frac{1}{n-1}\cdot\frac{n\bar x^2}{s^2}\right)^{n/2} .
\end{align*}
This is an increasing function of $\frac{n\bar x^2}{s^2}$, so $\Lambda>k$ is
equivalent to $\frac{n\bar x^2}{s^2}>c$.
\item[(b)] Write
\[T = \frac{n\bar X^2}{S^2} = \frac{\left(\frac{\bar X}{\sigma/\sqrt n}\right)^2/1}
{\frac{(n-1)S^2}{\sigma^2}\Big/(n-1)} .\]
Under $H_0$, $\frac{\bar X}{\sigma/\sqrt n}\sim N(0,1)$, so its square is
$\chi^2(1)$; $\frac{(n-1)S^2}{\sigma^2}\sim\chi^2(n-1)$; and $\bar X$ and $S^2$
are independent. By definition~\ref{def:fdist}, $T\sim F(1,n-1)$. For
$n = 20$ and size $0.05$,
\[P(F(1,19)>c) = 0.05\quad\Longrightarrow\quad c = 4.38 ,\]
so the test rejects $H_0$ if $\frac{20\bar x^2}{s^2}>4.38$. Since
$T = \left(\frac{\bar X}{S/\sqrt n}\right)^2$ is the square of the one-sample $t$
statistic and $t_{19,0.025}^2 = 2.093^2 = 4.38$, this is the two-sided $t$ test.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

The same distributional facts give the variance of $S^2$, which is needed when
comparing estimators of $\sigma^2$.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:vars2}
For a random sample from $N(\mu,\sigma^2)$, find $\operatorname{Var}(S^2)$.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
Since $W = \frac{(n-1)S^2}{\sigma^2}\sim\chi^2(n-1)$ has variance $2(n-1)$,
\[\operatorname{Var}(S^2) = \operatorname{Var}\left(\frac{\sigma^2}{n-1}W\right)
= \frac{\sigma^4}{(n-1)^2}\cdot 2(n-1) = \frac{2\sigma^4}{n-1} .\]
\end{solution}

\begin{example}
\label{ex:glrttwoexp}
Let $X_1,\dots,X_n$ and $Y_1,\dots,Y_m$ be independent random samples from the
exponential distributions with means $\theta$ and $\mu$:
\[f(x;\theta) = \frac1\theta e^{-x/\theta},\ x>0,\qquad
f(y;\mu) = \frac1\mu e^{-y/\mu},\ y>0 .\]
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Show that the generalised likelihood ratio for testing
$H_0\colon\theta = \mu$ against $H_1\colon\theta\ne\mu$ is a function of
\[T = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n X_i}{\sum_{i=1}^n X_i + \sum_{j=1}^m Y_j} .\]
\item[(b)] Find the distribution of $T$ under $H_0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Write $s_x = \sum x_i = n\bar x$, $s_y = \sum y_j = m\bar y$ and
$s = s_x + s_y$. The log likelihood is
\[l(\theta,\mu) = -n\ln\theta - \frac{s_x}{\theta} - m\ln\mu - \frac{s_y}{\mu} .\]
Setting $\frac{\partial l}{\partial\theta} = -\frac n\theta + \frac{s_x}{\theta^2}$
and $\frac{\partial l}{\partial\mu}$ to zero gives $\hat\theta = \bar x$ and
$\hat\mu = \bar y$, and then
\[L(\hat\theta,\hat\mu) = \bar x^{-n}\bar y^{-m}e^{-(n+m)} .\]
Under $H_0$ the two samples share the mean $\theta$, $l(\theta) =
-(n+m)\ln\theta - \frac{s}{\theta}$, the restricted MLE is
$\hat\theta_0 = \frac{s}{n+m}$, and
\[L(\hat\theta_0,\hat\theta_0) = \left(\frac{s}{n+m}\right)^{-(n+m)}e^{-(n+m)} .\]
Hence
\[\Lambda = \frac{\left(\frac{s}{n+m}\right)^{n+m}}{\bar x^n\bar y^m} .\]
With $t = \frac{s_x}{s}$ we have $\bar x = \frac{ts}{n}$ and
$\bar y = \frac{(1-t)s}{m}$, so the powers of $s$ cancel and
\[\Lambda = \frac{n^n m^m}{(n+m)^{n+m}}\cdot\frac{1}{t^n(1-t)^m} .\]
This depends on the data only through $t$. The GLRT rejects $H_0$ when
$t^n(1-t)^m<c$, that is, when $t$ is too close to $0$ or too close to $1$: one
sample has a much larger total than the other would suggest.
\item[(b)] Under $H_0$, divide numerator and denominator by $\theta$:
$T = \frac{U}{U+V}$ with $U = \frac{\sum X_i}{\theta}$ and
$V = \frac{\sum Y_j}{\theta}$. Each $\frac{X_i}{\theta}\sim\text{EXP}(1)$ with
m.g.f. $(1-u)^{-1}$, so $U$ has m.g.f. $(1-u)^{-n}$ and $U\sim\text{GAM}(n,1)$;
likewise $V\sim\text{GAM}(m,1)$, independent of $U$. By
example~\ref{ex:gammabeta}, $T = \frac{U}{U+V}\sim\text{BETA}(n,m)$. The critical
values are therefore beta quantiles, chosen so that the two tails of
$t^n(1-t)^m<c$ have total probability $\alpha$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{solution}

\subsubsection{The large-sample distribution of the likelihood ratio}

In the examples above the GLRT reduced to a statistic with a known distribution.
When it does not, the following result of Wilks gives an approximation.

\begin{thm}[Wilks]
\label{thm:wilks}
Suppose the regularity conditions for the asymptotic normality of maximum
likelihood estimators hold, $\Omega$ is an open subset of $\mathbb{R}^p$, and
$\Omega_0$ is defined by fixing $r$ of the parameters (or, more generally, by $r$
independent smooth restrictions). Then under $H_0$,
\[2\ln\Lambda(X)\ \xrightarrow{d}\ \chi^2(r)\qquad\text{as } n\to\infty .\]
The approximate GLRT of size $\alpha$ rejects $H_0$ when
$2\ln\Lambda(x)>\chi^2_{r,\alpha}$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}[Sketch for one parameter]
Take $p = r = 1$ and $\Omega_0 = \{\theta_0\}$, so $2\ln\Lambda = 2\left[l(\hat\theta) -
l(\theta_0)\right]$. Expand $l(\theta_0)$ about $\hat\theta$, where $l'(\hat\theta) = 0$:
\[l(\theta_0) = l(\hat\theta) + \frac12 l''(\theta^*)(\theta_0 - \hat\theta)^2\]
for some $\theta^*$ between $\theta_0$ and $\hat\theta$. So
\[2\ln\Lambda = -l''(\theta^*)(\hat\theta - \theta_0)^2
= \frac{-l''(\theta^*)}{nI(\theta_0)}\left[\sqrt{nI(\theta_0)}\,(\hat\theta - \theta_0)\right]^2 ,\]
where $I(\theta)$ is the Fisher information in one observation. Under $H_0$,
$\hat\theta\to\theta_0$, the law of large numbers gives
$-\frac1n l''(\theta^*)\to I(\theta_0)$, and
$\sqrt{nI(\theta_0)}(\hat\theta - \theta_0)\xrightarrow{d}N(0,1)$. So the first
factor tends to $1$ and the second to the square of a standard normal, which is
$\chi^2(1)$.
\end{proof}

The degrees of freedom $r$ are the number of free parameters under $H_1$ minus the
number under $H_0$. In example~\ref{ex:glrtztest}, $r = 1 - 0 = 1$ and
$2\ln\Lambda = z^2$ is exactly $\chi^2(1)$; in example~\ref{ex:glrtttest}, $r = 2 -
1 = 1$ and $2\ln\Lambda = n\ln\left(1 + \frac{t^2}{n-1}\right)\approx t^2$, again
$\chi^2(1)$ for large $n$.

\begin{example}
\label{ex:wilkspoisson}
The numbers of calls arriving at a switchboard in $50$ one-minute intervals have
mean $\bar x = 2.4$. Assuming a Poisson$(\lambda)$ model, test
$H_0\colon\lambda = 2$ against $H_1\colon\lambda\ne2$ at the $5\%$ level with the
GLRT and its $\chi^2$ approximation.
\end{example}

\begin{solution}
The log likelihood is $l(\lambda) = n\bar x\ln\lambda - n\lambda - \sum\ln x_i!$,
with unrestricted MLE $\hat\lambda = \bar x$. So
\[2\ln\Lambda = 2\left[l(\bar x) - l(\lambda_0)\right]
= 2n\left[\bar x\ln\frac{\bar x}{\lambda_0} - (\bar x - \lambda_0)\right].\]
With $n = 50$, $\bar x = 2.4$ and $\lambda_0 = 2$,
\[2\ln\Lambda = 100\left[2.4\ln 1.2 - 0.4\right] = 100\left[0.43757 - 0.4\right] = 3.757 .\]
Here $r = 1$ and $\chi^2_{1,0.05} = 3.841$. Since $3.757<3.841$ we do not reject
$H_0$; the approximate $p$-value is $P(\chi^2(1)>3.757) = 0.053$.

For comparison, the Wald statistic $\frac{\bar x - \lambda_0}{\sqrt{\lambda_0/n}} =
\frac{0.4}{0.2} = 2.0$ has square $4.0>3.841$ and would just reject. The two
statistics agree to first order in large samples, but at the edge of the critical
region they can lead to different decisions; the likelihood ratio uses the shape
of the whole log likelihood and is usually preferred.
\end{solution}

\subsection*{Further reading}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Neyman, J., and Pearson, E. S. (1928), On the use and interpretation of
certain test criteria for purposes of statistical inference, \emph{Biometrika},
20A, 175--240 and 263--294.
\item Neyman, J., and Pearson, E. S. (1933), On the problem of the most efficient
tests of statistical hypotheses, \emph{Philosophical Transactions of the Royal
Society A}, 231, 289--337.
\item Wilks, S. S. (1938), The large-sample distribution of the likelihood ratio
for testing composite hypotheses, \emph{Annals of Mathematical Statistics}, 9,
60--62.
\item Karlin, S., and Rubin, H. (1956), The theory of decision procedures for
distributions with monotone likelihood ratio, \emph{Annals of Mathematical
Statistics}, 27, 272--299.
\item Lehmann, E. L., and Romano, J. P. (2005), \emph{Testing Statistical
Hypotheses}, 3rd edition, Springer, chapters 3 and 12.
\end{enumerate}


\section{Practice problems}

Problems on hypothesis testing. Work each one before opening the
solution.

\begin{problem}
Consider a distribution with p.d.f. $f(x;\theta)=\theta x^{\theta-1}$, $0<x<1$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Based on a random sample of size $n=1$, find the most powerful test of
$H_0:\theta=1$ against $H_1:\theta=2$ with $\alpha=0.05$.
\item[(b).] Compute the power of the test in (a) for the alternative $\theta=2$.
\item[(c).] Derive the most powerful test for the hypotheses of (a) based on a
random sample of size $n$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a).} Under $H_0$ the density is $f_0(x)=1$ --- the uniform on $(0,1)$ ---
and under $H_1$ it is $f_1(x)=2x$. By the Neyman--Pearson lemma the most powerful
test rejects when the ratio is small:
\[\frac{f_0(x)}{f_1(x)}=\frac{1}{2x}\leq k.\]
Since $\frac{1}{2x}$ \emph{decreases} in $x$, this is equivalent to $x\geq c$ for
some $c$. Fixing the size,
\[\alpha=P(X\geq c\mid\theta=1)=\int_c^1 1\,dx=1-c=0.05
\quad\implies\quad c=0.95.\]
\[\therefore\quad \text{reject } H_0 \text{ when } X\geq 0.95.\]

\textbf{(b).} The power is the same probability computed under the alternative:
\[\text{power}=P(X\geq 0.95\mid\theta=2)=\int_{0.95}^1 2x\,dx
=\left[x^2\right]_{0.95}^1=1-0.9025=0.0975.\]

\begin{note}
A power of $0.0975$ against a size of $0.05$ is dreadful --- barely better than
tossing a coin weighted $1:19$. That is not a flaw in the test; it is the most
powerful test there is at this size. One observation simply cannot distinguish
$\theta=1$ from $\theta=2$, which is exactly why part (c) asks for $n$.
\end{note}

\textbf{(c).} For a sample of size $n$,
\[\frac{L(1)}{L(2)}=\frac{1}{2^n\prod x_i}\leq k
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \prod_{i=1}^n x_i\geq c.\]
Taking logarithms and reversing the inequality (the logarithms are negative),
\[-\sum_{i=1}^n\ln x_i\leq c'.\]
Under $H_0$, $X_i\sim\text{UNIF}(0,1)$, so $-\ln X_i\sim\text{EXP}(1)$ and
\[-\sum_{i=1}^n\ln X_i\sim\text{GAM}(n,1),\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad
-2\sum_{i=1}^n\ln X_i\sim\chi^2(2n).\]
\[\therefore\quad\text{reject } H_0 \text{ when }
-2\sum_{i=1}^n\ln X_i\leq\chi^2_{1-\alpha}(2n),\]
the lower tail, because large values of $\prod x_i$ favour the alternative.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Suppose that $X\sim\text{POI}(\theta)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Derive the size $\alpha$ MP test of $H_0:\theta=\theta_0$ versus
$H_1:\theta=\theta_1$ $(\theta_1>\theta_0)$ based on an observed value of $X$.
\item[(b).] Rework (a) based on a random sample of size $n$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a).} The Neyman--Pearson ratio is
\[\frac{f_0(x)}{f_1(x)}
=\frac{e^{-\theta_0}\theta_0^x/x!}{e^{-\theta_1}\theta_1^x/x!}
=e^{\theta_1-\theta_0}\left(\frac{\theta_0}{\theta_1}\right)^x.\]
Because $\theta_1>\theta_0$ the base $\frac{\theta_0}{\theta_1}$ is less than $1$,
so the ratio decreases in $x$ and ``ratio small'' means ``$x$ large''. The test is
\[\text{reject } H_0 \text{ when } X\geq c,\qquad\text{where }
P\left(X\geq c\mid\theta_0\right)=\alpha.\]

\textbf{(b).} For a sample, $\sum X_i$ is sufficient and
$\sum_{i=1}^nX_i\sim\text{POI}(n\theta)$. The ratio becomes
\[\frac{L(\theta_0)}{L(\theta_1)}=e^{n(\theta_1-\theta_0)}
\left(\frac{\theta_0}{\theta_1}\right)^{\sum x_i},\]
decreasing in $\sum x_i$ by the same argument, so
\[\text{reject } H_0 \text{ when } \sum_{i=1}^nX_i\geq c,\qquad
P\left(\sum X_i\geq c\ |\ n\theta_0\right)=\alpha.\]

\begin{note}
Because $X$ is discrete, a $c$ giving \emph{exactly} size $\alpha$ usually does not
exist --- the attainable sizes are the jumps of the distribution. In practice one
takes the smallest $c$ with $P(X\geq c\mid\theta_0)\leq\alpha$, giving a
conservative test, or randomises at the boundary to hit $\alpha$ exactly.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X\sim NB(k,0.5)$ with $f(x)=\binom{x-1}{k-1}0.5^k0.5^{x-k}$,
$x=k,k+1,\ldots$
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] Derive the MP test of size $\alpha=0.125$ of $H_0:k=1$ against
$H_1:k=2$ based on an observed value of $X$.
\item[(b).] Compute the power of the test in (a) for the alternative $k=2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Note first that $0.5^k0.5^{x-k}=0.5^x$ whatever $k$ is, so
\[f(x)=\binom{x-1}{k-1}(0.5)^x.\]
Under $H_0$ $(k=1)$: $f_0(x)=(0.5)^x$ for $x=1,2,\ldots$
Under $H_1$ $(k=2)$: $f_1(x)=(x-1)(0.5)^x$ for $x=2,3,\ldots$

\textbf{(a).} The ratio is
\[\frac{f_0(x)}{f_1(x)}=\frac{1}{x-1},\]
which decreases in $x$, so the most powerful test rejects for large $X$. For the
size, the $H_0$ tail sums as a geometric series:
\[P(X\geq c\mid k=1)=\sum_{x=c}^{\infty}(0.5)^x=(0.5)^{c-1}.\]
Setting this equal to $0.125=(0.5)^3$ gives $c-1=3$, so
\[\boxed{\ \text{reject } H_0 \text{ when } X\geq 4\ }\]
and the size is exactly $0.125$ --- one of the rare discrete cases where $\alpha$
is attained on the nose.

\textbf{(b).} The power is
\[P(X\geq 4\mid k=2)=1-P(X=2)-P(X=3)
=1-(1)(0.5)^2-(2)(0.5)^3=1-0.25-0.25=0.5.\]

\begin{note}
The size came out exact because $0.125$ is itself a power of $\frac12$. Ask for
$\alpha=0.10$ instead and no critical value gives it: the choices are $0.125$ at
$c=4$ and $0.0625$ at $c=5$, and nothing in between.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Consider a random sample of size $n$ from a distribution with pdf
\[f(x;\theta)=\frac{3x^2}{\theta}e^{-\frac{x^3}{\theta}},\qquad x>0.\]
Derive the form of the critical region for a uniformly most powerful test of size
$\alpha$ of $H_0:\theta=\theta_0$ versus $H_1:\theta>\theta_0$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
For any $\theta_1>\theta_0$,
\[\frac{L(\theta_1)}{L(\theta_0)}
=\left(\frac{\theta_0}{\theta_1}\right)^n
\exp\!\left[-\sum x_i^3\left(\frac{1}{\theta_1}-\frac{1}{\theta_0}\right)\right]
=\left(\frac{\theta_0}{\theta_1}\right)^n
\exp\!\left[\sum x_i^3\left(\frac{1}{\theta_0}-\frac{1}{\theta_1}\right)\right].\]
Since $\theta_1>\theta_0$ we have $\frac{1}{\theta_0}-\frac{1}{\theta_1}>0$, so the
ratio is an \emph{increasing} function of
\[T=\sum_{i=1}^nX_i^3,\]
and it depends on the sample only through $T$. The family therefore has a monotone
likelihood ratio in $T$, and the critical region is free of $\theta_1$ --- which is
what makes one test uniformly most powerful for the whole composite alternative:
\[\text{reject } H_0 \text{ when } T=\sum_{i=1}^nX_i^3\geq c.\]

To find $c$, substitute $Y=X^3$. Then $Y\sim\text{EXP}(\text{mean }\theta)$, so
under $H_0$
\[T\sim\text{GAM}(n,\theta_0)\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\frac{2T}{\theta_0}\sim\chi^2(2n).\]
\[\therefore\quad\text{reject } H_0 \text{ when }
\frac{2}{\theta_0}\sum_{i=1}^nX_i^3\geq\chi^2_{\alpha}(2n).\]

\begin{note}
The whole argument turns on the ratio depending on $\theta_1$ only through a
constant multiplier, never through the statistic. That is precisely the monotone
likelihood ratio property, and it is why a single critical region works against
every $\theta_1>\theta_0$ at once.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Consider a random sample $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ from a p.f.
\[f(x;\theta)=\frac{1}{\theta+1}\left(\frac{\theta}{\theta+1}\right)^x,
\qquad x=0,1,2,\ldots\]
where $\theta>0$. Find a UMP test of $H_0:\theta=\theta_0$ versus
$H_1:\theta>\theta_0$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
This is the geometric distribution with success probability
$p=\frac{1}{\theta+1}$, counted as the number of failures, so $E(X)=\theta$. The
likelihood is
\[L(\theta)=\frac{1}{(\theta+1)^n}\left(\frac{\theta}{\theta+1}\right)^{\sum x_i}.\]
For $\theta_1>\theta_0$,
\[\frac{L(\theta_1)}{L(\theta_0)}
=\left(\frac{\theta_0+1}{\theta_1+1}\right)^{n}
\left[\frac{\theta_1(\theta_0+1)}{\theta_0(\theta_1+1)}\right]^{\sum x_i}.\]
Compare the two products inside the bracket:
\[\theta_1(\theta_0+1)=\theta_0\theta_1+\theta_1,\qquad
\theta_0(\theta_1+1)=\theta_0\theta_1+\theta_0,\]
and since $\theta_1>\theta_0$ the first exceeds the second, so the bracket is
greater than $1$. The ratio is therefore increasing in $\sum x_i$: a monotone
likelihood ratio in $T=\sum X_i$. Hence
\[\text{reject } H_0 \text{ when } T=\sum_{i=1}^nX_i\geq c,\qquad
P\left(T\geq c\mid\theta_0\right)=\alpha,\]
and this same region is most powerful against every $\theta_1>\theta_0$, so the
test is UMP. Under $H_0$, $T$ is negative binomial --- the sum of $n$ independent
geometrics --- which fixes $c$.

\begin{note}
The step worth copying is comparing $\theta_1(\theta_0+1)$ with
$\theta_0(\theta_1+1)$ directly rather than trying to simplify the fraction. The
common term $\theta_0\theta_1$ cancels and the comparison reduces to
$\theta_1>\theta_0$, which is given.
\end{note}
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be a random sample from $X_i\sim\text{GAM}(\beta,\theta)$ with
\[f(x)=\frac{1}{\theta^\beta\Gamma(\beta)}x^{\beta-1}e^{-\frac{x}{\theta}},
\qquad x>0.\]
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a).] If $\beta$ is known: (i) show that $f(x_1,\ldots,x_n;\theta)$ has a
monotone likelihood ratio; (ii) derive a UMP size $\alpha$ test of
$H_0:\theta\leq\theta_0$ versus $H_1:\theta>\theta_0$; (iii) sketch the power
function of the test in (ii).
\item[(b).] If $\theta$ is known, derive a UMP size $\alpha$ test of
$H_0:\beta\leq\beta_0$ against $H_1:\beta>\beta_0$. Sketch the power function.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{(a)(i).} With $\beta$ known,
\[L(\theta)=\frac{1}{\theta^{n\beta}\Gamma(\beta)^n}
\left(\prod x_i\right)^{\beta-1}e^{-\frac{1}{\theta}\sum x_i},\]
so for $\theta_1>\theta_0$
\[\frac{L(\theta_1)}{L(\theta_0)}
=\left(\frac{\theta_0}{\theta_1}\right)^{n\beta}
\exp\!\left[\sum x_i\left(\frac{1}{\theta_0}-\frac{1}{\theta_1}\right)\right].\]
The exponent's coefficient is positive, so the ratio increases in
$T=\sum X_i$: the family has a \textbf{monotone likelihood ratio} in $T$.

\textbf{(ii).} By the Karlin--Rubin theorem, a family with MLR in $T$ gives a UMP
test of $H_0:\theta\leq\theta_0$ against $H_1:\theta>\theta_0$ that rejects for
large $T$. Under $\theta=\theta_0$,
